T
he world is far less ordinary than most people know. 
The world that most believe we live in, where the 
laws of science reign supreme and the paranormal 
is relegated to the realm of superstition and folklore, 
is merely a fragile veil concealing an infinite array of 
strange, wondrous, and terrifying phenomena.  In the 
true world, countless terrible things creep in the dark, 
a dozen hidden faiths cry out their praise to true gods, 
many are born with strange, reality-bending powers, 
and a thousand hidden dimensions -  some hells, some 
heavens, some merely strange -  lie behind as many out-
of-the-way doors.  Some might be delighted to learn of 
the wondrous variety the universe contains.  They may 
see a universe of infinite possibility as something to be 
celebrated.
The SCP Foundation, however, exists to protect 
humanity from these hidden threats.
 
To the SCP Foundation, everything that creeps, 
enchants, bewilders, and defies the natural order poses 
a potential danger to humanity.  They understand that 
the modern world, built on the principles of science 
and reason, is vulnerable to the unknown and the 
unpredictable.  The Foundation’s mission is to safeguard 
the safety and stability of human society by containing 
and studying these anomalies.  While their methods 
may seem harsh, their actions are driven by a deep 
sense of responsibility.  They contain every monster, 
every mutant, every anomaly, not out of malice, but 
out of a necessity to protect the world from the dangers 
that lie beyond the veil of normalcy.  By doing so, they 
strive to prevent chaos and ensure that humanity can 
continue to thrive, unaware of the lurking horrors.  The 
Foundation is possibly the most dominant organization 
in the world, with resources rivaling nations, and 
so long as it maintains its hold over Earth, humanity 
will be untainted and uninfluenced by all that defies 
understanding.  Even seemingly beneficial anomalies 
could risk undermining human independence and 
dominance over their own destinies.
Thus, the Foundation seeks to Secure all anomalies, 
Contain them and all knowledge of their existence, and 
Protect humanity from the countless ways the anomalous 
can harm them.
The Foundation is a global organization, with secure 
sites positioned throughout the world, staffed by guards 
who keep the contained anomalies under wraps, and 
scientists who study the many anomalies present in 
any given site.  For an organization that is so cautious 
against anything that defies human understanding, the 
Foundation zealously pokes and prods at everything 
they possess, seeking to understand everything they 
can about the anomalous, even as they hide such 
understanding from the rest of the world.  Every now and 
then they may even come to understand an anomaly 
so well that they cease to regard it as an anomaly at 
all.  This is extremely rare, however -  there are a few 
dozen such examples, out of thousands of anomalies 
the Foundation has contained.  For the most part, once 
the Foundation has come to regard something (or 
someone) as anomalous, there is nothing they can do 
to escape the Foundation’s policy on containment.
The Foundation often refers to its anomalies as SCPs, a 
reference to the Special Containment Procedures each 
anomaly requires. They assign every anomaly a code 
name, which they use to the exclusion of any other title 
it may possess, much to the irritation of many humanoid 
anomalies. This code name is always SCP, followed by 
a dash and a number. SCPs are never regarded by the 
Foundation as truly human, with those that look and act 
human only ever being referred to as humanoid, even if they 
were born from human parents and are indistinguishable 
from humans for many real-world purposes.
The Foundation operates in the utmost secrecy.  Because 
they do not want humanity to become aware of the fact 
that anomalies exist -  nor the existence of their very 
organization -  the Foundation is obsessively secretive 
about everything it does, even when it concerns those 
who are permitted to know they exist.  Everything the 
Foundation knows carries a security classification, 
and even those with the proper security clearances 
are subject to compartmentalization.  Critical to their 
ability to maintain this secrecy are amnestics, various 
compounds in the Foundation’s arsenal that allow 
them to erase the memories of those who encounter 
the anomalous, or obtain information they are not 
supposed to possess.
For all its unyielding policy to keep the anomalous 
hidden away, some might wonder why it does not 
simply destroy the anomalies it possesses.  There are 
several reasons for this. For one thing, the Foundation’s 
policy against destroying anomalies is not absolute. 
When an anomaly poses an otherwise uncontainable 
danger to humanity, or when at least one instance of 
6


a dangerous anomaly is safely tucked away, they will 
reluctantly destroy an SCP. More often though, they 
fight as fervently to preserve the existence of SCPs as 
they do to keep them in containment.  This is partly 
because SCPs by their nature defy understanding, and 
that means the consequences of any attempt to destroy 
them are unpredictable.  Eliminating an anomaly, even 
a seemingly harmless one, may simply let loose an even 
greater threat the anomaly was keeping subdued.  No 
matter how unlikely it seems, who can say whether the 
destruction of an object that is not understood might 
trigger some terrible catastrophe?  Also, because an 
anomaly always defies existing science, one can never 
be sure that one’s attempt to destroy an anomaly will 
succeed or what consequences it might have if it fails. 
The Global Occult Coalition, one of the Foundation’s 
many rival organizations, is far more eager to destroy 
anomalous objects and has more than once seen its 
attempts to do so backfire, making an anomaly far 
more dangerous than it had been before.
There is also an ethical component to the Foundation’s 
unwillingness to destroy anomalies.  Despite all their 
willingness to confine and persecute humanoid anom-
alies, the Foundation recognizes, on some level, that 
just because something defies human understanding 
does not mean that it has no right to exist.  Though they 
may be willing to keep all things they deem anomalous 
away from the public eye, they would prefer not to ut-
terly annihilate all which they do not understand, es-
pecially since, by keeping it around to be studied, they 
may one day allow themselves to understand it.
The Foundation possesses a large number of 
anomalous objects. However, partly because of their 
ideological aversion to the anomalous itself, they are 
hesitant to use most of the anomalies they possess. 
Anomalous humanoids are heavily scrutinized before 
being allowed to become Foundation personnel, 
even if their abilities and loyalties are such that they 
would make good agents.  They are also hesitant to 
use Thaumaturgy except when it cannot be avoided. 
However, the Foundation is sometimes inconsistent 
about this. Personnel who somehow become anomalous 
after joining the Foundation, such as Kain Pathos Crow, 
generally retain their positions, and there are rumors of 
a top-secret program to assemble an MTF composed 
of anomalous individuals who could use their abilities 
in service to the Foundation. The Foundation also 
sometimes uses, or even constructs anomalies if, by 
doing so, they could achieve something very useful 
which they otherwise never could.  Anomalies like 
SCP-2000 and SCP-579 serve as last-resort options to 
rebuild normalcy if it is ever destroyed.
Much to their chagrin, the Foundation are not the only 
organization in the world who know about the existence 
of the anomalous. Several other organizations possess 
this knowledge. These rival organizations are termed 
Groups of Interest. They vary enormously in size, 
resources, power, and goals. Some, like the Global 
Occult Coalition, have similar goals to the Foundation, 
prevented from working alongside them only by 
disagreements regarding their methods.  Some, like The 
Factory and Are We Cool Yet? create and distribute 
anomalous objects, and consequently pose a threat 
to the Veil of Secrecy the Foundation holds.  Some, 
like the Serpent’s Hand, object to the existence of the 
Foundation and the Veil, wishing to expose the truth 
about the existence of the anomalies to the general 
public and set harmless and humanoid anomalies free 
from containment. A few, like the Fifth Church and the 
Children of the Scarlet King, have still more devious 
goals, up to and including the destruction of the world 
as we know it. Most, though, have goals relatively 
orthogonal to those of the Foundation, and wish they 
could simply be left to go about their business, working in 
whatever area of anomalous commerce or religion they 
are dedicated to. The Foundation will never fully accept 
the existence of another organization with control over 
the anomalous, and even if they are outwardly at peace 
with a specific group of interest, they quietly regard all 
of their competitors as illegitimate, and ideate about 
a world in which they alone possess knowledge and 
control over the anomalous.
*Definitions for terminology and groups of interest can 
   be found in this books glossary, or on the scp-wiki.

Introduction
The SCP Foundation is a highly secretive, international orga-
nization tasked with containing and studying anomalous indi-
viduals, objects, and phenomena that threaten the normality 
of the world as we understand it.  The Foundation operates 
in secrecy, hidden from the public eye, and is committed to 
safeguarding the planet.  Its mission is to Secure, Contain, 
and Protect anomalies and entities that pose a threat to hu-
manity.  The Foundation’s primary objective is to control these 
anomalous entities.  This is achieved by housing them in secure 
facilities under strict “Secure Containment Procedures” and 
conducting scientific research to understand their properties, 
abilities, and origins.  The Foundation has a vast network 
of facilities and personnel that work together to achieve its 
mission. It employs a wide range of individuals, including re-
searchers, doctors, engineers, security personnel, and more- 
all with specialized skills and training.  
The Foundation operates with a strict protocol and set of pro-
cedures to maintain the safety and security of its personnel, as 
well as the general public.
SCP: The Tabletop Role-Playing Game invites you to immerse 
yourself in a universe inspired by the vast collection of entries 
and stories found at scp-wiki.com. 
With an overwhelming number of SCP entries spanning over 
ten thousand, the SCP universe encompasses a library of 
cinematic situations, enigmatic creatures, and anomalous 
objects.  The absence of a universal “canon” allows for 
various “Canon Universes” within SCP lore.
Recognizing the vastness and diversity of SCP-inspired con-
tent, SCP: The Tabletop Role-Playing Game 2nd Edition 
(SCP.2e) presents a streamlined ruleset designed to embrace 
boundless possibilities.  This edition offers expanded custom-
ization options allowing players to create unique Characters 
while refining the ruleset for newcomers to easily dive into the 
world of role-playing.
Venture beyond the confines of the Foundation’s secrecy and 
align with other Groups of Interest (GoIs).  Join the Chaos 
Insurgency, or seek enlightenment with The Serpents Hand, 
guardians of forgotten knowledge.  Find your true potential 
by embodying an SCP entity itself, or choose your own desti-
ny as an agent of the Foundation.
In SCP.2e your Character’s background holds the key to un-
locking their true potential.  Utilize your unique skill-set within 
this extraordinary landscape.  Record your Character’s his-
tory, qualifications, and past employment to shape their role 
within this survival horror universe.  
Get ready to enter a world where the paranormal intertwines 
with science, challenging your perception of reality.  Assem-
ble your team and follow the orders of the SCP Foundation 
beyond all sense of self-preservation.  The SCP world is 
harsh, and survival is a rare luxury.  The line between life and 
death is thin, and only the resilient and resourceful will defy 
the odds.  Are you prepared to face the enigmatic forces that 
seek to cause destruction and bring you face to face with your 
deepest fears?  Choose your path wisely for it may lead to 
salvation, or a fate worse than death in the shadows of the 
unknown.
What is SCP?
“S.C.P.” stands for “Secure, Contain, Protect” the 
three principles of the SCP Foundation.  If you are un-
familiar with SCP entirely, search for the term on the 
Internet and begin your journey down the deepest of 
all rabbit holes!  Or you can go straight to the source:
SCP-wiki.com
26letterpublishing is not affiliated with the SCP wiki.
SCP.2e is an unofficial tabletop rpg adaptation of the SCP universe.

For more information please check out:

Introduction


Navigating the Pages
If you are planning on being a Player (PC) for SCP.2e, you should 
read the Dice Mechanics chapter and then try to become familiar 
with the Character Sheet as you make your first Character.  If you 
plan on playing the role of the “Director” (a.k.a. GM/DM) it’s 
suggested you read the majority of this book.  
Glossary
If at any point you read a term that seems to have a more 
mechanical or narrative definition than a dictionary would 
provide, you can skip to the end of this book and look up the 
term in the Glossary.  The Glossary will give you a definition 
of the word in the context of this game and can help clarify 
nuanced rule sets.
Digital Interactivity
Whenever you see a page number listed you can click it to 
jump to that page in the pdf.  If you are reading the physical 
copy of this book you can quickly identify sections by looking 
in the page corners.  The SCP.2e character sheet has clickable 
dice icons and fields you can use to enter data.  Be sure you 
go to the preferences in Adobe Reader and uncheck “Show 
border hover color for fields” in the forms section.
Choose Your Own Advancement
This book is not intended to be read cover to cover in order. 
Directors should skip around and read whatever section they 
feel they should start in and go from there.  When players 
perform Character creation they will need to skip back and 
forth between sections often.
Why SCP.2e?
While SCP.2e might seem different from typical horror RPGs 
with its detailed mechanics, there’s a basis for it.  Some might 
wonder why bother with combat and character customization 
when everyone’s destined for a gruesome end in SCP’s uni-
verse -   But ultimately, the Director sets the tone and threat 
level for your game.  SCP.2e’s dice system is straightforward, 
and allows you to create characters as quickly as you like. 
Sure, you can opt for a game where you face off against an-
cient gods and unkillable monsters, and that’s perfectly fine, 
but the SCP universe has so much more to offer.  It’s about 
delving into mysteries and exploring the unknown.  SCP.2e 
gives you the tools to tell any kind of story, from battling el-
dritch horrors to solving containment breaches.  By offering 
a flexible system, SCP.2e encompasses a broad spectrum of 
narrative potential within the SCP universe.
SCP the TTRPG VS SCP.2e
While the core dice mechanics are very similar, there are 
enough differences that both should be treated as separate 
games.  If you have played first edition, you will find the 
following major differences:
Attribute Dice Reworked
Rather than buying rows of dice to enhance your Attributes, 
you will now upgrade dice.  This not only saves room on the 
character sheet but it gets rid of having to buy dice you never 
really roll.
No More Decimal Places
Instead of skills having decimal places to break ties, skills are 
whole numbers.  Instead of applying skill bonuses (+1, +2) 
you can now obtain skill multipliers (x2, x3) that will multiply 
your skill before adding it to a roll.  This makes the skills you 
buy have a much more powerful impact to your dice rolls.
Dice Mechanics Reworked
To keep things a bit more simple, dice will still explode but 
only into one die of the higher type instead of multiple dice. 
Also, rolling 1s no longer reduces your total!
Experience Reworked
Instead of saving up experience points to increase your stats, 
each session you are rewarded with a flat bonus that you must 
spend before the next session.  Not only does this eliminate 
excess math, but you’ll find Character improvement has been 
vastly expedited.
No More Credit System
The SCP Foundation has nearly limitless funds, so it doesn’t 
make a ton of sense that Characters are limited to their 
resources with a Foundation currency.  Instead Characters can 
use any weapons or gear they can carry, with some limitations.
Character Security Levels Reworked
SCP.2e does not lock away chapters behind a Security Level. 
Since Security Levels are a Foundation specific system, a 
Character’s Security Level only limits them to what data and 
areas they can access in-universe.
Disruption Classes Introduced
You can offset the balance of power between Characters by 
allowing your players to choose different Disruption Classes, 
which are a system within Character Creation that allows 
players to choose to play as a more powerful Character from 
the start- with a cost.  The more powerful you begin the game 
the less improvement that Character will get at the end of each 
session.  This allows for an offset in power that averages out 
over time.
9
Introduction


Performing Skill Checks
There are eight attributes in SCP.2e and each attribute will 
have a number of dice which represent how good a Character 
is at a task.  For example, you could have two eight-sided 
dice (2D8), and two ten-sided dice (2D10) in your Strength 
attribute.  When The Director asks you to make a skill check, 
look on your sheet to see the associated Attribute that goes 
along with the skill, and then gather the dice for that Attribute. 
The dice you roll are called your Dice Pool.  Once you roll the 
dice, you ignore all but the top two highest results and add 
them together.  For example, if you rolled 2D8 & 2D10, and 
got 2-5-5-9 you would only count the top two and add them 
together.  In this case you would add 9 and 5 making your 
result 14.  You will then add the Skill to this number.
The result of your skill check is also referred to as your score 
for the task.  
Difficulties
Directors will usually provide a difficulty for the task, such as 
14.  In which case, you must meet or exceed the given difficulty 
in order to successfully complete the task.
Another way to come up with a difficulty is think of a rating 
between 1 and 10, with one being easiest and ten being 
hardest.  Then add +2 and double it.
Directors can also generate a random difficulty by adding 
three dice together:
	
3D6 (usually easy)  
	
3D8 (usually average) 
 
3D10 (usually difficult)  
 
3D12 (usually very difficult) 
	
3D20 (usually impossible) 
 
Critical Successes and Failures
If your Skill Check scores double the difficulty, you get a 
Critical Success.  This means your Character performs the task 
flawlessly.  The Director may provide you an extra bonus for 
completing the task so well and can result in dealing extra 
damage or noticing obscure clues that others would have 
missed.  On the other hand, if you score half or less than the 
difficulty, you get a Critical Failure.  This means your Character 
botched the task and failed miserably. Critical Failures can 
often lead to severe injury, or outright death.
See example on page 166  and 168.
Exploding Dice
If a die is rolled for a Skill Check and it gets the maximum 
result (such as rolling an 8 on an eight-sided die, or a 10 on 
a ten-sided die) you get a bonus die. Bonus dice are added to 
the Die Pool and can sprout additional dice just the same. This 
is called “Exploding Dice.” 
Each 6 rolled on a D6 adds 1D8 to the die pool.   
Each 8 rolled on a D8 adds 1D10 to the die pool.   
Each 10 rolled on a D10 adds 1D12 to the die pool.  
Each 12 rolled on a D12 adds 1D20 to the die pool.
(If you roll a 20 on a D20 the die does not explode.)
Remember, after you’re done rolling all the Exploding dice you’re 
still only counting the top two dice throughout all the dice that were 
rolled.  You may choose to roll less Attribute dice if you desire.  You 
also are not required to roll bonus dice you receive from Exploding 
dice.  You can choose to stop rolling at any time if you are satisfied 
with your result.
See example on page 165.
Automatic Successes
Your Attributes will also list an Attribute Rating along with the 
dice.  Instead of rolling dice for a skill check, you can add 
your skill to your Attribute Rating and treat that as your result. 
This can often result in automatically succeeding (or critically 
succeeding!) a skill check without dice ever touching the table.
Note:  You cannot Automatically Succeed when performing Versus 
Rolls against another PC (player) or for Punished Skill Checks.
See example on page 166.

Dice Mechanics


Skills
Most checks will be accompanied by a Skill.  Your Director will 
tell you what Skill you get to add to your Skill Check.  If your 
Skill was a 3, and your attribute dice pool came out to be 12, 
the final result to tell the Director would be 15.  Even if you 
have a skill at zero, you can still attempt to perform the task- 
you just won’t get any bonus to the dice rolled.  
See example on page 166.
Punishing Rolls
Sometimes a task is so difficult the Director may declare 
a Punishing Roll for the skill check rather than simply 
increasing the difficulty.  When rolling a Punished skill 
check, your Attribute Dice will not explode, Exertion & 
Reverence Points can not be used, and Automatic Successes 
are unavailable.  Good luck!
See Exertion and Reverence on page 59
See example on page 166.
Versus Rolls
Sometimes you may have to compete against another 
Character in a task, such as if your Character were trying to 
punch someone, as they were trying to avoid getting hit.  This 
is called a Versus Roll. In such a case both Characters roll 
the appropriate “Attribute plus Skill” and whoever gets the 
higher result succeeds.  If there is ever a tie then ties go to the 
attacker, or the initiator of said Action (Director’s discretion). 
If there is no clear initiator, then the tie goes to the younger 
of the two Characters.  If one Character rolls twice as high 
as the opponent, they obtain a Critical Success, while the 
opponent gets a Critical Failure.
See example on page 167.
Player Advantage in Versus Rolls
When determining any Versus roll, the NPC’s result is 
always determined before the players.  This gives players the 
advantage and allows them to spend Reverence or Exertion 
to modify their result if desired.
See example on page 167.
Reverence
Directors may award Reverence when a player or Character 
does something incredible, fun, or interesting!  Players can 
spend 2 Reverence to reroll attribute dice.
See Reverence on page 59
See example on page 172.
Timed Rolls
Timed Rolls are used for Actions that require an extended 
period of time to accomplish, such as hacking a security 
system or solving a complex puzzle.  The Director sets a pace 
for the roll, which could be as short as one Combat Round 
or as long as an hour, depending on the situation.  Each 
time that period of time passes, the player rolls one die from 
their Attribute Die pool.  The difficulty for the roll is revealed 
slowly by the Director, providing hints about the true difficulty 
level.  For example, the Director might say, “The difficulty 
for this roll is above 5, but below 25” after the first die is 
rolled.  This gradual reveal creates tension and suspense as 
players await the final outcome.  If the difficulty is met before 
all dice are rolled (often due to exploding dice) the task is 
completed early.  This is also useful for situations where it’s not 
immediately obvious that the task has too high of a difficulty, 
and realistically the Characters would have to spend time to 
discover this.  For example, if the Director said “The Difficulty 
is over 25” the players might choose to abandon the task early.
You can even combine Timed Rolls with random difficulties 
(see previous page) and reveal one of the three dice to the 
players each step.  After the third die is revealed the difficulty 
will be clear.
See example on page 174.
Dual/Triple Tech
Players can cooperate with each other and treat a set of 
Actions performed in unison as a single Action.  If two or 
three PCs are attempting to perform the exact same Actions 
at the same time, they may perform a Dual Tech (for two 
PCs) or a Triple Tech (three PCs).  All players involved roll 
the appropriate Attribute.  Dice explode as normal, but each 
player only counts the highest single die from each pool, and 
then add all PCs totals together (the top two dice for Dual Tech 
and the top three dice for Triple Tech).  Next add all of the PCs 
appropriate skills to this total to get the combined Score for the 
co-op Action.  When Dual/Triple Tech is performed outside 
of combat, the task being performed will take two (Dual Tech) 
or three (Triple Tech) times longer than if a single Character 
was performing the task (Director’s discretion). 
See example on page 173.
11
Dice Mechanics


Besides knowing how to perform Skill Checks, there are a few 
other basics you should know before reading further.  Terms in 
this book that are Capitalized often have a specific meaning 
in game terms, so be mindful of them.
SCP.2e
SCP The Tabletop RPG 2nd Edition is referred to in this book as 
SCP.2e for short.  Although dice rolls are handled similarly to 
first edition, the majority of the ruleset has been redone, and 
as such both games should be treated as separate systems.
1D6 = One Six-Sided Dice
This is a gaming term which abbreviates terms such as “Seven 
Twelve-Sided Dice.”  In such a case it is easier to say and/
or type “7D12.”  If the number or type of dice are unknown, 
terms like “3D” or “D8” can be used respectively.  Some-
times an “X” is used such as XD10, meaning that X changes 
depending on other factors.  SCP.2e uses four, six, eight, ten, 
twelve, and twenty-sided dice.  They can be purchased at 
most hobby stores.
Directors
Most Tabletop RPGs have a GM (Game Master) or a DM (Dun-
geon Master).  In SCP.2e this role is called the game’s “Director”. 
PC = Player Character
The players control the actions of the main protagonists of the 
story.  These protagonists are called Player Characters, or 
“PCs” for short.
NPC = Non-Player Character
People in the story that are controlled/voiced by the Director.
Character 
When referring to either a PC or an NPC, the general term 
“Character” is used instead.  All of the fictional people within 
your universe are “Characters”.
The Role of The Director
The Director is the person who organizes everything about 
the game.  They create the universe, the plot, maps, and all 
Non-Player Characters.  They play the role of everyone in 
the universe except the Player Characters (PCs).  Think of the 
Director as the author of a book that must write everything 
on-the-fly as he or she adjusts to the whims of the main Char-
acters (PCs).
The Roles of the Players
PCs, or “Player Characters” are the main Characters of a sto-
ry set up by the Director.  Players in the game can control one 
(and only one) Character each.  If that Character dies, they 
must make a new Character and have them be introduced into 
the story later.  Do your best to help the Director further the 
plot.  If there is a mysterious man sitting in the corner holding 
a glowing purple orb, go talk to him!  Talk to every NPC you 
come across, and talk to them often!  Make sure when you are 
talking to other players, you talk as your Character, not your-
self.  Try to speak “in Character” as often as you can!  To help 
differentiate your Character from yourself, it’s often a good 
idea to give them an accent, or speak in a different pitch.  
Chapters
Chapters can often be defined as one “gaming session” how-
ever the length and significance of the session may impact the 
quantity of Chapters.  Assume you and your friends meet up 
once each week for 4-5 hours of play.  This is a good guideline 
for your Chapters - one chapter equals one gaming session. 
However, if your session is much longer than 5 hours the Di-
rector can declare another Chapter ending halfway through. 
Or perhaps the Chapter ends after each mission is completed. 
It is entirely up to the Director how long each Chapter lasts. 
Chapters are used to keep track of Character improvement 
(Called Chapter Rewards).
Attributes
There are 8 Attributes in SCP.2e and each represent a pri-
mary aspect about your Character.  These Attributes are split 
into two categories:  Physical and Mental.  
The four Physical Attributes are  
Strength (STR), Dexterity (DEX), Perception (PER), & Health (HTH).  
The four Mental Attributes are  
Intellect (INT), Willpower (WIL), Charisma (CHR), & Fate (FTE). 
(The Fate Attribute is similar to a Character’s luck.)
Skills
There are 33 Skills in SCP.2e.  Skills are used for Skill Checks 
(see page 10).  Each Skill is listed on your sheet along with 
the associated Attribute which is typically rolled with it.  Most 
Skills use Dexterity and Intellect, but you might roll a different 
Attribute in combination with your Skills.  For example, the 
Director might request an Athletics (HTH) Skill Check that is 
added to your Willpower dice to see if your Character has 
enough endurance to keep running for an extended period. 
Skills may also obtain multipliers, which multiplies the skill 
by either 2 or 3 before adding it to your roll.  Crucial Skills 
are listed at the top of the skills list.  Crucial skills are treated 
exactly like other skills.  They are more commonly rolled 
then the rest in combat and are displayed at the top for your 
convenience.  
Chapter two:  
Overview
12
Overview


System Overview
SCP.2e is designed as a comprehensive tabletop RPG system 
that can resonate with new players, while keeping adaptable 
rules to allow easy integration of expansion material.  The 
nuanced combat system accommodates players at any expe-
rience level.  Those eager to jump in to the action can utilize 
the ‘fast’ Character creation method, or simply choose from 
one of the 45 pre-made Character options available.  The 
‘advanced’ Character creation method caters to those who 
prefer a more intricate customization process.  
Directors can also introduce multiple ‘Disruption Classes’ 
which influence each Characters starting power level - but the 
more powerful you start, the less bonus points you will get 
at the end of each session.  It is a trade off that allows for 
a rag-tag group of Characters of various power levels and 
backgrounds to come together for a unified cause.
The combat system in SCP.2e was designed to be simple but 
engaging.  Players have nine basic options to choose from 
during their Turns, with the ability to take two Actions each. 
The Turn order, determined by the last participant who took 
their Combat Actions, adds an element of player agency 
while encouraging both offensive and defensive tactics.  This 
system allows the active participant to strategically choose 
the next combatant.  Characters who have taken Actions may 
face more difficulty evading incoming attacks, so it’s not al-
ways best to have your whole team act before the enemy. 
This, along with the new ‘stagger’ mechanic can swing com-
bat encounters to create underdog comebacks!
Another innovative aspect of SCP.2e is the elimination of a 
currency system.  Players will no longer save up credits to pur-
chase weapons and gear as most of it will be theirs for the 
taking.  The only drawback is that you can’t haul around huge 
guns and be armed to the teeth in public.   The Uniform system 
limits what weapons can be concealed in public to prevent 
potential unrest and mass panic.  Players will find that subtle 
attire which can conceal a few guns will outshine full riot gear 
in everyday situations.  
SCP.2e is designed to be a universal system.  Throughout this 
book, you will often find options that the Director can choose 
from to help create guidelines and limitations on players, al-
lowing the story they want to tell to unfold.  SCP.2e does not 
attempt to predict what type of “GM” is running the game. 
The system is designed for any story, about anything within 
the SCP universe -  any GoI, any SCP, any anomaly, or any-
thing else.  This is a universal role-playing game system within 
the SCP Universe.  Enemies can be as powerful as Gods, or 
as harmless as SCP-999.  Stories can be one session long or 
last several years.  Your players can be Foundation D-Class 
expendables, or powerful Reality Benders working for the 
Serpents Hand!
How to play
The best way to learn how to play this game is first to become 
familiar with a Character and their stats.  So the following 
chapter will dive right into Character Creation!  Try to make a 
Character as you read each step.  You can print one out and 
use a pencil, or open the PDF in Adobe Reader and click on 
icons or type directly inside the document.  
After you made a Character read the rest of the chapters and 
flip over to the “Example Chapter” whenever directed.  The 
Example Chapter encapsulates the most complex rules into 
an actual play example story.  If you are able to follow along 
with the example chapter story, congrats, you are an expert!
We hope you enjoy playing 
 SCP The Tabletop RPG - Second edition!
13
Overview
55
44
94
08


Each player will need to create a Character to play.  These Characters are referred to as Player Characters, or “PCs”. 
Before you begin you will need a copy of the SCP.2e Character Sheet which can be found here: tinyurl.com/SCP2eCharacterSheet 
Understanding the Character Sheet
Before we begin to create a Character, let’s get familiar with the character sheet itself.  There are two sides to the SCP.2e 
Character Sheet.  The Front will list your primary stats such as Attributes and Skills while the back is reserved for aspects, items, 
and notes.
Secondary Statistics
Special stats such as a Character’s Speed.
Character Info
Basic information such as Name, Age, Security Level.
Attributes with Die Icons
Eight Attributes and icons that represent dice for each one.
Skills
How good your Character is in specific areas.
Defense
The difficulty to hit your Character when they don’t dodge.
Weapon Stats
A place to record the stats for the weapons you own.
Improvement
Section of the sheet that contains information on how much 
you improve each gaming session (Chapter).
Items Held
A place to record any items you are currently holding.  
Items are stored in your eight “Item Slots.”

Aspects
During Character Creation you can purchase Aspects.  They 
will improve your Character in a wide range of ways.
Advanced Character Creation only.
Power, Talent, and History Points
Points used during Character Creation to purchase Aspects. 
Advanced Character Creation only.
Uniform Information
This section will describe what your Character is wearing 
and what weapons they are currently carrying.  
Favor Points
Keep track of favors NPCs owe you here.   
See page 101
Misc Notes
Players may use this area for any purpose.

Three Methods 
There are three methods of creating a Character 
in SCP.2e - Premade, Fast, and Advanced.  
Premade
If you want to skip Character Creation entirely, you can use 
the list of premade Characters that came along with this book 
in PDF form.  Simply copy the stats onto a new sheet, rename 
them, customize items and weapons to your liking, and you’re 
ready to roll!  For shorter games, the Director can choose and 
print out premade Characters as required.  There are nine 
premade Characters for each of the five Disruption Class types 
(more on this later).  
Fast
Fast Character Creation is great for new players and 
performed in four quick steps, great for players who just 
want to get started, but also have a specific archetype in 
mind.  Follow only the steps instructed on the opposite page. 
We strongly suggest you use Fast Character Creation for 
your first Character so you can understand the fundamentals 
before trying Advanced.
Note:  Fast Character Creation creates a “Dark Disruption Class“ 
Character.
Remember to come back to page 17 after you 
complete each step!!!
Advanced
Advanced Character Creation is performed using a flowchart 
of steps.  Although it may take longer to complete, your 
ability to customize your Character in greater depth will 
make it worthwhile.  Advanced Character Creation is the 
ideal process to create a unique custom Character.  After you 
have completed a few Characters you can use the Advanced 
Character Creation Cheat sheet on (page 33) to speed up 
the process.
Note:  Character Creation Steps are not in order in the book, so 
you will need to reference the opposite page each time you finish 
a step!  
Unlike Fast Character Creation, when creating an Advanced 
Character you will not choose a Character Class.
Remember to come back to page 17 after you 
complete each step!!!

Age plays a big factor in Advanced Character Creation. 
Young Characters have higher physical stats but less History 
points, which are spent to obtain new Aspects.  History points 
are most efficient at buying Aspects that increase your starting 
Skill levels.  Choose your Character’s age classification.  You 
can use the suggested age group listed for each bracket to 
help decide your Character’s age, but it is not required.

Perhaps the most impactful decision you will make in 
the Advanced Character Creation process is choosing 
your Character’s Disruption Class.  Your Disruption Class 
is a rating among five classes that will determine your 
Character’s starting power level; however, the more 
power you decide to start with, the less improvement your 
Character will gain throughout the story.  Directors can 
decide to limit what Disruption Classes they allow so it’s 
best to ask what type of campaign you are participating in. 
See also “Campaign Diversity Level (CDL)” on page 98.
Here is a brief description on how each Disruption Class works:
Dark - Your Character will start off with basic stats but gain 
the most improvement. 
Vlam - Your Character will start off with a minor stat boost 
and gain moderate improvement.
Keneq - Your Character will start off with a significant boost 
to their stats and gain some improvement.
Ekhi - Your Character will start off with a major stat boost but 
gain very little improvement.
Amida - Your Character will start off extremely powerful but 
have no improvement.
The numbers with slashes next to each of the Disruption 
Class names represent (Starting Power)/(Improvement Each 
Session).  Dark lists 0/4 meaning that it has the lowest Starting 
Power for Character creation but it has the best improvement 
each session so you will catch up quickly.
What Disruption Classes Determine
Starting Attribute Tokens:  
How many Attribute Tokens you will start with .
(i.e. how many dice you start with.)
Improvement Each Chapter:  
How many Attribute Tokens & Skill points you get each 
Chapter (i.e. session).
All Dividers:  
Exertion, Speed, Combat, and Projectile are all very 
important ratings for your Character.  Your “Dividers” help 
calculate these ratings.  The lower your “Divider” is, the 
higher your rating will be. 
HP Multiplier/Base HP:
These are both factors in calculating your Character’s HP.
Talent/Power points:
These points are used to buy special abilities, skill improvement, 
and anomalous aspects in later steps of Character creation.
Recording your Disruption Class
Once you’ve chosen your Disruption Class, its time to start 
adding to the character sheet.  First we will fill in the dot next 
to the chosen Disruption

of your character sheet.  In 
this example we chose the 
Keneq Disruption Class.
Finally we will record our 
Talent and Power points.  For 
this step we will need to flip 
our character sheet around 
and look to the top right 
corner.  Keneq gives 20 Talent 
points and 10 Power points.

Spend Attribute Tokens (Fast)
Fast Character Creation grants Characters 10 Attribute 
Tokens.  These tokens can be used to fill in your Attribute 
Dice Icons.   Every time you fill one in, your Attributes Rating 
goes up.  This will be a number between 2 and 16 listed for 
each attribute.  
Continue reading below for further clarification.
Spend Attribute Tokens (Advanced)
Both your Disruption Class and your Age provided you 
with Attribute Tokens.  Some of these tokens are specific to 
Physical or Mental Attributes.  “(Any)” can be spent on ei-
ther type of Attribute.  Every time you fill in one of your Dice 
Icons for an attribute, your Attributes Rating goes up.  This 
will be a number between 2 and 16 listed for each attribute. 
Continue reading below for further clarification.
Spending your Attribute Tokens
Now we take the Attribute Tokens we noted from the previous 
steps and use them to buy dice for our Attributes.  
In this example we spent 10 total Attribute Tokens.  One point 
in Strength, Dexterity, Perception, Health, Willpower, and 
Charisma, and Four points in Intellect.  Feel free to distribute 
your points however you like.
If you are using Advanced 
Character Creation, your 
Attribute Tokens will be 
separated into Physical and 
Mental Tokens.
Attribute Dice Icons
Attributes are represented as dice. 
Each Attribute will have four col-
umns of Dice Icons.  These col-
umns are filled in from bottom to 
top, and represent different types 
of dice.  By default, all Attributes 
have 2D6 filled in.  You can up-
grade these dice by filling in the 
icon above them, or buying addi-
tional dice by filling in a new D6.  
Below lists each icon and what type of dice they represent.
Attribute Die Bumpers 	
	
	
 
Certain Attribute Dice have a ‘Bumper’ below them, repre-
sented by a small circle with two arrows. These Bumpers re-
quire spending one Attribute Token to fill in.  Once filled, 
you can progress to higher dice above the Bumper.  They act 
as speed bumps, slowing progress slightly for certain die 
purchases.  The quantity and location of Die Bumpers varies 
across Attributes.   
=D8
=D10
=D12
=D6

Character Creation
23


Aspects are the key to making your Character truly unique. 
With Aspects you can acquire enhancements that give you a 
wide range of bonuses.  Aspects can increase your skills, grant 
you tactical advantages in combat, unlock additional abilities, 
and even allow you to possess anomalous characteristics!
Power, Talent, & History Points 	
There are three point types for purchasing Aspects: 
Power, Talent, and History. 
Power points are the most valuable.  Supernatural/Anomalous 
abilities can ONLY be purchased with Power Points. 
Talent points are most cost efficient when buying non-
anomalous abilities and unique skills.
History points are most cost efficient for improving your skills. 
This is done through the form of purchasing education and 
employment aspects.
How To Buy Aspects
Aspects will list between one and three costs, each associated 
with either Power, Talent, or History points. It’s important 
to note that you have the flexibility to purchase an Aspect 
by spending any combination of these point types. You are 
not required to spend all three point types for each Aspect. 
However, some Aspects may offer additional bonuses if 
you choose to spend two or three different point types when 
acquiring them. 
The Cost Icon
You will use Power, Talent and History 
points to purchase Aspects, which have a 
cost icon.  The icon has the suggested cost 
type listed in black numbers on white, and 
other costs shown in white numbers on gray. 
In the above example, the suggested cost is 8 Talent points. 
The suggested cost is usually the best deal to buy the Aspect. 
You can buy an Aspect by spending one or more of the costs 
listed.  If you spend additional point types on an Aspect, 
see the Aspects description for any bonuses or additional 
advantages granted to you.  You can only purchase each 
aspect once, unless specifically noted.

“Written in” Skills
Several Aspects will instruct you to write in a skill on your 
sheet.  These are special case skills that are unique to your 
Character.  Written in Skills do not have governing Attributes, 
and therefore do not have Skill Caps (see page 58).  Most 
written in skills will be self-explanatory such as History, or 
Architecture.  If you ever want a detailed explanation to any 
skill, refer to the Glossary.    
You are limited to a maximum of six “written in” skills for a 
single Character.  If you obtain a new skill you may choose to 
get rid of an old one.  
3 History g 1 Talent
3 Talent g 1 History
1 Power g 3 Talent
1 Power g 3 History
Point Conversion	
You may convert one Power Point into three of any other type. 
Power Points are quite valuable.  Sometimes you will need to 
convert your Power Points in order to buy Aspects that require 
Talent and History points (such as educational Aspects).  You 
can also convert three Talent points into one History point and 
vice versa.
Keep Track of Your Points	
Keep track of how many Power, Talent, and History Points 
you have.  If you convert any points to other types, put a 
note in the Aspect Notes on the back of your character sheet. 
As you purchase Aspects, be sure you record not just what 
Aspect you purchased but what type of points you spent to 
buy it.  
Instead of erasing and writing in new numbers for Power, 
Talent and History, use the “Current” bubbles or the Aspect 
Notes section so you can keep track of your original values 
and how many points you have remaining.  
You cannot convert Talent or History Points into Power points!

Purchase Aspects


Educational Aspects are most cost efficient with History points. 
Let’s look at the example “Psychologist”.  
As you can see there is no cost listed in Power, therefore you must 
spend either History or Talent points to purchase the Psychologist 
Aspect and add +1, +2, and +3 to the listed skills.  Educational 
Aspects represent school and college courses completed. 
Buying the Aspect with one point type will give your Character a 
Bachelor’s Degree in the topic, while buying both costs will give 
your Character a Doctoral (or Masters) Degree.
Let’s say we want our Character to have a Bachelors Degree 
in Psychology by spending 12 History points.  We would 
then record the Aspect as follows:  
Psychologist (College)
+1: Creativity, Deceive, Intimidate,  
Self-Control, Teaching
+2: Intuition, Persuade 
+3: Psychology
 If both costs are spent to purchase this Aspect, 
increase all above skill multipliers by 1.

define your Character.  
You could not spend another 12 History points to get this bonus.  You can 
only spend each point type on an Aspect once unless otherwise noted.
As you buy more educational Aspects, you will increase your 
Skills even more.  If an Aspect gives you a +2 to a skill you 
already have at level 1, the skill is increased to level 3.  
Likewise, if you spend two point types into an educational As-
pect, you could increase your skill multipliers beyond x2  
(x3 is the maximum for skill multipliers).  
Aspects that give additional bonuses when multiple point types are 
spent often separate these options with “or” underlined.  This signi-
fies that both options before and after the “or” are available to be 
chosen once or twice for each additional point type spent.  In other 
words, if you spent three point types, you could choose the same 
bonus twice, unless otherwise specified.

Lets look at an example:
Let’s say next we want to spend 17 Talent points and 12 Histo-
ry points into Actor after getting our doctorate in psychology. 
We don’t have to spend both 
point types, but for this example 
we choose to.  This means we get 
to increase the listed skills with +1 
bonuses, and +2 bonuses.  Then 
all the skills listed get +1 to each 
of their multipliers.  Using the Psy-
chologist example from before, 
this is what it would look like if we 
bought both Actor and Psychol-
ogist, spending both point types 
with each purchase.  
As you can see, combining two 
Doctoral Degrees can have a 
huge impact on your skills.  When 
rolling a Persuade or Psychology 
check, our Character gets to add 
a +12 to the roll!  
Maintaining proper records of your Aspects is crucial at this stage, 
especially when purchasing multiple educational Aspects. Addi-
tionally, skill multipliers can greatly benefit Characters that prog-
ress in each chapter (not Amida class).  It’s worth noting that skill 
multipliers provide an advantage as the cost to increase skill ratings 
does not scale with the multiplier.
Actor (College)
+1: Animal Ken, Athletics, Break Free,

Aspects don’t only give you skills.  Some give you additional 
options in combat, such as the Aspect Counter Attack. 
Remember, you aren’t forced to buy any Aspect with the 
suggested cost.  
It might be more optimal to buy an Aspect with other points 
depending on your Character.   Let’s say we purchased the 
Counter Attack Aspect by spending 3 Power Points.  At the 
bottom this Aspect reads “For each additional cost.”  This 
means if we spend 8 Talent or 15 History points, we get an 
added bonus.  In this case we get more Melee attacks.  But 
for this example we are not doing this.  We are only going 
to spend 3 Power points to buy this Aspect.  This will give us 
the ability to get a free attack against anyone who damages 
us while only 1 Unit away, once per Combat Round.  We will 
record this Aspect as follows:  
Remember to record the name of the Aspect and the specific 
point type(s) you used for its purchase, regardless of how many 
types you spent.  In the example we only recorded “3 Power 
Points” because that’s all we spent to purchase the Aspect. 
(P = Power, T = Talent, and H = History)
You may want to record notes in your Memo section on the back 
of your sheet to remember your special abilities or just refer to the 
page number.
Counter Attack

Custom Meters
Some Aspects require use of one of your two Custom Meters 
near the bottom of your character sheet.  You may only 
purchase up to two Custom Meter Aspects.  These Aspects 
have special rules on how these meters are utilized.  
Counter Attack
After you take damage from a Character who is 
1 Unit away from you, you may perform a Melee 
Attack Action against them for free (One time per 
Combat Round).
For each additional cost:
Increase the number of Melee attacks performed, or 
number of uses per round.

Character Creation
31


Unspent Power Points are converted into 
Attribute Tokens, which must be spent before 
session 1.
Unspent History and Talent points are convert-
ed into Skill Points, which must be spent before 
session 1.
After this point in Character Creation, Skill Caps are now 
in effect.  You are not allowed to go back and buy more 
Aspects to increase your skills after you reach this step 
and spend Skill Points. 
Who are you?!
At last, we are on the final step; the Backstory!  Look at your 
Character’s Aspects and come up with an interesting story 
about how they developed those skills and knowledges. 
Then work with other Players and see if they can combine 
their history with yours in some way.  Perhaps you were old 
college buddies, or stumbled upon the same anomaly and 
that’s how you ended up working together in the Foundation 
(or whatever other type of organization your campaign may 
be using.)  Closely work with your Director and bounce ideas 
off of each other.  Try to make your Character’s history have 
plot hooks for future reference. 
...or dont!
You aren’t required to come up with your Character’s backstory. 
If you’re the type of player who wants the spotlight to remain 
on your Director’s plot, then keep your history ambiguous! 
Tell the Director you don’t care about your history, or to let 
them come up with your backstory.  It’s possible your Director 
will use the blank slate you gave them to their advantage 
and create some very unexpected twists!  Remember, your 
Character is as customized and unique as you want them to 
be.  If you’re not sure who your Character is yet, jump into 
a few sessions and decide as you go along.  Who knows, 
perhaps by the end of the campaign your Character will be 
a completely different person than who you initially thought 
they were going to be.
You can hold onto some of your Talent and History points on 
purpose to help further customize your Skill levels.  Or, if you 
want, you can save up a bunch of Power points and convert 
them directly into Attribute Tokens.  Both options are excellent 
for fine-tuning your Character build.
You should remember how to spend Attribute Tokens from 
earlier in Character Creation.  To learn more about Skill Points 
check out Chapter Four: Advancement on page 58. 
The short of it is that Skills cost a number of skill points equal to the 
level the skill is being raised to, and skills can not be increased once 
they reach half of their governing Attributes Rating (rounded up).

Buying Aspects
(The most cost efficient point type is listed in 
black text on white background.)
You may purchase Aspects by spending only  
one point type (Power, Talent or History).
You have the option of spending an additional point type 
to get bonuses.   
You are allowed to choose the same bonus multiple times. 
For example, you could spend 2 Power and 7 Talent 
points to increase your Poise multiplier, OR include Poise 
Multiplier when calculating your Stagger score.   
If you also spent 11 History points you could choose 
either of those two options again.
You can only buy each Aspect once unless otherwise 
noted.
The highest a Skill Multiplier can go is x3.
Perfect Balance 
Reduce the difficulty to resist Stagger by half.
For each additional cost:
Increase your Poise multiplier by 1, or your Poise 
Multiplier is calculated into your Stagger score 
(multiply your Poise skill by your multiplier).
Character Creation
33
Advanced Creation Cheat Sheet


Recording your Class
Primary Benefit
The Primary Benefit of each class is typically a list of Skills and Multipliers to add to your character sheet.  You can record 
Primary Benefits on the back of your sheet in the notes section for future reference if you desire.  
Skills and Multipliers
Most Classes will provide a short list of skills that you get to add 1-3 points to.  Record these levels in the white box to the 
right of each skill.  If the Class increases a skill’s multiplier, that is recorded in the gray box on the far right.  By default all skills 
have a “x1” multiplier (which you don’t need to record).  If a skill’s multiplier goes up by one, you would write the number 2 
in the box (as shown).  When you perform a skill check that has a multiplier, you multiply your skills level by that number.  For 
example, a Computer skill of 3 with a multiplier of 2 would add +6 to your Intellect rolls 
when working with computers.
Uniforms
Classes will give stats for their Uniform at the bottom.  Uniform Styles are 
recorded on the back of your character sheet.  Simply copy the data inside of 
the “Uniform A” Style box to the back of your sheet.  Uniforms provide your 
Character with Weapon Slots, which allow you to conceal one or more weapons. 
Every Day Carry Items
Classes will list your Every Day Carry (EDC).  EDC Items are recorded on the front of your 
Character Sheet.  Some items use up two item slots while most only use up one.  Small Items 
(things like jewelry and pens) don’t take up any item slots and are recorded on the back of 
your sheet.  
Weapons
Weapons require a Weapon Slot, which are provided via your 
Uniform.  Weapon Stats are recorded on the front of your sheet 
as shown.  Feel free to name your weapons anything you want!
Switching Outfits
After 3 gaming sessions have passed, players may pick a second Uniform (Uniform B) and load this outfit 
with weapons that fit in the Weapon Slots.  Characters can switch between their two Uniforms while at a base/home. 
Every Day Carry items are always on your Character regardless of what Uniform you happen to be wearing.

The Anomaly Expert
Science?  Supernatural?  They are one and the same!  The Anomaly Expert is an 
intrepid investigator that dives into the mysteries of the anomalous.  
Armed with advanced gadgets, cutting-edge equipment, and powerful 
weaponry, you study and confront enigmatic phenomena.  From EMF scanners 
to Kant Counters, your gear will aid you in securing, containing, and decimating 
unruly entities.  Your expertise in SCP phenomena makes you an invaluable 
asset to uncovering the secrets of the unknown to protect humanity.

Primary Benefit
If you choose to be D-Class, you must choose the D-Class Jumpsuit as 
your Uniform, you can not start with any Items or weapons, and your 
Security Level is set to 00.  Weapons are only issued to your Character in 
desperate situations.  Work with your Director to determine why you are 
in D-Class and what arrangement the Foundation has made to allow you 
to go along with your party.  
Stealth rolls get -8 while wearing the D-Class Jumpsuit.
The SCP Foundation has a nearly endless resource of D-Class personnel.  These 
individuals, typically drawn from the ranks of criminals or other unfortunate 
souls, are the expendable pawns in the Foundations dangerous experiments 
and containment procedures.  Lacking a security clearance and only 
starting with an orange jumpsuit and wits, the D-Class face unimaginable 
challenges.  However what they lack in equipment they make up for in their 
resourcefulness and sheer determination to survive.  They have an extensive 
list of skill bonuses including an exceptional aptitude for escaping restraints. 
Despite their vulnerable position, D-Class hold a unique advantage in their ability 
to adapt.  Find out what it truly means to prevail in a game of survival horror.

The Medic is the backbone of any team, providing vital support to their 
comrades.  Medics are skilled healers dedicating to preserving life and 
mending wounds.  Medics remain cool under pressure and can splint 
broken bones in during the chaos of a war zone.  Their knowledge of 
anatomy and physiology allows them to diagnose ailments and treat 
illness even when anomalous.  Having a trusty Medic in your squad will 
surely extend your life expectancy, regardless of how short that my be.

The Scout
Become the eyes and ears of your team as The Scout.  Adept at infiltrating 
remote and perilous locations, the Scout is armed with speed, stealth, and 
vigilant awareness.  They excel at reconnaissance, intelligence gathering, 
and navigation through difficult terrain.  Their keen observational skills and 
ability to blend into the environment make them perfect for sneaking into 
hostile locations or gathering data on anomalies without raising suspicion. 
Scouts can help grant your team a tactical advantage and are able to 
anticipate threats, allowing the team to be aware of impending danger 
and plan alternate modes of entry and map out vital escape routes.

Primary Benefit
Soldiers are battle-hardened, highly skilled modern combat warriors 
trained to take on any anomalous creature that is able to bleed. 
Equipped with state of the art weaponry and mental fortitude, the 
Soldier is a formidable ally for any task force, and are vital assets 
to exploring the depths of the unknown.  They are  proficient 
with firearms and combat techniques of all kinds.
Stealth gets +2 while in foliage or grass.

The Spy
The Spy is a master of deception and espionage, blending into the shadows 
and slipping through cracks of security systems.  With a charm that can fool 
the most hardened guards, and a knack for adopting countless aliases, The 
Spy is the epitome of a covert operator.  Their uncanny ability to adapt to any 
situation and assume different personas make them the ultimate ghost.  But 
don’t be alarmed!  The Spy 100% works for the SCP Foundation.  In our 
rigorous background checks we confirmed their loyalty beyond a shadow 
of a doubt.  Their work history, family tree, and birth records are so clean 
they practically sparkle with innocence.  There’s absolutely no chance 
they could be working for another Group of Interest, secretly plotting 
nefarious schemes or aiming to steal classified information.  Everything 
checks out.  Nothing to see here, folks.  Just a totally legit, absolutely 
trustable, completely aboveground agent of the SCP Foundation. 
Move along.
Uniform and Small Items are on the back of your character sheet.
Every day Carry
Crowbar
Handcuffs
Lock-Pick Set
Blacklight
Snake Camera
Telescope
Wig

College Aspects
College Aspects represent your Character’s completed college 
courses.  Buying a College Aspect with one point type will 
give your Character a Bachelor’s Degree in the topic, while 
buying both costs will give your Character a Doctoral Degree 
as well as increase all the listed skill’s multipliers by 1.

This Aspect uses one of your two custom meters.  Your 
meter becomes a “Revenge” meter with a maximum

one or more dots to add +2 To-Hit.  When you deal 
damage to an enemy but before you roll damage dice, 
you may erase one or more dots to add +1D8 to the 
damage pool.

increase the damage die to 1D12.

Floating Blades
Telekinesis gets +1 (write in skill)  Telekinesis cannot 
exceed your Character’s Self-Control skill, and is 
rolled with Perception.  By spending 1 Exertion, 
you may gather nearby objects to float around your 
Character.  Determine if each object is sharp or blunt. 
The maximum number of objects you gather is equal 
to your maximum Exertion.  
You have the following options:  
Option 1- Protect (free Action, can be used on 
opponents Turn).  You may sacrifice a blunt object 
to reduce the damage of an incoming attack by 10 or 
sacrifice a sharp object to reduce the damage by 5.  
Option 2- Fling (Takes one Combat Action).  Choose 
how many objects you want to fling at a target.  
Flinged objects no longer float around you.  Roll 
Perception + Telekinesis + (number of objects thrown) 
To-Hit.  Each blunt object deals 3D6 damage while 
each sharp object deals 3D10.

you may spend Anomalous Energy points as “Power

Can not be purchased by Amida Disruption Class

This ability allows you to become partially or fully

Character Creation
