Chapter four:  
Advancement
Skill Caps
Once a Skill reaches half of its governing Attribute 
Rating (Rounded up) it can not be increased any 
further using Skill Points.   This rule is listed on your 
character sheet in the cost reminder box.
For example, if your Dexterity Rating was 8 you could not 
increase Dodge, Melee, Disarm, etc. after they reach level 4, 
because all of those skills are governed by Dexterity.   
(Governing Attributes are listed after each skill on your character sheet.)
Throughout Character creation you can typically ignore the Skill 
Caps rule because you are increasing your skills with Aspects 
or Classes.  It’s only after you’re done with Character Creation 
(or at the “Spend Remaining Points” step on page 32) that Skill 
Caps come into effect.  It’s often best to increase the majority of 
your skills with Aspects instead of buying your skills individually, 
because not only is it more cost efficient, but it allows you to 
ignore Skill Caps.
It’s still good to be mindful of your skill levels and attribute ratings 
for when you get your end of Chapter Rewards (if you get any!)
recalculate stats
After you increase your Attributes, your Ratings may go 
up.  Check and see if your Speed, Combat, Projectile, Exer-
tion, Max HP, or Reaction/Stagger has gone up as a result! 
(See Calculate Substats on page 28 for Fast or page 30 
for Advanced Character Creation.)  Check the stats listed be-
low when their associated attribute has been increased.
Strength: 	
Combat Rating
Dexterity: 	
Speed Rating and Reaction score
Perception: 	
Projectile Rating
Health: 	
Max HP
Willpower: 	
Exertion
Fate: 	 	
Stagger score
Dodge Skill: 	 Reaction score
Poise Skill: 	
Stagger score
Skill Cap = 1/2 
of governing 
attribute rating.
Most Characters will advance and become stronger through-
out the story.  At the bottom right of your character sheet you 
will see what advancements you get each Chapter based off 
of your Disruption Class.   Chapter Rewards are typically giv-
en at the end of each gaming session, however this can vary 
per campaign.  Your Director should always alert you when a 
Chapter has concluded and you gain your rewards.  
You can not save Chapter Rewards for later spending.  Un-
spent rewards are lost immediately preceding subsequent re-
wards (in other words spend these points or you’ll lose them!)
Chapter Rewards come in the form of two types of points:  At-
tribute Tokens, and Skill Points.  If you don’t remember how to 
spend Attribute Tokens, check out “Spend Attribute Tokens“ 
on page 22.
Chapters Survived
You should keep track of how many Chapters your Character 
has survived!  This will act as a sort of “Level” for your Char-
acter and helps the Director decide what level of threats are 
too dangerous for you.  Each time your Director gives you 
a Chapter Reward, increase this number.  Chapters typically 
conclude at the end of each gaming session.
Spending Skill Points
Each Chapter you will gain Skill Points to increase your Skills. 
The cost to increase a skill is equal to the level it is being raised 
to.  For example, if you have the Athletics Skill at level 3 and 
would like to increase it to level 4, it would cost you 4 Skill Points. 
If you don’t have enough Skill Points to increase a skill, you 
may want to consider the following:
 
You have the option to exchange one Attribute 
Token for two Skill Points.  
This is a great option for many Disruption Classes that are not 
offered many Skill Points each Chapter.  
Remember, you can not save Skill Points between Chapters!
Note:  You can not revert Attribute Dice or Skill Points or back into 
Attribute Tokens.
Additionally, skills have a maximum value called a Skill Cap.
Note:  “Written In skills” usually do not have a Skill Cap.
58
Advancement


Exertion
Think of Exertion as your Character’s adrenaline.  You can 
spend it to get a temporary boost to activities, but you 
require rest afterward.
If your Character gets some rest (see Sleeping and Short Rests 
on page 66), they refresh all lost Exertion.
You can spend Exertion in one of four ways:
Attribute Roll Boost
You must declare you are spending Exertion before rolling 
dice.  You may spend 1 Exertion point to add 1D12 to any 
Attribute roll.  You may spend multiple Exertion to add 
multiple D12 to the roll. 
You can not use this option when the Director requests a 
Punishing Skill Check.  (See Punishing Rolls on page 11.)
 
Speed/Combat/Projectile Rating Boost
You may spend Exertion to temporarily increase your Speed, 
Combat, and Projectile Ratings.  Increasing your Speed 
lasts until the end of the Combat Round, while increasing 
Combat or Projectile Ratings counts for one Action.
You are allowed to spend Exertion to increase Combat/Projectile 
Ratings before or after your To-Hit roll has been made. (This will 
make sure the attack hits your target!)  
For example, if you had a Combat Rating of x2 and you spent 3 
points of Exertion, you would have a Combat Rating of x5 for the 
attack. 
Dodge an Attack
In some cases you can not dodge incoming attacks, such as if 
you have already taken your Turn in combat.  You may spend 
1 Exertion to bypass this rule and roll to Dodge as normal.  
Note:  You can NOT spend Exertion to Dodge if you are Staggered.
General Exertion Use
Exertion can be used to negotiate with the Director, such 
as asking “Can I leap over the fence without having to roll 
dice if I spend a point of Exertion?”  General Exertion Use 
is entirely up to the Director on how it can be implemented. 
Reverence
As a reward for good, interesting, entertaining, smart, 
and/or humorous roleplaying you’ll be awarded 
Reverence Points by the Director.  You can also help out 
by recommending Reverence rewards from the Director to 
other players for their actions.  You begin the game with 
zero Reverence, and you can not have more than seven 
Reverence at any given time.  You can spend these points to 
reroll a skill check.
Reroll Skill Check
After you roll an Attribute Check, if you are unsatisfied with 
the way the dice landed, you have the option of spending 
two Reverence Points to reroll your Attribute Dice and 
replace the result.  
You can not use this option when the Director requests a 
Punishing Skill Check.   (See Punishing Rolls on page 11.)
If you spent Reverence to reroll you may spend Exertion on the 
second roll.  Additionally, any Exertion spent on the initial roll will 
carry over to the reroll.
Reverence overflow
When your Reverence meter becomes full (7 points) and 
the Director awards you additional Reverence, each excess 
point instead gives your character +1 to Maximum HP.
PC’s have two resources to help them push the story in their favor.  Exertion and Reverence.   
59
Advancement
01
57


Combat Procedures
There are a few ways to handle combat in SCP.2e.  Directors 
can handle combat narratively, meaning that no dice rolls are 
involved and they simply explain what happens.  Narrative 
combat works best for SCPs that are extremely overpowered, 
such as SCP-096, SCP-173, SCP-682, SCP-2006, SCP-
3000, SCP-3812, The Scarlet King, and literally thousands 
of others.  Yes, there are many, many, many SCPs which are 
so powerful that your dice and hit points won’t matter.  But 
there are also many, many SCPs that might give you a fighting 
chance, not to mention dealing with other GoI (most likely 
composed of humans) on a regular basis.  
Standard Combat Procedure
In any Roleplaying game when Characters begin to fight or 
there is a conflict with other Characters in some way, time 
needs to slow down and we must calculate each moment.  This 
is called “Combat Time”; Characters are only able to move a 
distance equal to their Move Speed, most Actions require an 
Attribute Check or a Versus Roll, and advanced combat rules 
are implemented.  Each Combat Round represents a period 
of around 2 seconds.  Each Round is split up into Turns taken 
by each of the Characters.  This chapter will walk you through 
how combat is structured.
Combat Overview
When Combat Time begins, the Director will explain the im-
mediate danger and give a brief description.  After that Com-
bat Rounds initiate and Characters take Turns to complete two 
Combat Actions.  The person who controlled the last Charac-
ter to act will decide who gets to take the next Turn.  Once 
all Characters have taken one Turn, the next Combat Round 
begins and the process is repeated.
When to Initiate Combat Time/Ambushing
Sometimes it will be quite obvious when Combat Time starts; 
The PC’s open a door and see an enemy draw their gun- 
suddenly combat begins.  Other times it’s not quite so clear. 
Combat Time begins as soon as any Character performs, or is 
suspected to perform, an action that another Character would 
likely want to prevent.  This means that if Characters are in a 
small meeting talking about business for a period of time, and 
suddenly a Character is shot in the shoulder from a distance, 
Combat Time begins immediately after the stray bullet hits its 
target.  While not in combat, a Character’s Defense Score (the 
difficulty to hit them) will default to their Reaction score.  You 
can treat it as if the first Character to take an offensive action 
acts first in the Combat Round.  For example, you could pull out 
your gun and fire in one round.  If nobody was expecting you 
to do this, you would get the first Turn in Combat, and make an 
attack against the enemies Defense score.  After the action is 
resolved, you get to choose who acts next in Combat (see Tak-
ing Turns, below).  Characters may plan an ambush to exploit 
this rule.
Combat Phases
Pre-Combat: Explanation
The Director will take a moment to explain the situation to 
all combatants in detail, possibly even draw out a map and 
show where all combatants are located.  They will explain the 
distance in relation to the other Characters and answer any 
questions.  Optional pre-generated maps could have a hex-
agonal or square pattern upon them, and each Unit should 
represent one meter (often referred to as a Unit) in distance. 
Character movement is set up in meters, so if your Character 
has a Move Speed of 3, you can move 3 meters per Move 
Action.  The PCs should ask any clarifying questions about the 
area and positions of the Characters at this opening phase. 
Remember though, this phase is only about information clari-
fication and not declaring Actions.  Once the Director feels the 
situation has been properly explained, the next phase begins.
First to Act:
In most cases the Director should simply decide who is the most 
appropriate Character to act first in the first Combat Round. 
In other cases the Director may call for an Initiative roll. 
To do this, all Characters roll Intellect + Awareness to deter-
mine their Initiative score. (The Awareness Skill uses Perception 
as it’s governing Attribute, but you ignore this fact and roll Intel-
lect + Awareness instead.)  The player with the highest Initiative 
score chooses who goes first for the first Combat Round.  If 
there’s a tie, the winner of the tie goes to the player with the 
youngest Character.  If an NPC has the highest score, the Di-
rector decides who goes first. 
Taking Turns:
The player who goes first takes their Turn and performs up to 
two Combat Actions.  After their Turn, they choose what Char-
acter goes next (may choose a PC or an NPC).  If they choose an 
NPC, the Director decides who goes next, which may be an-
other NPC.  If an ally NPC is chosen, the Director should pick 
someone to go next who will benefit the players team, while 
if an enemy NPC is chosen, they should choose someone who 
will be detrimental to the player’s team.  Each Character gets 
one Turn per Combat Round.  Once everyone has had a Turn, 
the round ends, and the next Combat Round begins.  Instead 
of rolling Initiative again, the Character that acted last gets to 
choose who acts first on the next round- and they MAY choose 
themselves!  When many PCs/NPCs are involved in combat, 
the Director should note Character Turns using tally marks 
( 
 ).  Using tally marks will not only keep track of who has 
and has not yet acted, but will also keep track of how many 
total Combat Rounds have passed.
Chapter Five:  
Combat
Combat
60


Defense & Evading
When an opponent tries to hit your Character, your Defense 
Score determines the difficulty to hit you.
Before You’ve Taken your Turn:
If attacked before your Turn in the current round, roll Dex-
terity + Dodge to evade.  If the roll is lower than your com-
bined Dexterity Rating and Dodge skill, adjust it to match.  
The final result becomes your Defense Score.
(Note: Dodging is not considered an “Action”)
After You’ve Taken your Turn:
If attacked after you have finished a Turn, your Defense 
Score is equal to your Dexterity Rating plus Dodge skill.  
This number is also called your “Reaction” score, and is 
listed at the bottom center of your character sheet. 
You have the option to spend 1 Exertion to roll Dexterity + 
Dodge and use the result as your Defense Score instead.
Additionally, Characters have the option to take a “Defense 
Action” on their Turn (see page 63) which involves using one 
of your Actions to treat an attack as though you haven’t taken 
a Turn yet, regardless of whether or not you’ve already acted.
Free Movement:
After you successfully evade any attack, you have the option 
to move 1 Unit in any direction.  
(This free one-Unit movement is an option after any successfully 
avoided attack against your Character, either from a successful 
Dodge roll, or an attack avoided through your Reaction score.)
Note:  An Attack of Opportunity does not trigger Free Movement.
Defending Multiple Attacks:
If attacked by multiple Characters within the same Round, 
subtract 2 from your Defense Score for each additional 
attacker after the first (cumulative modifiers).  Spending 1 
Exertion allows you to roll Dexterity + Dodge to replace 
your current Defense Score without this penalty.  
Note that this penalty does not apply just from facing multiple 
attacks from a single Character.  It is only applied if attacked by 
more than one Character.
Stagger
If you score a Critical Success when attacking, your oppo-
nent becomes Staggered unless they roll Health + Poise with 
a difficulty equal to the damage dealt.  Characters remain 
Staggered until they take a Turn.  The stagger effect continues 
even to the next Combat Round if the Character hasn’t acted. 
While Staggered, the difficulty to hit the Character is equal 
to the Character’s Reaction or Stagger score, whichever is 
lower  (these values are listed at the bottom of your sheet).
Additionally, any Damage Reduction (DR) the Character has 
is ignored.
Do not include your Poise Skill Multiplier (if you have one) when 
calculating your Stagger score.
 
Staggered Characters can never dodge. 
If a Melee Attack is performed against a Staggered Charac-
ter, the Damage is doubled.
Note: When you deal double damage, you roll your damage as 
normal and then double the result.
See example on page 178.
New Directors may find it helpful to keep this page readily accessible during 
combat to aid in recalling the rules until they become more familiar with them.
Combat
61
Defense & Stagger


Combat Action Types
When a Character’s Turn comes up in combat, they may de-
clare up to two Actions, such as moving & attacking, or mov-
ing twice.  These are called Combat Actions.  You can even 
choose the same Action twice in a row.  There are three types 
of Actions to be aware of.  
Standard Actions   
Standard Actions are typical actions taken by a Character 
in combat.  You may choose any two Standard Actions or 
choose the same Action twice.  Examples include movement, 
shooting a gun, swinging a sword, or ducking behind a car. 
All Standard Actions will be given detailed rules in the “The 
Eight Combat Actions” section on the following page.  Most 
Combat Actions are Standard Actions.
Non-Standard Actions
Non-Standard Actions are any actions that the players wish 
to perform that are not covered in these rules.  Non-Standard 
Actions use up one Action just as Standard Actions do (you can 
only perform up to two Actions each Turn).  Directors decide how 
all Non-Standard Actions play out.
Extended Actions
Extended Actions take longer than normal Actions.  Some-
times they may use up both of your Actions you get per 
Turn, other times they could take several consecutive Turns 
to resolve.  If you would like to perform something while in 
combat that would take a little while to complete, the Direc-
tor may declare you must partake in an Extended Action. 
Free Actions
Free Actions cost the Character no time at all, and a few may 
be done for free each Turn with some limitations.  Examples 
include flipping a light switch, turning your head, dropping a 
weapon to the ground, or speaking. 
(Directors can allow PCs & NPCs to speak to each other 
mid-combat to add drama and tension even if it goes against 
realism if they desire.) 
Damage
After an attack lands against the opponent, dam-
age is dealt.  To calculate how much damage they 
receive, use the following formula:  
Base Damage + (X Damage times your Multiplier). 
All weapons will list an X Damage, such as 1D8. 
If you are attacking with a Melee weapon, this 
is multiplied by your Combat Rating.  If you are 
attacking with a Firearm, it is instead multiplied 
by your Projectile Rating.  
Unlike Attribute and Skill checks, Damage dice never explode 
and you add all the dice together.
Example:  You have a knife with a Base Damage of 1D4 and an X 
Damage of 1D6.  Your Combat Rating is x2, so you would deal a 
total of 1D4+2D6 damage.
Damage Reduction 
If your Character has any Damage Reduction (DR) you would 
reduce the damage you take by that amount before applying 
to your current HP.  DR is always applied last after all other 
calculations, such as double damage.
Mutilations
Some situations may cause a Character to become Mutilat-
ed.  Additionally, players may choose to take a Mutilation 
voluntarily for a chance of reducing the damage they receive. 
Mutilations often cause permanent disadvantages and run 
the risk of killing your Character outright, so it’s usually only 
in rare cases that a player should consider this desperate and 
risky option.
Death Saves
If you ever go below 0 HP that’s bad news.  Your Character 
falls to the ground and is now in the Crouch/Prone stance (see 
Crouch & Prone on page 66).  At the beginning of your next 
Turn, you must roll Fate + Resist Death, and score equal to or 
greater than twice the amount of negative HP you have.  This 
roll is called a Death Save.  
If you succeed a Death Save you go to Zero HP.  Characters 
with exactly Zero HP are limited to just one Action on each of 
their Turns until they heal at least 1 HP.
If you fail a Death Save your Character dies and your game 
is over!  Your Character is allowed to take one more Combat 
Action (or two if you can spend a point of Exertion) but that is 
your final act of the game!
Note:  You can not restore HP if you go into negative numbers.
Combat
62


Aim
All firearms will list an “Aim” bonus.  Performing the Aim 
Action will give you a bonus to the next time you fire that 
gun, even if it is on a later Turn.  You must declare a Target 
while Aiming.  If you lose line of sight to your Target, or your 
Character takes any Action besides Defense, they lose their 
Aim bonus.  Dodging an attack or taking damage does NOT 
cause your Character to lose their Aim bonus.  Unless otherwise 
noted, you can only perform the Aim Action one time before 
firing.  Your To-Hit modifier is still applied to your To-Hit roll. 
For example, if your To-Hit was -3 and your Aim bonus was 
+5, if you aimed and fired you would get a +2 to your roll. 
Disarm
This Action requires you have a free hand and are within one 
Unit of your opponent.  You attempt to pry away a weapon or 
object from them.  Roll Dexterity + Disarm versus your oppo-
nents Defense Score.  If you get a Critical Success, you steal 
the item.  If Disarm is a non-Critical Success, roll Strength + 
Disarm versus your opponents Strength + Break Free.  If you 
succeed, the item drops to the ground.  
Restrain
This Action requires that you are within 1 meter of your 
opponent.  Roll your Dexterity + Brawn versus your 
opponents Dexterity + Break Free.  If you fail, the Action is 
wasted.  If you succeed, neither you nor your opponent can 
take Combat Actions until they break free or you let them go. 
On your opponents Turn, they may spend Actions to attempt 
to escape, but take no other Actions until free. When they 
spend an Action they roll Strength + Break Free versus your 
Strength + Brawn.  If they succeed, the grapple is escaped, 
otherwise their Action is wasted.  While restraining your 
opponent, consider both Characters as a single target with 
a Defense Score equal to the lower of the two Characters’ 
Reaction scores.  Both Characters will split damage from 
regular attacks (Director’s discretion) unless a Called Shot is 
performed, which bypass this rule.  
See Called Shots on next page.
Move
Players may declare a Move Action to move a number of 
Units (meters) equal to their Move Speed throughout their 
Turn.  This Action may be performed multiple times to simulate 
running.  The Move Action covers all forms of movement while 
in combat, such as jumping a fence, entering a car, turning 
to look in a different direction, or peeking around cover. 
Directors can adjust the distance your Character moves 
depending on terrain, doors, and other obstacles.  Characters 
can take their other Action mid-movement if approved by the 
Director.  If you declare the Move Action twice in one round, any 
opponents aiming at your character lose their Aim bonus.
Use Item/Reload
If you need to use a generic item such as a radio, fire ex-
tinguisher, or banana, you use the Use Item/Reload Action. 
This is a catch-all Action for any non-combat related, station-
ary movement.  Use Item/Reload is used when your Charac-
ter needs to reload their gun (change magazines).  You can 
put away any weapon with just one Use Item/Reload Action. 
In order to draw a weapon look at the Weapon Slots “Actions 
to Draw” box to determine how many Actions are required to 
ready the weapon.
Defense
Treat attacks against your Character later this round as if 
you have not yet taken your Turn. (Roll Dexterity + Dodge 
to evade.  If the roll is lower than your combined Dexterity 
Rating and Dodge skill, adjust it to match.  The final result 
becomes your Defense Score.)
Melee Attack
Melee weapons will list a Range of 1*.  The asterisk denotes 
that the Melee weapon has a maximum range of 1 Unit, 
meaning you can only attack opponents 1 meter away or less. 
To attack your opponent, roll Dexterity + Melee versus your 
opponents Defense Score.  If you succeed, you roll the Base 
Damage of your Melee weapon plus (X Damage multiplied 
by your Combat Rating).
Fire
When rolling to shoot a gun, you roll Perception + Firearms 
against your opponents Defense Score, but there are a few 
other factors that are applied to this To-Hit roll.  Firearms will 
list a To-Hit modifier.  This is always applied to your To-Hit roll 
when firing that gun.  Firearms will also list a Range.  Count 
how many Units away your opponent is from you, and divide 
that by your Range (rounded down).  The result is subtract-
ed from your To-Hit roll.  If you succeed, you roll the Base 
Damage of your Firearm plus (X Damage multiplied by your 
Projectile Rating).  If the same gun is fired twice in one Turn, 
subtract the guns Recoil from the second To-Hit roll.
The Eight Combat Actions
Combat
63
Combat Actions


Called Shots
If you want to aim or strike at a specific location on a single 
opponent for an extra effect or damage, perform a Called 
Shot.  You must decide on one of the four desired outcomes 
(see below) before rolling dice.  
Roll To-Hit as a Punished Skill Check.  The Director may then 
adjust your opponents Defense Score depending on how 
difficult the Called Shot would be to pull off.  
If you hit but don’t get a Critical Success, you deal only the 
Base Damage of your weapon to your opponent.  
Only if you get a Critical Success is the Called Shot successful. 
Called Shots have four outcomes you may choose from:
• 
 Deal extra damage:  Increase your Combat or Projectile 
Rating by +5 for this attack.
• 
 Automatically Stagger the enemy:  Your opponent will not 
get to roll to resist Stagger.  Attack deals Base Damage only.
• 
 Cause a desired effect:  Disarm your opponent by shooting 
their weapon or slow them down by shooting their legs. 
Desired effects cannot outright kill the target or cause 
effects similar to Mutilations, but you can request other 
types of results to the Director.  Attack deals no damage.
• 
 Attempt a Fatality.    If your opponent’s current 
HP is half or less than their Maximum HP, then the 
attacker performs a Fatality.  The attacker gets to 
describe the effects of their devastating attack, which 
kills the enemy instantly (or knocks them unconscious!) 
If the opponent did not have half or less their Maximum HP, 
Roll damage as normal.  Note:  Your Director may declare some 
types of NPCs as being exempt from Fatalities and Mutilations, 
such as SCP’s.
When Calling a shot with a Projectile weapon, you must Aim 
before firing (see Aim Action on page 63).
See example on page 179.
Dual Wield
Characters may wield a weapon with one hand if their Strength 
Rating equals or exceeds the weapon’s Mass, allowing them 
to equip two weapons simultaneously.  When dual wielding, 
Characters must designate one as the Primary Hand and the 
other as the Off Hand, specifying which weapon goes where. 
Apply the following rules:
• 
Off Hand To-Hit rolls are considered Punished Skill 
Checks.
• 
Off Hand damage multipliers (Combat/ Projectile ratings) 
are halved, rounded down when calculating damage.
• 
Roll one less Die for your To-Hit Dice Pool for both 
weapons.
• 
If you use the Fire and/or Melee action(s) and utilize 
both weapons in each of your hands on the same Turn, 
you gain one bonus Combat Action.
• 
While Dual Wielding, you cannot perform Use Item/
Reload, Disarm, or Restrain Combat Actions.
• 
The Aim Action is performed independently* for each gun.
• 
Recoil is applied independently* for each gun.
• 
You may only target opponents within a 45-degree cone 
of vision.
*If you Aim with Gun A, the bonus is only applied to the next time 
Gun A is fired. You may Aim twice (once for Gun A and once for 
Gun B), and the Aim bonuses are applied to each gun when they 
are fired.  After Gun A is fired its Recoil is only applied if Gun A is 
fired again later that Turn.
Dual/Triple Tech in Combat
If a Dual/Triple Tech is performed during Combat (See Dual/
Triple Tech on page 11) all PCs involved must agree to act at 
the same time (and haven’t yet taken a Turn).  The PCs involved 
must perform the exact same Actions in unison (move and at-
tack, aim and attack, etc.)  If the Action results in rolling Dam-
age against an enemy, only one PC is chosen to roll based on 
the weapon they are using, but they get a temporary bonus to 
their Combat or Projectile Rating equal to the number of PCs in-
volved.  After the Triple Tech is complete, the PC who declared 
the Triple Tech will choose who acts next in combat.  (All PCs 
who were involved in the Triple Tech have expended their Turns). 
Dual/Triple Tech used in Combat must target a single enemy.
See example on page 173.
Special Attack Rules
Combat
64


Attacks of Opportunity
If a Character comes within one Unit of you, and then leaves 
that Unit, you get to perform a free Melee Attack against that 
Character immediately.  
Attacks of Opportunity always involve an independent 
Versus Roll of Dexterity + Melee vs Dexterity + Dodge. 
(This Versus roll does not affect or replace your current Defense 
Score, nor does it take into account if a Character has acted this Turn.) 
Note: “Free Movement” (see page 61) does NOT trigger an 
Attack of Opportunity.  You may also prevent an Attack of Oppor-
tunity by only moving one Unit away with a Move Action.
Momentum Attacks
If you perform a Move Action (in a straight line) immediately 
before a Melee Attack on the same Turn, increase your Com-
bat Rating by the number of Units traveled (not exceeding 
your current* Combat Rating) for that attack.  
*Combat Rating can be increased with Exertion.
See example on page 167.
Tackling
If you perform a Momentum Attack, you have the option of 
performing a Tackle.  Roll Dexterity plus Brawn (instead of 
Melee) and increase your To-Hit modifier instead of your 
Combat Rating for each Unit you traveled this Combat Round. 
The additional bonus to hit cannot exceed your Character’s 
Strength Rating.  You then deal your Unarmed Attack Damage 
to the enemy (see below).  This attack will also bypass your 
enemies Damage Reduction (if any).  If you score a Critical 
Success with this type of attack, your opponent receives 
the Knocked Back Mutilation effect (without the reduction in 
damage; see “Knocked Back” on page 68).  
Unarmed Attack Damage
Characters that are not equipped with a Melee Weapon can 
still perform Melee Attacks with their fists, kicking or the butt 
of their guns.  The damage that unarmed Melee attacks deal 
is based on the Character’s Strength Rating using the below 
chart.
Strength

X.Dam

1D12

Overwatch
You may delay pulling the trigger of your firearm until later 
on.  Instead of declaring you are firing your gun, you can 
declare an Overwatch on a 45o cone of view to a chosen di-
rection.  If at any point a Character travels through this line 
of sight, you can declare that Character as your target and 
resolve your Action to Fire immediately.  You may only de-
clare you are firing at one opponent using Overwatch.  Your 
Overwatch remains in effect until you take the shot, or you 
cycle through to your next Turn.  Combining Overwatch with 
the Aim Action is a great way to deal with enemies who peek 
around corners quickly.  If an Overwatch attack causes the 
enemy to become Staggered, their Turn ends immediately!  
Note: Enemies who enter the Overwatch cone of view and are shot at 
will have the option to Dodge, since they have not yet finished their Turn.
If Overwatch carries over to the next Round, you may resolve your 
shot and take your following Turn as normal, but must include any 
recoil modifiers caused from firing the gun.
Sustained Aiming
After you Aim the maximum number of times for a 
gun, if your To-Hit bonus exceeds your Characters’ 
Dexterity Rating + Firearms skill, then each additional Turn you 
maintain that Aim bonus you must spend 1 point of Exertion. 
This simulates a Character holding their breath while looking down 
the scope for long periods of sustained aiming.
Shooting past an Ally
If you are attempting to shoot an opponent but an ally is 
slightly in the way, your opponent gets a +2 to a +6 modifier 
to their Defense Score (Director’s discretion) depending on 
the severity of the situation.  If you get a Critical Failure, the 
bullet hits your ally instead, but only deals Base Damage.
See example on page 173.
Spray & Pray
If your firearm has an Automatic setting, you may target all 
visible enemies within a 45o cone of view.  Roll Perception + 
Firearms as normal, and then each targeted enemy compares 
this roll against their Defense Score.  Those who fail the check 
take damage.  When rolling damage, you roll one less dam-
age die for each enemy you targeted, regardless of whether 
or not they were hit.  If your damage dice use multiple die 
types, you must reduce damage by the highest die type first. 
For instance, if your damage is 4D10+3D6 and you shot at 
three enemies, subtract the D10s first, resulting in dealing 
1D10+3D6 damage to the enemy.  
Spray & Pray reduces the weapons magazine by 10 rounds.
Note: When rolling damage to multiple enemies using this method, 
roll separately for each target.
See example on page 172.
Special Melee Attack Rules
Special Firearm Attack Rules
Combat
65


Crouch & Prone
Characters can Crouch or go Prone (lay down on the 
ground) by spending a Combat Action.  Likewise they 
can spend a Combat Action to stand up (remove Crouch/
Prone).  While in Crouch/Prone, apply the following: 
Add +2 to Defense Score vs Projectile attacks.  Character can 
only perform the following Combat Actions while in this state:
• 
Fire
• 
Aim
• 
Move (1 Unit only)
• 
Defense
• 
Use Item/Reload
Unconscious
Characters that become Unconscious fall to the ground and 
can not take any Actions until they wake.  To wake up, the 
Character must roll Health + Resilience as a Timed Roll (See 
Timed Rolls on page 11) with a pace of one Combat Round 
and a difficulty of 18.  If the difficulty is not made after all 
Health Dice are rolled, reduce the difficulty by 2 and repeat 
the process.  Other Characters attempting to help wake them 
can also reduce this difficulty (Director’s discretion).  Once the 
difficulty is made the Character wakes up without any sense 
of how much time has passed.  The Director may increase the 
Timed Roll pace or difficulty depending on the situation.  The 
Defense Score of Unconscious Characters is equal to their 
Fate Rating. 
On Fire
If your Character catches fire, signified by gaining a Fire 
Intensity Level, they suffer 1D10 damage for each level of 
intensity at the end of every Combat Round.  The Intensity 
Level then increases by 1 (up to a maximum of 5) unless the 
Character is in the Prone position, then it is instead reduced by 3. 
Characters on fire get a -1 penalty to all skill checks for each 
level of Intensity.
See example on page 178.
Poisoned
If a Character becomes Poisoned, they must roll Health + 
Resilience difficulty 16 at the end of each Combat Round, 
and see below.
• 
If the roll gets a Critical Success, an Antidote is used, or 
the Character goes below Zero HP, the Character is no 
longer Poisoned.
• 
If the roll gets a standard Success, the reduce the difficulty 
by 1 (but Character remains poisoned).
• 
If the roll fails, increase the difficulty by 1 and the 
Character takes 1D6 damage.
• 
If the roll gets a Critical Failure, increase the difficulty by 
2 and the Character takes 1D10 damage.
Characters below zero HP can not be healed through 
non-anomolus means.
(except for CPR with Defibrillator; see below.)
First Aid (Without First Aid Kit)
Performing First Aid without a First Aid Kit requires 4 
consecutive Combat Actions.  Roll Intellect + Medical. 
Character will recover an amount of HP equal to the result of 
this roll minus 10 (minimum zero).  First Aid without a First 
Aid Kit cannot restore HP beyond half of your Max HP. 
May only be performed on each Character once between rest periods.
First Aid (With First Aid Kit)
Performing First Aid with a First Aid Kit requires 2 consecutive 
Combat Actions.  Roll Intellect + Medical.  Character will 
recover an amount of HP equal to the result of this roll.  If used 
on self, half the amount healed.  First Aid Kits are good for 
one use only. See example on page 170.
CPR (Without Defibrillator)
If a Character fails a Death Save, another Character 
can attempt CPR (without a defibrillator) by spending 4 
consecutive Combat Actions and rolling Intellect + Medical. 
The difficulty for CPR is equal to the Character’s negative 
HP + (The number of Combat Rounds that have passed). 
If CPR succeeds, then the dead Character may attempt to roll 
a Death Save one additional time.  
CPR (With Defibrillator)
If a Character fails a Death Save, another Character 
can attempt CPR (with a defibrillator) by spending 2 
consecutive Combat Actions and rolling Intellect + Medical. 
This inflicts 1D4 damage upon the incapacitated Character 
for each instance of CPR they’ve received during their lifetime. 
The difficulty for CPR is then equal to the Character’s negative 
HP + (The number of Combat Rounds that have passed).  If 
CPR succeeds, the incapacitated Character recovers HP equal 
to half of the result of the CPR roll (rounded up) and they are 
granted one extra attempt to roll a Death Save.  
(CPR with Defibrillator is the only way to “heal” Characters that are 
below zero HP.) 
Sleeping & Short Rests
After a full night of sleep, roll Health + Resilience.  Your 
Character heals HP equal to the Score of this roll (Top two 
dice + Resilience).  Characters can also take a Short Rest by 
relaxing for 30 minutes at single location.  This will restore the 
Character’s HP equal to their Health Rating, but can only be 
performed twice per day.  
Performing a Short Rest or Sleeping will restore all lost 
Exertion.  See example on page 174.
Special Status and State Rules
Recovery Options
Combat
66


The first time an attack would reduce a Character’s Hit Points below zero each Combat Encounter, they must roll for a Mutilation. 
Mutilations often reduce incoming damage significantly, but other times they can cause permanent disadvantages or even 
outright kill you.  Players have the option of taking a Mutilation for any incoming damage.  If a Mutilation halves incoming 
damage, always round down.  If a Mutilation says your Character dies instantly, you do not get to roll a Death Save or perform 
a final dying act.  You are instantly dead.
Mutilations can be optional if it helps the pace of your game.  Instead of using the Mutilation system Directors can simply describe a 
gruesome event whenever a Mutilation would normally occur.  When doing so Directors are encouraged to reduce raw damage in 
exchange for applying a permanent disadvantage.
Mutilations
How to determine a Mutilation
The Director will determine the Damage Type of the attack (for example a sword would have the Cutting damage type, and 
a baseball bat would have Blunt).  Each Mutilation is assigned a Severity rating by rolling a twelve-sided die (1D12), then 
find a Mutilation of that level of Severity which matches the attacks Damage Type.  If the Damage Type does not have a cor-
responding Mutilation at the exact Severity rating rolled, follow the arrow matching your grayed out Damage Type to find the 
appropriate Mutilation.  
For Example, if the Director determines that the Damage Type is Blunt, and the D12 roll came up as a 1, the Mutilation list 
shows “ ↓ ” meaning that you should look at the next highest Severity rating to see if Blunt is listed.  Continue this process until 
you come to a Mutilation for Blunt damage.  Severity Level 2 shows “
“ Meaning that it is compatible with Blunt damage.
Damage Types are visually represented by icons for quick identification.
Directors should judge the appropriate Mutilation for ambiguous Damage Types.
Directors may also incorporate Special Damage Types, such as anomalous or spiritual damage.  The Director will determine 
the outcome of Mutilations (if any) when incorporating Special Damage Types.
Blunt
Cutting
Piercing
Fire
Electricity
Explosion
Acid
Cold

This section provides a resource to maintain the consistency 
of your game.  It outlines universal rules governing the inter-
actions between objects and Characters within your game 
world.  These rules often address uncommon scenarios that 
may or may not arise during typical gameplay.  They serve 
primarily as reference for Directors to apply them as needed, 
but Directors should improvise as needed during the heat of 
the moment when complex circumstances arise.  If a Charac-
ter gets into a car accident, crashes through the window, and 
falls into a frozen lake, the Director should estimate the  dam-
age and outcome then move forward, rather than looking 
up the rules for car crashes (page 76), fall damage (page 
72), cold environments (page 77), and underwater 
movement (page 77).  These rules should only be followed 
if they add an interesting element to the situation.  None of 
this section needs to be memorized by the Director.  This 
section is an optional resource.
This chapter is broken down into sections:
Combat Guidelines
Rules that may or may not pop up in combat, and not strictly 
necessary for normal play.
Capabilities and Conditions
Rare scenarios that test a Character’s physical and 
mental capabilities.  
Objects
Ways the Director can deal with Characters interacting with 
inanimate objects, such as breaking through walls or lifting/
throwing heavy objects.
Vehicles
Rules for car chases and car accidents.
Environments
Rules for when Characters enter difficult environments, such 
as knee-high water, zero gravity, low Hume areas, etc.
Makeshift Weapons
Rules for if PCs attempt to create makeshift weaponry.
Chapter Six:  
Game Universe Guidelines
Combat Guidelines
Behind Cover/Cover System 
If your Character is behind cover, you may get a bonus to 
your Defense Score (Director’s discretion).
100% of body is exposed = 0
75% of body is exposed = +1
50% of body is exposed = +2
25% of body is exposed = +3
10% of body is exposed = +4
If a Character is completely behind cover, it will require a 
Move Action in order to peek around that cover to take a 
shot, even if they only expose 10% of their body.  Any type 
of movement that alters the state of Combat will always re-
quire at least one Move Action.  Characters are able to peek 
around a corner, fire, and then duck behind again using only 
one Move Action and one Fire Action.  This is allowed be-
cause the Move Action permits movement throughout a Char-
acter’s Turn.  This is a great defensive combat option that can 
only be countered by other Characters using Overwatch (see 
page 65) on their position.
Standard Modifiers
Some situations in Combat will provide a standard +2 bonus 
as a perk to being in an advantageous situation.  These bo-
nuses can combine with each other as well.  For example, 
if you were behind your opponent and fired a gun at them 
point blank, you would get a +4 bonus for your Perception + 
Firearms roll.  Directors are encouraged to apply modifiers to 
rolls according to the current situation, and are not limited to 
the list of standard bonuses.  Note that Attacks of Opportunity 
do not get any of the modifiers listed as they are independent 
skill checks.
Standard bonuses are as follows:
• Attacking an opponent from behind 
• Attacking a Character that is unaware of you
• Using a Firearm at point blank (one Unit away)
• Having the high ground (due to terrain)
• Melee attack a Character that is Prone
Descriptive Bonuses
Some players excel at describing their Combat Actions in 
great detail and help everyone really visualize the experi-
ence.  Reward these players!  Give them a bonus of +1 or +2 
to the associated roll and if they succeed permit an appro-
priate outcome based off of their action, or you can offer a 
Reverence point.  On the flip-side, don’t encourage this type 
of behavior so much that it becomes a distraction at the table.
Game Universe Guidelines
70


Out of Combat Damage
When a Character is attacked and Combat Rounds have not 
yet begun, the Director is encouraged to heavily favor real-
ism over system.  For example, if a maniac with a gun grabs 
someone else and holds them hostage by putting a gun to 
their head and then the maniac decides they are going to pull 
the trigger, the Director should not bother rolling to see if the 
To-Hit roll succeeds, nor should they bother rolling damage. 
Players and the Director should never use the game system as 
a way to shield incoming damage that otherwise would be 
obviously fatal.  However, after Combat Time begins Com-
bat Rules should be implemented (Director’s discretion).  For 
example, if a Character is shot in the head with a sniper rifle 
during a business meeting, that Character will die instantly 
(don’t even bother rolling damage!).  Then after chaos erupts 
and everyone is running around in a panic, the sniper would 
be forced to roll To-Hit and Damage as normal since “Com-
bat Time” has started.
Coup De Grâce
If a Character is at the point in which death is obviously im-
minent, any other Character with a weapon may perform a 
Coup de Grâce with Director approval.  This means that with-
out any further dice rolls, the Character is executed.  This is 
done to save time from pointlessly rolling dice when the out-
come is glaringly obvious. Coup de Grâce may be used freely 
on any sleeping/unconscious Characters, Characters at zero 
HP or less, and similar situations (Director’s discretion).
Pickpocketing
When a Character attempts to disarm another outside of com-
bat, the action shifts to a pickpocket attempt.  Pickpocketing 
may target weapons in weapon slots, or target other items 
and belongings.  The pickpocketer rolls their Dexterity + Thiev-
ery, and must score a Critical Success against their target’s 
Perception Rating combined with their Awareness skill bonus. 
(Note that the victim does not get a dice roll).  Failure to achieve a 
Critical Success exposes the pickpocketer, alerting the target 
to the attempted theft.  If the pickpocketer does achieve a Crit-
ical Success, they successfully obtain the targeted weapon or 
item without alerting the victim.  The Director is encouraged to 
modify the difficulty of pickpocketing based on the awareness 
of the victim, difficulty to pilfer the item, and overall situation.
Fatalities
If a PC Calls a Shot and then performs a Fatality (see page 
64).  The Player is then allowed to describe the event in 
gory detail as if they were temporarily the Director of the 
scene.  Players may receive Reverence rewards for creative or 
shocking executions.  Directors may declare some NPCs are 
immune to Mutilations and Fatalities.  Typically this is done 
with SCPs, but the Director may enforce this rule as they see fit. 
Avoiding Attacks of Opportunity 
Characters that move exactly one Unit away from an adjacent 
enemy avoid an Attack of Opportunity.  After you successfully 
dodge an attack, you have the option of moving one Unit in 
any direction, which is why this type of movement avoids the 
Attack of Opportunity.   If you are trying to escape from an 
adjacent enemy, you can use two Move actions.  For exam-
ple, two Characters are right next to each other.  Character 
A wants to run from Character B but also wants to avoid an 
Attack of Opportunity.  The round begins and it is Character 
A’s Turn.  They use a Move Action but only move one Unit 
away from Character B.  They now are two Units away from 
each other.  Character A then uses the Move Action again, 
traveling 5 Units away.  Character B does not get an Attack of 
Opportunity, regardless of their Melee Range. 
See example on page 173.
Out of Combat Characters
Occasionally the Director may find that one or more PCs have 
entered Combat while the other PCs are in another room, but 
rushing to assist.  To handle this situation, the Director should 
exempt the PCs whom are running to the combat zone from 
having their own Turns, and instead calculate their move dis-
tance at the end of each Combat Round.  Once the rushing 
PCs have entered the combat zone (are able to see enemies) 
then the next Combat Round those PCs will enter the pool of 
Characters that Turns are distributed to.  The same system can 
be used for NPCs called to back up the enemies.
Game Universe Guidelines
71
P


Falling long Distances
If a Character falls a long distance to the ground, to calcu-
late the damage they take, square the number of meters fall-
en, then reduce this number by the Character’s Athletics skill 
(times it’s multiplier) times 5. This is the amount of damage the 
Character receives, which bypasses DR (damage reduction).
Lack of Oxygen
To determine your Character’s breath-holding capacity, com-
bine their Health Rating with their Athletics skill, then multiply 
the total by 20 seconds.  This is how long your Character can 
hold their breath before needing to make a Health + Resil-
ience check at a difficulty of 14. With each passing Combat 
Round, the difficulty increases by +1.  Failing the roll results in 
the Character losing consciousness.
Jump Height/Distance
Characters can jump horizontally a number of Units equal to 
(Speed Rating + Athletics) divided by 2 (rounded down) with a 
minimum of 1.  Half this number again for your vertical jump height. 
Do not include Athletics multipliers when adding to Speed Rating.
Sickness 
When the Director feels that a Character has been exposed 
to a risk of getting sick (common sicknesses) the Director will 
roll in secret the Character’s Health + Resilience difficulty 14 
(Director can adjust this difficulty as needed).  On a Success, the 
Characters immune system prevents the sickness.  On a failure, 
the Character starts to become sick.  Over the course of the 
next 10 hours the Character will slowly lose 1 Exertion per 
hour until it reaches zero.  If the Character sleeps to regain 
Exertion before it reaches zero, or medicine is taken, a second 
Health + Resilience roll can be made.  If this roll is also failed, 
the Character becomes Sick.  If this roll is Critically Failed, the 
Character becomes Sick and Poisoned (see page 66).
While sick, Exertion is reduced to zero (with no restoration 
through sleep) and Max HP is temporarily halved.  When roll-
ing any Attribute, dice pools are reduced by one die.  
Every 6 hours the previously determined difficulty is reduced 
by 1 and an additional Health + Resilience roll can be made. 
Once the difficulty is made the Character is no longer Sick.
Lack of Sleep 
After a period of 36 hours without sleep, Characters must roll 
Willpower + Self Control Difficulty 9.  If the roll is failed, the 
Character will lose all Exertion and slowly begin to halluci-
nate.  If the roll is Critically Failed, the Character gets the “Un-
conscious” status found on page 66.  Every three hours this 
roll must be made again with +1 to its difficulty.  If caffeine is 
consumed reduce this difficulty by 1D4 (up to three times daily.)
Temporarily Disguising SUS
PCs can temporarily lower their SUS scores using Disguise. 
Have the player roll Charisma + Disguise difficulty 10+ (their 
current SUS level x2).  If the roll succeeds, the PC disguises 
their body to look less suspicious for one in-game hour (or 
five hours on a Critical Success).  During this time period they 
get to decrease their SUS level equal to their Disguise Skill. 
Void this system if the PC changes their Uniform at any time. 
Once the time elapses the disguise “wears off” and they must 
gather appropriate disguise materials to perform this ac-
tion again.  The disguise wearing off represents rips in cloth, 
makeup coming off, and general wear-and-tear of the make-
shift disguise.  Characters can NOT permanently alter their 
SUS Ratings using this technique, regardless of how realistic 
or logical it would be in the real world.  Directors should dis-
courage PCs from disguising SUS on a regular basis.
Capabilities & Conditions
Starvation & Dehydration
Although it’s not necessary for your players to declare each 
meal they eat, if a situation comes up where food and water 
become scarce for long periods of time, use the following rules.
Dehydration
After a period of 36 hours without water, Characters must roll 
Health + Resilience Difficulty 5.  If the roll is Failed, the Charac-
ter gets the “Organ Damage” Mutilation found on page 69. 
Every hour this roll must be made again with +1 to its difficulty. 
Starvation
After a period of (Character’s Health Rating) days without 
food, Characters must roll Health + Resilience Difficulty 12.
If the roll is Failed, the Character loses all their Exertion and 
can not restore any until they eat.  If the roll is Critically Failed, 
the Character both loses all their Exertion and get the “Organ 
Damage” Mutilation found on page 69.  Each new day 
this roll must be made again with +1 to its difficulty.  
Game Universe Guidelines
72


Memetics (memes)
Memetic Hazards
Memetic hazards are bits of knowledge that once known, 
can cause mental abnormalities which have various levels of 
severity.  Effects range from minor alterations of memory to 
permanently altering an individuals personality.  
When a Character is exposed to a Memetic Hazard, they roll 
Intellect + Self Control against a Difficulty set by the Director. 
If the roll gets a Critical Success, the Memetic Hazard has no 
effect upon the Character.  If the roll is a standard success, the 
Meme has a minor effect inflicted on the Character.  Failed 
and Critically Failed rolls cause the full effect of the Meme.  
Memetic Kill Agents
Memetec Kill Agents are Memetic Hazards in the form of  im-
ages, phrases, or ideas that are fatal upon exposure.  When 
a Character is exposed to a Memetic Kill Agent, they roll In-
tellect + Resist Death against a Difficulty set by the Director. 
If the roll gets a Critical Success, the Memetic Kill Agent has 
no effect upon the Character.  Otherwise they immediately go 
into cardiac arrest and their HP is reduced to -8.  Death Saves 
can then be attempted at that level.
Antimeme
An Antimeme is an idea with self-censoring properties; an idea 
which, by its intrinsic nature, discourages or prevents people 
from spreading it.  When a Character is exposed to an Anti-
meme the Director will roll that Character’s Willpower + Self 
Control against a chosen Difficulty.  If the roll gets a Critical 
Success, the Memetic Hazard has no effect upon the Charac-
ter.  Otherwise the Antimeme censors itself and the Character 
feels as if they are zapped forward in time to the point in which 
the Antimeme has relinquished control.  After this, Director 
should explain what memories can be recalled without hinting 
as to which memories are real and which are fake.
Infohazards
Infohazards are objects or entities that cause a Memetic effect 
whenever they are referred to, written about, described, spo-
ken of, or even simply conceived.  
Cognitohazards
Cognitohazards are objects or beings which cause a Memetic 
effect upon exposure through sight, sound, touch, proximity, 
or communication.  
Sanity
Many Characters in the SCP Universe are used to seeing the 
strange and unusual on a daily basis, and thus do not require 
sanity checks.  If your campaign involves Characters that are 
not used to the anomalous, sanity checks can be implement-
ed.  If a Character witnesses something terrifying or Love-
craftian in nature, the Director will request an Intellect + Self 
Control skill check with a secret difficulty.  The difficulty for 
this roll is determined by the Director based not only on the 
severity of the event witnessed, but the naivety of the Char-
acter exposed.  If the roll succeeds, then the Character has 
no adverse side effects and future sanity checks should have 
their difficulty reduced.  If the roll fails, the Character experi-
ences a psychological shock.  This can lead to them running 
for their lives, screaming uncontrollably, or balling up into the 
fetal position for several minutes until consoled.  Each of these 
Actions will be forced upon the Character by the Director nar-
ratively.  If the roll gets a Critical Failure, the psychological 
shock will come in the form of a more permanent disadvan-
tage.  It could be a new phobia based on the experience, 
getting streaks of white hair, or anything else the Director 
feels appropriate for the situation.  As Characters fail more 
sanity checks in the future, long-term mental damage can be 
caused which can take the form of hallucinations, personality 
changes, or complete breakdowns which could forever make 
the Character inconsolable.  It is suggested to Directors that 
this sanity system is utilized primarily for naive NPCs rather 
than PCs.  This is because it can take away agency and free-
will from your players, which often leads to unpleasant game 
play.  On the other hand, if your players opt to roleplay a 
slow sanity decline throughout the course of the story, it can 
make for a fun and interesting character arc, and Directors 
are encouraged to reward Reverence points to players who 
decide to act out a Character descending into madness.
Game Universe Guidelines
73


Objects
Object Mass & Durability
If a player attempts to break a weapon, treat the Structure 
Rating (see Building and Structure Damage) as the weapons 
Mass times two.  The Director may choose to apply negatives 
to damage or To-Hit rolls for weapons that have taken dam-
age, but this is not always necessary.  Once a weapon has 
been reduced to zero HP, it is permanently destroyed.    Weap-
ons can only take damage in combat through Called Shots. 
Called Shots aimed at weapons will deal damage directly to 
the weapon and not harm the person holding it.  If a weapon 
is shot at in order to disarm the opponent, the weapon would 
fall to the ground but no damage would be calculated (per 
Called Shot rules on page 64).  Non-weapon objects can 
Building & Structure Damage
Inanimate objects each have a structure rating of one or high-
er which represent not only their “HP” but how much damage 
is reduced by.  Characters hiding behind structures can utilize 
this damage reduction for defense.  If a structure is reduced to 
zero HP, it is effectively destroyed, but it does not necessarily 
collapse.  For example, a brick wall that is reduced to zero HP 
could have a large hole in the center, but it doesn’t automati-
cally mean that the entire building it supports will crumble.  A 
thin glass window has a rating of 2, this means that if 4 dam-
age is dealt to the window in a single hit, it will shatter.  While 
a Brick Wall would require 80 damage (or several attacks 
that dealt over 40 damage to wither away at its HP).

difficult to conceal the item is.

Throwing Objects
If a Character is attempting to throw an object, they roll 
Dexterity + Athletics to hit.  Then determine how many 
Units away the object is being thrown.  If this distance 
is equal to or less than the Character’s Strength Rating, 
then there is no modifier.  For each Unit past this distance, 
apply a “+1 modifier” (see below for how to apply modifier).  
• If attempting to hit an opponent, this modifier added 
to the opponents Defense Score.  If the object thrown is 
a bladed weapon, it deals only its Base Damage to the 
target.  If the object is not a weapon, it deals it’s “Mass 
times two” damage to the target.
• If attempting to hit an object, the Director will deter-
mine the base difficulty, and the modifier is added to it.
• If attempting to land the object onto a specific Unit 
(such as tossing a Grenade) then roll 1D8* and con-
sult the chart to see how close the object lands to the 
intended unit.  *The modifier mentioned above is then 
added to this die roll.  No To-Hit roll is necessary.  
*The Director may adjust the die type rolled depending on the sit-
uation, object shape, and weight.
Intended Unit
*Modifiers greater than +15  fall short of the intended zone.
Game Universe Guidelines

do not require this stat check.  Characters can lift objects 
based upon their Strength Rating + Brawn skill (include Brawn 
Multipliers when applicable) using the below chart.  The 
objects Mass will not always directly match up with the weight 
of the object, so the Director is encouraged to use logic in 
each situation.

Weapons with stats do not require a minimum Strength Rating 
to use.  This chart can be referenced to see if a Character can 
physically lift an object with raw strength, or used with anomalous 
aspects such as telekinesis.  The Director should use common sense 
rather than referencing this chart if the outcome is needed quickly 
or the information doesn’t seem realistic upon consultation.
Fuel & Fire
Characters that take damage from Fire gain the “On Fire” 
status in relation to a Fire Intensity Level (See On Fire on 
page 66).  The Intensity of the Fire represents how easily it 
spreads and how hot it is.  Object fires with an Intensity of 3 
or higher will increase the level of intensity by 1 each Combat 
Round.  Once the fire reaches an intensity level of 5, it will 
spread out 1 Unit in each direction each Combat Round, as 
long as the area is flammable.  
If a Character is On Fire and they come into contact with an-
other flammable source, the fire will spread faster, and may 
cause an explosion.  Each substance will list a minimum Fire 
Intensity and an effect.  If the object comes into contact with 
a Character (or another object) that is on fire at or above the 
level of intensity listed, then an additional effect occurs.
Use the chart below to determine how the flames effect the 
situation.  Directors are encouraged to adjust the effect based 
on each situation.

Splash Damage
Damage caused from explosions or areas of effect can have 
a Splash Damage rule applied.  This simply means that the 
damage dice are reduced by one for each Unit away from the 
initial Unit of damage occurrence.  For example, an explosion 
could have a damage of 4D12 reduced by 1D12 for each 
Unit away from the source when it explodes.  Directors are 
free to utilize this optional damage rule whenever they feel 
appropriate.  Some weapons, such as grenades, will also list 
a damage multiplier.  In these cases reduce the multiplier for 
each Unit before reducing the damage dice.
Game Universe Guidelines
75


Car Chases
When Characters engage in a car chase, the Director will 
declare Combat Time.  Then the vehicle attempting to es-
cape declares a “Speed Level” at the start of each Combat 
Round.   The pursuer then declares a Speed Level as well. 
Declared Speed Level must be  ±5 of that vehicles previous-
ly declared Speed Level (Director can adjust this depending on 
vehicle types).  The Speed Level determines the difficulty of 
navigating through streets and obstacles.  If the pursuing ve-
hicle chooses a Speed Level greater than that of the escaping 
vehicle, it gains one “Gaining Point” and another point for 
every 3 points over.   If they chose a lower Speed Level, it 
loses a Gaining Point, and another for every 3 points lower. 
If they match Speed Levels, there is no change.  Gaining 
Points can go into negative numbers.  At the end of the round, 
the Director declares an Obstacle Die (between D4 to D12) 
representing an unexpected turn or obstacle that the vehicles 
must navigate around or through.  The Obstacle Die is rolled 
once and added to both vehicles’ declared Speed Levels.
If multiple vehicles are chasing, each declares their Speed Levels 
as needed.
At the end of the Combat Round, all drivers roll Dexterity + 
Vehicles with a difficulty equal to their chosen Speed Levels 
plus the Obstacle Die roll.  
• 
If the pursuer fails while the escaping vehicle succeeds, 
the pursuer loses 3 Gaining Points.  
• 
If the escaping vehicle fails while the pursuer succeeds, 
the pursuer gains 3 Gaining Points.  
• 
If both fail, no change occurs.
• 
Critical fails result in a Car Accident (see right).
• 
Critical Successes by the escaping vehicle reduces the 
pursuers Gaining Points by 3.
• 
Critical Successes by the pursuing vehicle increases the 
pursuers Gaining Points by 3.
If a pursuer accumulates 5 or more Gaining Points, they catch 
up to the escaping vehicle and may attempt to force a crash 
the following Combat Round.  If a pursuer ever has -5 or less 
Gaining Points, they lose the vehicle and do not get addition-
al catch-up attempts.  While the pursuer is caught up, the ve-
hicles are neck-and-neck and firearms may be used against 
opposing drivers.  Treat weapon range as 8 Units between 
vehicles, and the Director may apply various penalties de-
pending on visibility and rough terrain.  
Sideswipe
To sideswipe a vehicle and force a crash, the pursuer rolls 
Dexterity + Vehicles, contesting their opponent’s Dexterity 
+ Vehicles.  This roll is allowed as a Combat Action when 
a pursuing driver has 5 or more Gaining Points, and has 
declared the same Speed Level as their target that round.  If 
the pursuer succeeds, they can choose to force the fleeing 
vehicle into a Car Accident (see below) or force them off the 
road to a stop.  If the roll is a Critical Failure, the pursuer gets 
Vehicles
into a Car Accident.  Directors can use the below chart to 
determine the estimated speed for the car chase.

Vehicle Qualification
Characters with a Vehicles bonus of 4* or more are qualified 
to operate air vehicles such as airplanes and helicopters. 
Vehicle bonuses of 6 or more allow you to operate military 
vehicles such as fighter jets and tanks. 
*Vehicle bonus of 4 could be a Vehicle skill of 4, or a Vehicle Skill 
of 2 with a x2 multiplier.
Directors may require that this qualification also requires 
a history or backstory that would reasonably involve the 
appropriate training.  Likewise, a Character who dedicates 
time in-game to train on a specific vehicle type are allowed to 
operate that vehicle type moving forward.
Car Accidents
To determine how much damage a Character takes from 
a car accident (both for Characters in the vehicle or 
getting struck by one) for every 20 Kilometers Per hour 
(or 10 Miles Per hour) add one die and increase all die 
types.  If your Character was wearing a seat belt, reduce 
the calculated speed by 40 KPH (20mph).  The Director 
may add or subtract to the damage to better reflect other 
possible circumstances.  See below for guidance.

Environments
Swimming & Underwater
Characters can swim underwater a number of Units equal 
to half of their “Speed Rating + Athletics Skill” (rounded 
down) with a minimum rating of 1.  When dealing damage 
to a Character that is underwater, reduce the damage by 
half (rounded down).  Swimming Characters get a -3 to their 
Defense Score, but also add their Athletics Skill to it.  Fire and 
Tackle Combat Actions are not available while underwater.  
Zero Gravity Environment
While in a zero gravity environment, The Director is encour-
aged to use ‘theatre of the mind” combat explanations rather 
than a 2D map with a grid.   Also apply the following rules; 
Half all Character movement.  After a Move Action is per-
formed it is repeated the following Combat Round in the same 
direction automatically.  During this time the difficulty to hit 
the Character is equal to their Stagger score, and they cannot 
perform the Defense or Move Actions.  Once the Character 
reaches a solid surface they may perform Actions again as 
normal.  Using Melee or Firearms can alter your Character’s 
trajectory.  Characters hit with a large force will begin to spin 
uncontrollably, become Staggered, and are unable to take 
actions until they grapple a solid surface.
Low & High gravity Environments
If Characters find themselves in an area where gravity is lower 
or higher than earths they will likely require a pressurized suit 
in order to survive.  Provided they do, apply the following 
adjustments below based on the ratio of gravity compared 
to Earth.

Areas that are partially flooded will apply modifiers to

Game Universe Guidelines
77


Dimensional Rifts & Portals
Portals and gateways into other realities can quickly break 
the laws of physics.  You can prevent logistical nightmares 
by creating a mystical hallway between the two realms. 
This will stop Characters from reaching around portals and 
placing their hand inside of the space that would normally 
occupy their own head.  If Directors are comfortable dealing 
with space-breaking dilemmas of this nature it can create 
interesting scenarios where the players can break the laws of 
physics to their advantage.  Ultimately it is up to the Director 
on how strange they want their portals to behave, but ask 
yourself some of the following questions to prepare:
• 
What happens if you touch the edge of the portal?  Will 
the razor thin atoms slice your arm in two?  Is there an 
anomalous force of resistance to prevent this? 
• 
Can air pressure or other conditions seep through the 
portal?  If the portal leads to empty outer space, will 
things get sucked through?
• 
If gravity is shifted in a different direction, how will that 
effect the Characters? 
• 
Can things get ‘stuck’ in the portal? 
• 
How does the portal close?  Does it slowly shrink to a 
point?  Does it vanish in an instant?  Can you get stuck 
between dimensions if you are halfway through when 
they close?  
• 
If a portal shifts where it leads, how would that effect 
someone who has their arm halfway through?
Prepare to know the answer to all of these questions because 
your players will surely probe and experiment to study the 
rules you have set in place.
Time Dilation
Time dilation occurs when Characters spend extended periods 
within dimensions that have faster or slower time rates than 
earth.  Time dilation can also occur when traveling at near-
light speeds.  Directors should be cautious about situations 
where one PC experiences time dilation while others do not. 
This can create a disparity in experiences where some players 
advance hours, days, months, or even years ahead, leaving 
others feeling disconnected from the ongoing game.  Such 
situations may hinder enjoyment for those who find themselves 
“trapped in time.”  Whenever possible involve the entire 
group in time dilation events.  This ensures that all players 
share the experience and no one is left waiting in real-time. 
With that all being said it can be a great moment in gaming 
when your players exit from a long trek within an alternate 
dimension only to find themselves back on earth several years 
in the future (or the past!).
Liminal Spaces
Liminal spaces are alternate planes of existence or pocket 
dimensions which seemingly use a single type of procedurally 
generated reality manifestation.  In other words, liminal 
spaces are alternate dimensions that seem to repeat the same 
thing over and over for infinity.  If your players are unfortunate 
enough to enter a liminal space, Directors should keep the 
following tips in mind:
• 
Liminal spaces never repeat another area exactly. 
For example, if the liminal space is an infinite void of 
suburban streets, each house will have slight variations 
to the others.  Pictures of families may differ.  Some may 
have a dog house while others do not, etc.
• 
Liminal spaces have exits.  There is always a way to leave 
a liminal space dimension, however finding the exit is 
akin to finding a needle in ten thousand haystacks.  Most 
often the exits will simply be located somewhere within 
the infinite maze of the liminal space, but occasionally 
the liminal space can require a series of steps that must 
be performed in order to ‘unlock’ the exit.  The required 
steps could be as obvious to pulling a lever in one area to 
unlock a door in another, or very abstract such as having 
to walk through a doorway backwards with your eyes 
closed. 
• 
Liminal spaces alone usually do not create engaging 
tabletop rpg content.  Directors should be wary of making 
a liminal space the centerpiece of their story.  You can 
have liminal spaces be an obstacle to overcome in order 
to accomplish a greater goal, however simply escaping 
a liminal space should rarely be the end goal itself.  The 
best way to incorporate liminal spaces is to have the 
space itself somewhat under control by the Foundation 
or another GoI.  Perhaps they are utilizing the liminal 
space to travel between two distant points, or keeping 
an SCP in containment within a liminal space.  Rarely will 
your players enjoy a survival simulator taking place in a 
liminal space within a tabletop rpg medium.  
• 
Maps for liminal spaces should only be used if the area 
has significant value.  If your players enter Combat or 
have an established base of operations within a liminal 
space then it can be appropriate to provide a square or 
hexagonal grid to represent Unit lengths.  Avoid endlessly 
drawing out walls and structures that span out for infinity. 
Game Universe Guidelines
78


Low & High Hume Environments
If Characters enter an area with abnormally high or low 
Hume levels, it can alter the local normalcy of reality.   The 
higher a Hume level, the more difficult it is to manipulate 
reality through sheer will.  Most humans and mundane objects 
have a Hume level between 1 and 10 (To determine the 
Hume levels of normal Characters you can roll 1D10) while 
most spatial reality has a Hume level of 100.  SCP Objects 
and Reality Benders often have Hume levels approaching 
or higher than 100.  Spatial Humes can fluctuate naturally 
from levels as low as 50 (Haunted Houses often have Hume 
levels below 70) while dense city locations can often register 
Hume levels in the 130-150 range.  Typically extremely high 
or extremely low altitudes with few counts of cognitive life 
often yield lower Hume levels than average, and locations 
with dense human populations yield slightly higher average 
Hume levels.  Any location with Hume levels below 50 or 
above 150 is considered an abnormal reading by the SCP 
Foundation.  Humans have Hume levels that naturally range 
from 1 to 10, while Type Green Reality Benders typically show 
readings between 80 and 100.  It is important to note that 
these hard values only scratch the surface of forced reality 
alteration.  There are many other side effects to high and low 
Hume environments.  For example, areas with Hume levels 
just below 75 have had the following minor side effects with 
humans that return normal (1-10) Hume levels:
• 
Minor Psychic Phenomena
• 
Seeing shadow figures
• 
OBE (Out of Body Experiences)
• 
Premonitions in Dreams
Areas with abnormally high Hume levels can often be 
identified through the following:
• 
Feelings of Déjà vu
• 
Meditation difficulty
• 
Feelings of extreme vividness
• 
Lack of dreams
Even Characters that have anomalous abilities (i.e. anomalous 
aspects) will have a random number between 1 and 10 as their 
base Hume count.  If an area has Hume levels reaching around 
20 or less, then it’s possible that anyone can attempt Reality 
Bending even if they do not possess the skills necessary to do 
so.
Reality Anchors (see page 120) are a device invented by 
Robert Scranton and can increase the level of Humes in an 
area.  The SCP Foundation often utilizes Reality Anchors to 
prevent SCP Objects from altering reality in a localized zone. 
Reality Anchors utilize a difficult to reproduce technology and 
therefore cannot be mass produced.
Another way to alter reality is through the use of Thaumaturgy. 
Thaumaturgy are procedures and rituals that can cause 
reality to become altered.  In such cases the Hume levels of 
the individual become moot as the Thaumaturgy procedure 
itself is not checked against the spatial hume environment.  For 
more on Thaumaturgy see page 130.  For more on Reality 
Bending, see page 127.
Game Universe Guidelines
79


Radiation Exposure
If PCs enter an area that exposes them to Radiation, the Director should occasionally request a Health + Resilience skill 
check, using the below chart to determine the difficulty (based off the areas Radiation level measured in millisieverts.) 
The Director should request this skill check (without saying the difficulty) whenever they feel it is dramatically appropriate. 
If the check is failed, the next time the Director will instead request a Health + Resist Death skill check using the same difficulty. 
If the Resist Death skill check is failed, the Character will experience the symptoms listed on the chart.

• 
(N) Sickness (Normal Sickness) - See Sickness on page 72 using the difficulty listed on the above chart to resist.
• 
(R) Sickness (Radiation Sickness) - Every 5 minutes the Character remains in the radioactive zone, they must roll Health 
+ Resist Death difficulty listed on the above chart.  If roll is a Success, there is no effect, otherwise the Character 
takes damage equal to the amount this roll fails by, and then becomes Sick (page 72) and Poisoned (page 66). 
Note: Antidotes can not be used to cure this type of “Poison” effect.
• 
Permanent Resist Death modifier - This is noted on the PCs sheet and subtracts from all future Resist Death rolls, permanently. 
• 
Outright Death - After the dice roll is made, the Character has that long to live.  Character will die a gruesome death.
Game Universe Guidelines
80


Makeshift/Crafting Weapons
Even though your Players most often have nearly limitless funding for any objects or supplies they desire, it is possible that 
your players will attempt to create makeshift weapons and destructive devices using common goods.  Directors should refer 
to this page in these situations in order to maintain continuity and balance.  To craft makeshift weapons, you’ll need the listed 
materials on the below chart.  If a material displays an asterisk, a fire source (like matches or a lighter) is also required to ignite 
the weapon.  Crafting a weapon involves a skill check of Intellect + Engineering with the difficulty provided by the list below. 
If the skill check results in a critical failure, the device will explode and inflict its full damage on the crafter (Director’s discretion). 
If you score a critical success, this will halve your crafting time.  If you lack the Engineering skill, you have the option to roll 
Ordnance or Mechanics although the difficulty will get a +5 and it will take twice as long to complete the crafting process. 
Note that players will not roll their Skill Check until after half of the required Time to Craft has elapsed.

Game Universe Guidelines
81


The SCP Foundation
In this section, you’ll discover a comprehensive 
overview of the SCP Foundation’s standard operat-
ing procedures and philosophical approach.  It also 
gives the Director a range of options for selecting the 
scope of the SCP Foundation, as well as the scope 
of anomalous activity.  Unlike most settings, the SCP 
Universe doesn’t adhere to a fixed canon for every 
campaign.  Instead, the Director has the flexibility of 
choosing from a diverse array of options.
While the SCP multiverse (more on this later)  can be viewed 
from the perspective of any of the infinite beings it con-
tains, it is usually viewed from the perspective of the SCP 
Foundation.  This association is why a universe defined 
by infinite diversity is predominantly linked with horror. 
The Foundation sees the world for what it truly is, shap-
ing the public’s perception to shield them from its horrors. 
 
The Foundation is a globe-spanning, world-controlling 
shadow organization dedicated to containing, conceal-
ing, and studying all that which defies ‘consensus reality.’ 
While it imposes its own rules on what is acceptable with-
in this reality, the Foundation is one of many entities with 
such power.  To the Foundation, anomalies are phenomena 
that do not align with baseline reality.  The public must be 
protected from these anomalies and prevented from know-
ing they exist.  For all their ruthless pragmatism, the Foun-
dation is driven by the belief that, should this veil of igno-
rance ever be punctured, humanity would regress to a 
chaotic age of magic, superstition, paranoia, and turmoil. 
 
In contrast to the strange and magical reality of the world in 
which they live, the Foundation’s perspective is clinical and 
scientific.  No matter how fantastical something is, they seek 
to understand it through the lens of science and meticulous 
study.  If infinite variety is the defining feature of the SCP set-
ting, the defining element of the “SCP tone” comes from the 
dissonance between the fantastical world the Foundation lives 
in and the clinical light in which they view it.  In the infinite vari-
ety of their cosmos, they focus on phenomena to be contained 
and mysteries to be studied.  In order to better understand the 
world’s magic and wonder, they employ an elaborate lexicon
