of quasi-scientific terms.  Magic is “Thaumaturgy.” Gods are 
“Pluripotent entities.”  Apocalypses are “K-Class Scenarios.” 
Magical beings and enchanted objects are “SCPs” (sometimes 
referred to as Skips) referred to by classification numbers as-
signed to them by the Foundation.  People with anomalous 
properties are often called “humanoid anomalies” which 
may be contained to prevent disruption of consensus reality. 
 
The Foundation can be extremely influential, even one of the 
most powerful institutions on Earth.  While it does not run any 
single nation, it can have agents embedded in every major 
government and corporation to the point where they can in-
fluence these entities to behave as it needs them to if neces-
sary to contain an anomaly.  In particular, the Foundation has 
many agents embedded within local police departments and 
other emergency services, as they are likely to be the first to 
learn of a newly emergent anomaly that has become a threat 
to the public.
The Veil
The convention of keeping the general public sheltered 
within a “consensus reality” and preventing them from 
knowing about anomalies is referred to as “The Veil.”  The 
Foundation is not the only organization that works to main-
tain it, though it is the largest.  While many do not like the 
Veil, most are unwilling to challenge those organiza-
tions by attempting to violate it.  The Foundation and other 
groups have proven quite capable of thwarting such efforts. 
 
Though the Foundation is part of the Veil, the world behind it is 
quite diverse and includes numerous organizations, entire cities 
such as the extradimensional Three Portlands, several religions, 
and many other wonders hidden from ordinary human sight.
Foundation Structure
The Foundation is a complex bureaucratic organization with 
a quasi-militaristic chain of command and complicated inter-
nal structure. Foundation personnel are sorted into six levels 
(0-5), which define their security clearance.
With a few exceptions, the only personnel with level 5 clear-
ance are the O5 Council (alternatively known as the Overseer 
Council) a group of thirteen individuals who collectively con-
trol the Foundation.  Their names and origins are well-guarded 
secrets.  They are referred to only by their numerical designa-
tion, O5-1 to O5-13.   Members of the O5 Council are of-
ten rumored to be anomalous beings themselves, or immortal 
through mysterious means, as many are seemingly hundreds 
of years old.  The Council denies this, and official Foundation 
protocol dictates that they avoid any contact with anomalous 
items, lest a cognitohazard affect their behavior and compro-
mise the Foundation by controlling its leaders.  All thirteen 
council members hold equal rank and make their decisions 
by majority vote.  Their control over the Foundation is nearly 
absolute as no other body is capable of overriding their de-
cisions except for The Ethics Committee (which rarely occurs).
Chapter Seven:  
The SCP Universe
The SCP Universe
82


Some rumors also speak of a mysterious “Administrator” who 
is independent of the O5 Council and holds equal or greater 
rank than them.  They are also the alleged author credited 
for writing the SCP Mission Statement.  However, it is unclear 
whether such a person actually exists.
Just below the O5 Council is the Ethics Committee, a group of 
Foundation bureaucrats tasked with ethically regulating the 
Foundation to ensure its habitual coldness does not lead it to 
acts of needless cruelty.  The Ethics Committee has the power 
to issue dictations which are binding against any other body 
of the Foundation up to and including the O5 Council itself, 
although the O5 Council has been known to overrule these on 
extremely rare occasions.
Below the O5 Council, the Foundation is divided into various 
Foundation Sites, each with their own Site Director, who is 
usually level 4 personnel.  Under each Director is a variety 
of researchers, Containment Specialists, and security person-
nel.  These individuals make up most of the rank and file of the 
Foundation, and PCs will most likely begin at this level.  Dif-
ferent departments have a wide variety of internal structures, 
suited to their individual purposes.
Various Mobile Task Forces (MTFs) and field agents serve 
the Foundation’s numerous departments and sites, or work 
autonomously, answering directly to the O5 Council.  Field 
agents are often based at one specific site.  MTFs by defi-
nition, often move from site to site, though they may have a 
default base in one location especially if they’re specialized in 
containing a single anomaly that is housed there.  They vary 
in size from single squads to small armies and have a wide 
variety objectives and purposes.  Most of their structures are 
militaristic in nature, with members holding traditional military 
ranks.  The average MTF squad sent out into the field will have 
a single member as leader, another as second in command, 
and the rest serving below them.
The lowest ranking members of the Foundation are D-Class 
personnel.  D-Class personnel are criminals, especially death 
row inmates, recruited from various justice systems and made 
to serve as test subjects for the Foundation.  They are con-
sidered disposable, and while the Foundation claims it will 
release them after a month of service when recruiting them, 
they are in fact retained until they perish, with their memory 
wiped monthly to keep them perpetually under the impression 
they are soon to be released.
In extreme circumstances, when the Foundation cannot ac-
quire enough D-Class personnel through typical means, they 
may draw on homeless or refugee populations to obtain 
D-Class personnel.  This is most likely to happen when D-Class 
meeting very specific requirements are needed to contain a 
specific anomaly.  The Foundation also sometimes demotes 
other personnel to D-Class as punishment for some crime 
against the Foundation.  Despite their disposability, D-Class 
are ultimately Foundation personnel, and some D-Class are 
able to earn promotions and escape D-Class status.  The 
Foundation is especially inclined to do this with non-criminals 
conscripted as D-Class personnel.  In some campaigns, PCs 
may begin as D-Class personnel and be forced to work their 
way up from the very bottom of the Foundation’s ladder.
Foundation Presence Level
SCP campaigns can take place in a variety of iterations on the 
SCP setting.  This means it’s up to the Director how prevalent 
the anomalous is in your universe.  The following list describes 
several iterations on the world of the SCP Foundation, and 
how you can employ each of them to make your campaign 
just as strange as you want it to be.
Unfounded
In some timelines, the anomalous is vanishingly rare, and the 
Foundation does not exist at all.  In an Unfounded campaign, 
the players are likely civilians rather than Foundation agents, 
and they likely live in a world with only a few anomalies (though 
some Unfounded universes may have other factions pick up 
the slack in the Foundation’s absence).  Still, in an Unfounded 
game there is far less a central authority managing the anom-
alous world.  That world is still a well-guarded secret, but no 
single entity is in charge of maintaining the Veil.  Because so 
few anomalies exist, it is unlikely your PCs will be anoma-
lous, though you could choose to explore the lives of anom-
alous people in a setting where the anomalous is very rare. 
Small Foundation
In a Small Foundation campaign, the Foundation exists but 
lacks the staggering power and resources it usually has.  In-
stead the Foundation is a small secret society that maintains a 
handful of sites and contains only a few hundred anomalies. 
This corresponds with how the Foundation was often depicted 
in the SCP Wiki’s earliest works.  The Foundation has resourc-
es at its disposal, and it still fights to maintain the Veil, but 
that struggle is an uphill battle as the Foundation must fight 
it without globe-spanning power and vast intelligence net-
works.  In this world, the anomalous would likewise be rare. 
It’s not an entire hidden world but merely a large handful of 
well-guarded secrets the Foundation keeps.  The Foundation 
is likely opposed by only a few Groups of Interest that exist, 
rather than the dozens of companies and cults featured on the 
modern wiki.  Because the anomalous is so rare in this setting, 
the Foundation is likely to be especially distrustful of it, and 
unlikely to employ anomalous agents.
The SCP Universe
83


Medium Foundation
In a Medium Foundation campaign, the Foundation is large 
and powerful, but not to the point of practically running the 
world.  It can influence the world’s governments and corpo-
rations, but does not control them.  Its resources are consider-
able, but still limited.  Likewise, there are more than a handful 
of anomalous phenomena, but likely nowhere near the thou-
sands of anomalies currently listed on the wiki.  This version of 
the Foundation would still be hesitant to use contained anom-
alies to their advantage, but may now have the resources to 
experiment a bit with putting humanoid anomalies to work.
Global Foundation
This is the Foundation as it is usually depicted in modern 
works.  In this campaign, the Foundation is a massive global 
conspiracy with world-controlling power and nearly limitless 
resources.  As a result, it’s able to maintain the Veil despite 
the anomalous world being as vast and varied as the wiki’s 
thousands of pages of material would suggest.  Entire reli-
gions, countries, and fields of science are concealed from the 
general public.  This Foundation’s vast resources mean they 
are more likely to experiment with using anomalies to their 
advantage, though they would still tend not to employ hu-
manoid anomalies as agents.
Last Hope
The Foundation’s hesitance to employ humanoid anomalies 
as agents is not simply a matter of paranoia or prejudice. 
In most timelines, the Foundation did once create a Mobile 
Task Force, MTF Omega-7 (“Pandora’s Box”) consisting en-
tirely of humanoid anomalies, hoping to use their anomalous 
abilities to the Foundation’s advantage.  This project ended 
in disaster when the murderously violent SCP-076 went on a 
rampage that killed numerous Foundation agents, leaving the 
Foundation hesitant to employ humanoid anomalies again.
In many timelines, that hesitation was eventually overcome, 
and the Foundation attempted a similar project again.  In 
these timelines, the Foundation employs MTF Alpha-9 (“Last 
Hope”) which consists entirely of humanoid anomalies who 
use their anomalous abilities for the benefit of the Foundation. 
Only cooperative and loyal anomalies are employed in this 
task force.  These anomalies defend normalcy just as much as 
any other Foundation agent, even though it means they can 
never openly exist out of Foundation custody.  Often they do 
this because the alternative is to rot in a cage for their entire 
life.  As Foundation agents they have more freedom and pur-
pose than they could ever have otherwise.
In this world, the Foundation has embraced the anomalous 
well enough to accept anomalies as agents, but it still fights to 
hide them from the world at large.  Though Alpha-9 is allowed 
to exist and do important work, it is likely to be tightly con-
trolled, facing special limitations other MTFs don’t deal with. 
At the end of the day they’re still anomalies in containment, 
just ones the Foundation has found a use for.
Like other Mobile Task Forces, MTF Alpha-9 is divided into 
multiple squads, so it would make perfect sense for your play-
ers to play a squad of anomalous agents fighting under the 
Alpha-9 banner.
Broken Masquerade
The larger the anomalous world is, the harder it is for the 
Foundation to keep it a secret.  In some timelines the Foun-
dation fails and the Veil is lifted.  In a Broken Masquerade 
campaign the Veil has fallen and the public is aware of the 
Foundation and the anomalous.  This was likely the result of 
some sort of horrible catastrophe, the details of which you are 
free to invent.  However, the fall of the Veil does not eliminate 
the Foundation’s desire to impose an anomaly-free normalcy 
on the world.  The Veil may no longer blind the public, but 
the Foundation still works tirelessly to contain anomalies and 
minimize their effect on everyday life.  What this means for 
humanoid anomalies varies.  In some timelines they may con-
tinue imprisoning them, perhaps now with the open coopera-
tion of the world’s governments.  However now that they have 
to answer to the general public they may have a hard time 
imprisoning innocent people and could decide that it’s easier 
to monitor them while allowing them to exist in public, espe-
cially now that there’s no longer a Veil for them to endanger.
Now that the public knows about the anomalous the Founda-
tion has far less reason to avoid the use of anomalous agents. 
Even if a humanoid anomaly is too dangerous for the Founda-
tion to allow them to live as a civilian it is a good PR decision 
to give them a fulfilling career within the Foundation.  In addi-
tion to MTF Alpha-9, Foundation Sites likely include mixtures 
of anomalous and non-anomalous agents of various degrees. 
Anomalous personnel that become unruly due to their nature 
are more subject to containment.
Anomalous Country
While the Foundation works tirelessly to conceal the anoma-
lous from most of the world they may not be able to do so ev-
erywhere.  Whether or not the Veil still stands there are pock-
ets of the world where the anomalous is not just known, but 
normal.  For instance your campaign could take place in Three 
Portlands, and extradimensional city where the anomalous is 
known and embraced.  Three Portlands is a haven for mages 
and reality benders who would find themselves contained by 
the Foundation if they lived openly in the wider world.
Three Portlands is only the largest of several “Free Port” cities 
where the anomalous openly exists.  Sloth’s Pit Wisconsin is 
a hotbed of anomalous activity themed around the idea of 
narrative.  SCP-4036 is a mostly ordinary country operating 
under the Foundation’s supervision, where the vast majority 
The SCP Universe
84


of the population follow the anomalous religion of Sarkicism 
and are capable of anomalous flesh manipulation.  The Third 
Antarctic Empire is another nation with anomalous inhabi-
tants.  Indeed, the SCP multiverse is full of anomalous pocket 
dimensions and microcosm worlds, each strange in their own 
way, yet utterly normal to the people who call them home.
A campaign, or perhaps merely a portion of one, set in any 
of these places can explore a locale where the anomalous is 
openly accepted while leaving the rest of the SCP Universe 
largely intact.  Directors are encouraged to find more details 
by visiting the Nexus Hub on the scp.wiki:  
scp-wiki.wikidot.com/nexus-series
Vanguard
In some timelines the SCP-6500 phenomena occurs.  When 
this happens, anomalies everywhere begin to lose their 
anomalous properties, especially when they are contained. 
Because the Foundation does not wish to destroy anomalies, 
they regard the total destruction of the anomalous to be unac-
ceptable.  In some timelines they may resolve this issue and go 
back to maintaining the Veil as normal, but in the Vanguard 
timeline of the “No-Return” canon, the Foundation ultimate-
ly chooses to embrace the anomalous rather than allow it to 
vanish from the world.
In such timelines, the Foundation transforms into “Van-
guard,” an organization which works to protect the public 
from dangerous anomalies while also helping to integrate 
other anomalies into the world.  As a result, the anomalous 
in these timelines are not only known, but truly embraced. 
Anomalous beings can openly exist and mix freely with the 
rest of the world, and there’s certainly nothing stopping them 
from working for Vanguard.
Some tabletop gaming groups might be unaccustomed to 
both the need to maintain secrecy and the moral ambiguity 
that comes from playing Foundation agents.  If this is likely to 
be a problem for your group it may make sense to set your 
campaign in the Vanguard timeline, where the Foundation is 
more openly benevolent and the fantastical is accepted by 
society at large.
Anomalous Worlds
Timelines where the anomalous is accepted are still not the 
strangest iterations of the SCP universe.  In some worlds the 
anomalous isn’t just embraced, it has fully taken over the 
world, fundamentally changing its nature.  This could be be-
cause your campaign is set in a fantasy world where there 
was never a Veil, or because the Foundation failed its mission, 
allowing an anomaly to take over.
Worlds taken over by the anomalous come in infinite varieties. 
In the “Ad Astra Per Aspera” canon, the earth became hos-
tile to human life, forcing humanity to spread across the gal-
axy, where they encounter alien creatures and civilizations. 
“Straight on Till Morning” tells the story of the Foundation’s 
non-earth-based personnel who must make their way home 
after Earth is suddenly moved to a different solar system.
There are various post-apocalyptic timelines in the Founda-
tion’s multiverse, including the “Daybreak” canon where the 
sun has become a deadly anomaly, and the “Insect Hell” can-
on where ravenous locusts have begun devouring the world. 
There are also timelines in which the villainous Daevite civ-
ilization has survived into the modern day, forcing the free 
world into open war with them.  In the “Bellerverse” an apoc-
alypse happened in the distant past, thousands of years later 
a society has emerged that comprehends neither the anom-
alous nor basic technology and misremembers the Founda-
tion’s top agents as gods.  In the “Competitive Eschatology” 
canon, just about every possible apocalypse is happening at 
once leaving humanity scrambling to survive.
Anomalous worlds do not have to be so malevolent.  SCP-
6001 is an alternative dimension where the Foundation and 
several of its adversaries decided decades ago to come to-
gether to and create a Compendium that worked to solve 
every single one of the world’s problems, anomalous or oth-
erwise.  They were so successful that the resulting society, 
called Avalon, is more or less perfect, though it could still face 
threats from the outside.  The “End of Death” canon explores 
a scenario where the Foundation, having made a horrible 
discovery about the true nature of the human afterlife, used 
anomalous means to eliminate death itself, rendering all hu-
mans immortal.
The infinite SCP multiverse can contain countless worlds of 
your own design.  Some stories may be about a more familiar 
Foundation encountering such a world, while others may take 
place entirely within it.  Either way, any world you can imag-
ine has a place in the SCP Roleplaying Game!
For more inspiration check out the scp-wiki!
The SCP Universe
85


Authority
The Foundation has a complex relationship with mainstream 
law enforcement agencies.  For the most part, ordinary law 
enforcement personnel are not under the Veil, and not al-
lowed to know about anomalous phenomena.  However, 
the Foundation maintains a network of informants within the 
world’s law enforcement and emergency service agencies. 
Members of this network include firemen, police officers, 
emergency number operators, and paramedics.  Their job is 
to be on the lookout for anomalous happenings and report 
them to the Foundation so it can be the first organization on 
the scene of an anomalous incident.
Foundation agents arriving at such a scene are likely to be 
disguised as ordinary emergency service workers.  The Foun-
dation has little trouble creating fraudulent police badges and 
similar markers of authority that allow them to fool members 
of the public.  When in public, Foundation agents will almost 
always be disguised as members of other organizations.  If 
they do not appear to be emergency service workers, they will 
disguise themselves as FBI or CDC agents, soldiers, or the like. 
The existence of the Foundation is a secret, so they will often 
avoid wearing Foundation iconography in places where the 
public is likely to see them unless they have plenty of amnestics 
at the ready.  Likewise, Foundation sites are often disguised as 
secretive government facilities or military bases.
While most lower level emergency service and law enforce-
ment personnel are unaware of the anomalous, the leaders 
of these institutions generally are aware of it, and capable 
of contacting the Foundation in the event of an anomalous 
encounter.  Most governments respect both the Founda-
tion’s power and their goals and cooperate with them in 
most situations.  The Foundation has liaisons within every 
world government, as well as international institutions like 
the Vatican, NATO, the World Health Organization and the 
United Nations -  though the UN is most affiliated with their 
own paranormal organization -  the UNGOC (United Nations 
Global Occult Coalition).
The extent of the Foundation’s power relative to the rest of the 
world is well-illustrated by the events of SCP-5000, which de-
scribes an alternate timeline wherein the Foundation attempt-
ed to exterminate humanity.  In the resulting worldwide war, 
the Foundation triumphed, despite all the world’s govern-
ments and groups of interest working collectively against them 
as they unleashed horrifying anomalies across the globe.  For 
more information check out the events of SCP-5000 here:
scp-wiki.wikidot.com/scp-5000
Field Agents & High Death Count
The Foundation can be shockingly ruthless.  The organiza-
tion and its agents have been responsible for an enormous 
amount of death & suffering, and for the most part, they don’t 
regret an ounce of it.  They take a big picture perspective and 
are willing to perform just about any act if it’s necessary to 
achieve their goals.  While the Foundation prefers that signif-
icant ethical quandaries to be considered and discussed by 
executives and other bureaucrats, especially their own Eth-
ics Committee, they understand that field agents may have to 
make quick, difficult decisions in the line of duty, and they are 
likely to be lenient in policing the actions agents take, so long 
as those actions can reasonably be defended as necessary to 
pursue the Foundation’s goals.
That said, there are limits to what agents are able to get away 
with.  Every action an agent takes in the field will be reported 
to and examined by their superiors and, if deemed necessary, 
the Ethics Committee.  These institutions will accept reason-
able moral compromises and reasonable mistakes made in 
the field.  However there are limits to what they will tolerate. 
Agents who take gallingly unreasonable actions, or who re-
peatedly make similar mistakes will provoke the ire of their 
superiors.  Those deemed to have taken unjustifiable actions 
will be punished.  Depending on the severity of their actions 
this punishment could be something minor, such as janitorial 
duties or a black mark on their record, or something severe, 
such as termination or demotion to D-Class status. 
Field Agents & Backup Requests
The Foundation has a lot of resources, so much so that it can 
be tempting to think of its resources as endless.  However, 
they are not.  Foundation field agents are drawn from a very 
limited pool of potential recruits and receive extensive train-
ing.  Each of them is a very valuable resource for the Founda-
tion, and the Foundation is scrupulous about how it distributes 
them.  This means that the Foundation will not always be able 
to provide backup when it is called for.  Such requests should 
be handled on a case-by-case basis.  In some situations it 
might be reasonable for PCs to want more than four or five 
agents to handle a situation and some missions will function 
perfectly well with the PCs serving as part of a large force. 
However as Director, you should be designing most missions 
with your party in mind, meaning they will generally be ap-
propriate for a group their size, rather than a larger force.
The Foundation is unlikely to be sympathetic to extravagant 
requests for help if they think the PCs should be able to handle 
a situation themselves.  The Foundation can marshal armies to 
deploy in extreme situations, but they do not have armies to 
spare for every situation.  In addition, because most of what 
the Foundation does is secretive, they are hesitant to send a 
large force to handle most situations, because the actions of 
such forces are much harder to cover up than those taken by 
small squads.
Foundation Protocol
The SCP Universe
86


Bottom line, if the Foundation has sent the PCs to deal with a 
situation, it’s because they think the PCs are capable of do-
ing so without any additional help.  If they thought a larger 
force were needed they would have already sent one.  Unless 
something changes about the mission the PCs have been sent 
on, and the factors that led them to send the force they did, 
the PC’s commanders are unlikely to be too receptive to re-
quests for further resources. 
K-Class Scenarios
Some of the threats the Foundation faces are so powerful, 
they can end the world or human society as we know it.  Such 
destructive events are termed K-Class scenarios by the Foun-
dation.  There are several kinds of K-Class scenarios.  XK-
Class scenarios cover most end-of-the-world events.  SK-
Class scenarios are situations where another species besides 
humanity becomes dominant on earth.  IK-Class scenarios 
end global civilization, and ZK-Class scenarios mark the end 
of existence itself.
The Foundation deals with many threats capable of causing 
K-Class scenarios, and the multiverse is riddled with timelines 
in which they failed to prevent them, resulting in the end of the 
world, and that iteration of the Foundation.
XK-Class Event Response
K-Class scenarios are taken very seriously by the Foundation. 
As an organization, they are extremely aware of the fact that 
things can go horribly, world-endingly wrong, as well as the 
fact that such an event could emerge from something seem-
ingly innocuous.
If PCs come to suspect that the threat they’re dealing with is ca-
pable of ending the world, there are standard procedures by 
which they are expected to report this to Foundation higher-ups. 
Upon receiving such a report, dedicated committees will 
judge whether they agree with the field agents issuing the 
report about the gravity of the danger and the PC’s direct 
superiors will respond accordingly.  Agents won’t normally 
be punished for making a report by the standard channels, 
even if Foundation higher-ups think they’ve overestimated the 
danger.  However, PCs who routinely cry “XK-Class Scenar-
io” in response to ordinary dangers will try the patience of 
their overseers, especially if they use this as a tactic to get 
additional resources or make their own jobs easier.  PCs who 
do this repeatedly will eventually face repercussions and they 
will damage their credibility with Foundation officials, some-
thing that they could come to deeply regret if, one day, they 
do encounter a genuinely world-threatening danger. 
Amnestics
The maintenance of the Veil would not be possible were it not 
for amnestics; drugs which the Foundation and others employ 
to erase the memories of those who have seen things they 
shouldn’t have.  Amnestics come in numerous forms, with dif-
ferent limitations.  Almost all amnestics are themselves anom-
alous in some way, often produced by specific SCP objects. 
Most commonly amnestics eliminate all memory of the recent 
past, but the Foundation also possesses more complex means 
by which they can eliminate all knowledge of a certain topic 
without disrupting a person’s other memories.
Amnestics and the Public
Amnestics play a critical role in keeping the public ignorant 
of the anomalous world.  Without them, the Veil would col-
lapse as soon as a handful of people encountered an anom-
alous phenomenon and lived to tell the tale.  Fortunately for 
the Foundation, they have many effective ways of distributing 
amnestics.  Keeping in mind that Foundation agents usually 
arrive at the scene of an anomalous incident disguised as 
Emergency Service workers, the Foundation’s default tactic 
for convincing civilians to take amnestics is to misrepresent 
them as drugs necessary to safeguard them against some 
long-term negative effect of whatever they just experienced. 
For instance, Foundation Agents might claim that an undead 
was infected with an exotic disease, and present amnestics as 
emergency vaccinations or precautionary antibiotics.
Most people obey perceived authority figures, especially 
when led to believe their lives are in danger and the Foun-
dation seldom has much trouble getting people to allow am-
nestics to be administered.  Indeed, a member of the public 
who is unusually resistant to pills or injections being offered 
by disguised Foundation agents might make said agents sus-
pect that they know something they shouldn’t, or perhaps are 
even a member of a Group of Interest.  On the rare occasion 
someone does resist the administration of amnestics, trained 
Foundation agents seldom have difficulty subduing them, at 
least if they genuinely are civilians.  In the event they are a 
member of a Group of Interest with training or anomalous 
abilities of their own, a meaningful battle might ensue.
There are several kinds of amnestics, some of which allow the 
Foundation to manipulate individual memories with a high 
degree of precision.  However, the most common types of 
amnestics issued to field agents simply erase a person’s recent 
memories, causing them to forget a period of time ranging 
from the past several minutes to the past several days.  Persons 
treated with such amnestics will quickly become confused as 
to where they are and how they got there, but they will find 
Foundation agents prepared with suitable cover stories which 
reassure them that nothing strange has happened.
Occasionally an anomalous incident is witnessed by enough 
civilians that individually amnesticizing all of them is infeasible. 
The SCP Universe
87


In such situations Foundation agents use various devices to 
disperse amnestic aerosol over a large area.  Such actions will 
be followed up by broadcasts or other mass communication 
serving the same purpose as the follow-up reassurance 
performed on individual civilians.
The Foundation has many personnel whose entire job is to 
generate cover stories to suit the wide variety of anomalous 
incidents the Foundation must cover up. However in the field, 
it is often necessary for agents to improvise and concoct cover 
stories on the fly.  This usually works out fine.  Even if there is 
some small discrepancy between what an agent said at the 
time and what officials say later, the public is unlikely to notice 
or care.  If this somehow does result in a situation that needs 
to be dealt with, further amnesticization combined with other 
standard misinformation techniques are usually sufficient.
Quarantine Procedures
Many anomalies are contagious.  Sometimes this is because 
they are literal diseases like SCP-008 or SCP-610.  Other 
times, it’s because they’re memetic hazards, ideas that man-
ifest anomalous properties in anyone who is aware of them. 
Still other times, the tendency of an object’s anomalous prop-
erty to spread to other similar objects is simply a part of the 
object’s anomaly.  An anomalous curse or condition could 
spread from person to person or object to object, its proper-
ties infecting everything it comes into contact with, everything 
that enters a certain range, or everything that meets some 
other, more esoteric criteria.
Anomalies with the capacity to spread are especially danger-
ous.  Because anomalies, by definition, defy the ordinary rules 
by which reality functions, any new anomaly the Foundation 
encounters has the potential to be contagious.   Therefore, 
when first arriving on the scene of an anomalous incident, the 
Foundation is already planning for the possibility that what-
ever its agents encounter might be capable of spreading.  In-
dividuals, animals, and objects present at the scene are thor-
oughly examined before being allowed to leave the area of 
an anomalous incident, and the location of the incident itself 
is thoroughly examined before the public is allowed to access 
it again.  If the Foundation has some reason to suspect this 
particular anomaly is capable of spreading, the Foundation 
will enact additional quarantine procedures.
Depending on the scale of an anomalous incident, the Foun-
dation may find themselves needing to block a rather large 
area, such as a building or even an entire town.  They do this 
under the guise of authoritative organizations the public will 
recognize, and invent cover stories to explain to the public 
why the area has been evacuated and why they are not al-
lowed to access it.  They will uphold this stance as long as 
necessary, studying the anomaly and those who have come 
into contact with it to determine whether it is evidently conta-
gious.  If they conclude it is safe to do so, they will eventually 
leave the area and allow the public to return.  If they conclude 
the area is likely to remain unsafe indefinitely, they will pur-
chase any land they need to and create a specialized site in 
the area to contain whatever is there.  They are also likely to 
purchase land and buildings nearby in order to make sure no 
member of the public has any reason to get too close to the 
anomaly.  They usually attempt to increase the value gained 
from such purchases by putting the land and facilities they 
have acquired to use.  For example, residential space near 
an anomalous house will be leased to Foundation agents and 
office space will be appropriated for Foundation purposes.
The Foundation usually regards their purchase of the property 
as sufficient compensation for those effected by its seizure, but 
in some situations, they may take further actions to ensure that 
those involved are not unduly harmed by their actions.   Such 
decisions are generally the purview of the Ethics Committee.
The Foundation is capable of securing and evacuating large 
areas including entire towns with thousands of inhabitants. 
The precise capabilities of your Foundation will depend on the 
Foundation Presence Level of your campaign, but by baseline 
assumptions, the Foundation is capable of relocating popula-
tions of thousands with minimal difficulty or risk of public sus-
picion.  Only in proper cities with populations in the hundreds 
of thousands do the logistics of evacuation and quarantine 
become difficult, and even then the Foundation is ultimately 
likely to be up to the challenge. 
SCP Securement
Before it can contain an anomaly, the Foundation must first 
secure it.  This process begins when an anomaly is identi-
fied.  Most anomalies are first brought to the Foundation’s 
attention through its extensive network of undercover agents 
embedded in police, fire, medical, and other emergency ser-
vices throughout the world.  Some of these embedded agents 
are Foundation spies who have infiltrated these institutions, 
while others were approached and recruited when they were 
already working in them.  Many of these embedded agents 
know relatively little about the Foundation or the anomalous 
world, having simply been instructed to go about their du-
ties while keeping an eye out for anything strange to alert the 
Foundation to.  Once alerted to a potentially anomalous inci-
dent, the Foundation rushes to the scene, disguised as which-
ever emergency service workers suit the situation.  In the event 
there is no apparent emergency to respond to, the Foundation 
will manufacture one, swarming the scene in uniform on the 
pretense that there has been a gas leak, a bomb threat, or 
something similar.  After arriving to the scene the Foundation 
will first do whatever they must to stabilize the situation.  Often 
this means genuinely doing the work of whatever emergency 
service workers they are disguised as - incapacitating violent 
The SCP Universe
88


threats, treating injured civilians, extinguishing fires, and the 
like.  Once the situation is stable they amnesticize civilians as 
needed and remove them from the area.  After clear, they 
begin to investigate the potential anomaly.  Their first order of 
business is to confirm that an actual anomaly is present.  This 
is not always the case.  Not everything strange reported by 
an embedded agent is anomalous in cause.  Foundation field 
agents will perform preliminary experiments to establish the 
presence, identity, and nature of the anomaly.  Note that it 
should not always be clear which object at the scene of an 
anomalous incident is the actual SCP.  Figuring out what is 
going on when dealing with an incident that is not yet under-
stood is one of the major challenges of being a field agent, a 
fact Directors you should keep in mind when designing adven-
tures.  Additionally, a situation does not have to be genuinely 
anomalous to be challenging or engaging.  SCP.2e is primarily 
about dealing with the anomalous, but Foundation agents will 
often respond to situations that turn out to be mundane, and 
there is still plenty of room for players to be challenged while 
dealing with unusual natural disasters or violent criminals.
Securement is often the most dangerous aspect of the Foun-
dation’s work.  Anomalies are by their nature unpredictable, 
and they are at their most unpredictable during their initial 
discovery when the Foundation yet lacks knowledge of their 
properties.  Foundation field agents often endure casualties 
at the hands of anomalies that will later be relatively easy 
to handle, simply because the initial responders do not know 
how the anomaly functions or how to interact with it safely.  As 
the Director, you can make your games tense and harrowing 
by making sure the anomalies you create are complex and 
full of nasty surprises.  At the same time make sure you don’t 
overdo it.  Try to keep anomalies relatively balanced and sur-
vivable and don’t overload your PCs with lethal gimmicks that 
unforeseeably kill or severely harm agents for actions they 
couldn’t have known they should avoid.  Cheap “gochya” 
moments are not engaging for players.  You should endeav-
or to strike a balance between making anomalies realistically 
unpredictable while still keeping them from being overly frus-
trating or unfair.  For example, a random household object 
that immolates anyone who gets close but carries no outward 
sign of this property, is sure to kill players unpredictably.  This 
might bring a single moment of shock and terror, but players 
will find it frustrating and disruptive.  The same anomaly can 
be made fair if warnings about its properties, such as scorch 
marks or charred corpses, are placed in the area around it. 
This allows smart players to deduce that something in the area 
might deal fire damage and prepare or respond accordingly, 
transforming a frustrating gimmick into a satisfying puzzle. 
Generally speaking, warnings like this will make anomalous 
scenes more realistic.  The Foundation is called to a scene 
when something anomalous appears to have happened 
there, so there logically should be traces of what the anomaly 
does in the area.  That said, even if it does ‘make sense’ for 
players to encounter an anomaly that instantly kills them in 
unforeseeable and unavoidable ways, you still shouldn’t do 
that without extensive warnings as it does not make for a sat-
isfying roleplaying experience.
It is worth noting that the process of securement is of utmost 
importance for you as the Director as it is likely to be the big-
gest aspect the Foundation will deal with in your game.  While 
some games may have your players working at a containment 
facility, the majority will cast your players as field agents jour-
neying out into the world to deal with currently uncontained, 
uninformed, anomalous situations. 
Once agents have identified an anomaly and its basic prop-
erties, they can attempt to move it to containment.  
SCP Containment
Once the Foundation has secured an anomaly and brought it 
to a containment site, the process of containment begins.  Pro-
visional investigations as part of an anomaly’s containment 
will have given the Foundation a basic understanding of what 
the anomaly is and why it is anomalous, which will give them 
a starting point in designing its containment procedures.
Containment procedures are designed by Containment Spe-
cialists.  This is one of the most important roles in the Foun-
dation.  It is the responsibility of a Containment Specialist to 
translate information about the properties of an anomaly into 
actionable containment procedures.  This requires an exten-
sive and broad knowledge of science and engineering, full 
of well-memorized protocols and guidelines on how best to 
handle a wide range of phenomena.  The Foundation has 
stock designs for numerous kinds of basic containment cham-
bers, such as simple lockers or humanoid containment cells, 
but these basic designs usually have to be modified to suit the 
anomaly being contained.  Several Containment Specialists 
are specialized to some degree and tend to work with anom-
alies within their area of specialization.  However often times 
anomalies must be transported to the nearest Foundation 
Site, forcing Containment Specialists to work on an anomaly 
far afield from their specialty, and some anomalies manifest 
new properties without warning.  Containment Specialists are 
capable of handling these situations because of their broad 
knowledge base.  The job of a Containment Specialist re-
quires a good deal of creativity, so having the PCs play the 
role of Containment Specialists at a busy Foundation Site 
could make for a fun and interesting campaign.  Every anom-
aly is unique, and a Specialist who attempts to lazily fall back 
on pre-packaged procedures is sure to be defeated by the 
unique properties of the anomaly they’re dealing with.  Con-
tainment specialists are constantly formulating novel solutions 
to complex problems.  Some SCP articles derive a significant 
portion of their entertainment value from the subtle cleverness 
of an item’s containment procedures.  
The SCP Universe
89


Such procedures, whether they be the complex way in which 
SCP-4885’s location is hidden, the pictographic nature of 
SCP-2521’s documentation, or the ingenious ways the count-
less aspects of SCP-001-K are played against each other, are 
all invented by Containment Specialists.  Their expertise and 
cleverness allow the Foundation to exist. 
SCP Protection
Once the containment procedures for an anomaly have been 
designed, they will be enacted.  A far more naïve organiza-
tion might consider their work finished at this point, but the 
Foundation knows that it’s never safe to assume you under-
stand an anomaly.  Once an anomaly is in containment they 
will proceed to poke and prod at it in order to obtain more 
information and refine its containment procedures accord-
ingly.  Some might view the extensive testing anomalies are 
subject to is overdoing it.  However, the Foundation performs 
these extensive tests, often spending a good deal of D-Class 
lives in the process, because they know that it’s folly to as-
sume you understand an anomaly.  Anomalies by definition 
do not follow the standard rules by which reality functions, 
and therefore are liable to manifest unexpected properties at 
any time.  As a result only extensive testing can ensure that the 
Foundation understands an anomaly well enough to safely 
contain it.  After an anomaly’s containment appears to be sta-
ble, they will de-prioritize research and shift resources to oth-
er SCPs.  However the process of studying an anomaly and 
refining its containment procedures never truly ends.  Every 
anomaly the Foundation contains is constantly monitored for 
novel behavior that might require its containment procedures 
to be revisited.
Extensive Foundation protocols give researchers a starting 
point in determining how to handle experimentation on var-
ious kinds of anomalies, but the Foundation’s working re-
searchers, much like Containment Specialists, must possess 
broad knowledge, adaptability, and creativity in order to 
do their jobs.  By its nature experimentation involves placing 
anomalies in novel situations where it is not known precisely 
how they will behave or react.  Researchers must constant-
ly balance the need to obtain novel information about the 
anomalies they study with the need to avoid unacceptable 
risks.  The Foundation always goes into an experiment exten-
sively prepared for every possible outcome.  Their security 
measures often seem like overkill to those new to the Foun-
dation and its workings, but veteran Foundation researchers 
know that a single unexpected development can kill several 
agents or even destroy an entire facility.
The Foundation usually treats the containment of anomalies as 
an end in and of itself.  However the act still carries the underly-
ing purpose of protecting the public from anomalous dangers. 
The dual goals of protecting the public and site staff serve to 
guide the Foundation throughout the entire containment pro-
cess.  While the Foundation will allow or even perpetrate harm 
to the public if they view it as necessary to keep an anomaly 
contained, they prefer to protect the public from both knowl-
edge of the anomalous world and dangerous anomalies. 
The Foundation’s “protection” doesn’t just extend to the pub-
lic.  They view anomalies themselves as under their protec-
tion and regard the destruction of an anomaly as a form of 
containment failure.  Though the Foundation’s treatment of its 
anomalies often seems cold and contemptuous, the Founda-
tion will seek to protect the life or continued existence of an 
anomaly as zealously as it seeks to contain it.  They will fu-
riously guard anomalies from external forces (such as other 
or groups of interest or even other anomalies) which seek to 
destroy them. 
Security Clearance Levels
The Foundation locks data away behind Security Levels to 
help compartmentalize data.  Employees (and guests) will be 
provided with Security Clearance Level of 00, which restricts 
them from information regarding anomalous objects or enti-
ties.  Security Clearance Level 01 is provided to those working 
in proximity to an anomaly.  Security Clearance Level 02 is 
provided to those who may need direct access to some anom-
alous information, and is typically what PCs would start with. 
This clearance level still requires special permissions to access 
sensitive data, and is provided to most positions that work 
closely with multiple anomalies.  Security Clearance 03 is of-
ten provided to senior security and research personnel and 
provides broader access to sensitive data.  Security Clear-
ance 04 is restricted only to those in senior administration and 
require site-wide or regional access to sensitive data about 
anomalies and top-secret Foundation operations.  Security 
Clearance 05 is the highest clearance level and is typically 
granted only to the O5 Council members and a very few se-
lect staff.  
Personnel Classes
Personnel Classes are designated to assign the integral impor-
tance of an employee.  Class A Personnel are those viewed 
as too essential to Foundation operations, and are restricted 
from any hazardous or dangerous activity.  Class B Personnel 
is for those whom may only interact with anomalous objects 
and entities under rigorous precautions.  Class C Personnel 
are allowed access to anomalies freely, and are typically 
what the PCs should be designated as.  Class D Personnel are 
expendable non-voluntary employees of the Foundation and 
are permitted to access hazardous anomalies without precau-
tion.  Class E Personnel are quarantined individuals who have 
been afflicted with harmful characteristics and should not be 
exposed to others.
For more information on Security Clearance Levels and Personnel 
Classes, visit:
scp-wiki.wikidot.com/security-clearance-levels
The SCP Universe
90


This section will provide a brief description of var-
ious noteworthy elements about the SCP universe. 
Each section will only scratch the surface of that cov-
ered on the SCP wiki.
In the setting of the SCP Universe, everything you can imag-
ine has the potential to be real.  This is the most significant, 
defining element of this setting.  Magic is real.  Unfathomable 
sci-fi technology is real.  Evil cults are trying to end the world. 
Predatory monsters stalk the night.  Reality itself comes in de-
grees and can be bent.  The galaxy is teeming with strange 
alien life.  Every myth is true.  Every fictional conceit is correct. 
Everything that could possibly be placed into an urban fan-
tasy setting is, in fact, present, and every part of it is capa-
ble of interacting with every other part.  Even concepts that 
contradict each other can co-exist because the Foundation 
exists in a vast multiverse where countless things are true in 
one universe but not another, thus allowing you to include any 
element in your game that serves the story at hand.
For the Director this is the best case scenario.  There are no 
limitations on what stories you can tell.  If you can conceive 
of something, it exists in the SCP multiverse, and you can lead 
your players through a story about it.  This setting is a place of 
infinite wonder and horror, where nothing is truly set in stone. 
What follows then, is not a series of restrictions on what your 
games can include while still complying with SCP “canon,” 
but merely some baseline elements that are generally regard-
ed as true by default in SCP works.  
Humes
In the universe of the Foundation, reality itself comes in de-
grees.  It is stronger in some locations and weaker in others, 
and every individual has a Hume count that determines both 
reality’s ability to influence them and their ability to influence 
reality.  There is a certain baseline number of Humes which 
causes a location or object to behave normally.  Locations 
and objects with high or low Humes can behave anomalously. 
In locations with low Humes reality is weaker and easily influ-
enced.  In locations with high Humes, entities that can other-
wise bend reality are weakened, and the location itself may 
have an anomalous ability to assert its will over those inside 
of it.  Objects with low Humes are easy to manipulate or have 
been manipulated, while objects with high Humes can assert 
their metaphorical will over a location.
Every person has an inherent Hume count. (For NPCs/PCs 
you can simply roll 1D10 to determine the Charac-
ter’s Hume level) Individuals with ordinary Hume counts 
behave the way we are used to people behaving.  They have 
free will and can alter the world around them through familiar 
mechanisms of muscle and mind, but not in ways that violate 
the rules of reality.  Individuals with zero or less Hume counts 
have less free will and are easily guided by life and circum-
stances to be whatever the world has willed for them to be. 
Most of these persons are exceptionally average and unin-
teresting, though some are guided along by a specific destiny 
the universe has assigned them, and which they lack the pow-
er to escape.
Still more interesting are those with Hume counts over 60. 
These individuals are considered Type Green reality benders 
and have the power to assert their will on the world around 
them in supernatural ways.  Reality bender status is partially 
relative.  In extremely low-hume spaces, nearly anyone can 
bend reality.  However, reality benders are capable of using 
their high Hume count to manipulate spaces with average or 
higher Hume counts.
Machines called Kant Counters are used to measure the Hume 
level of a location or object (see page 119).
Type Green/Type Blue
Because one’s Hume count is determined at birth, many real-
ity benders are born with their powers.  These reality benders 
are sometimes referred to as “Type Green.”  These people 
have the ability to alter the world around them simply by 
willing changes to occur.  Reality benders vary in power and 
those with higher Hume counts can cause bigger changes 
more easily, while those with smaller (but still above aver-
age) Hume counts can alter reality in only limited ways.  Some 
reality benders are more capable of exerting their will over 
some aspects of the world than others, causing their powers to 
take on apparent “themes.”  For instance, the powerful reality 
bender Jude Kyriot is particularly adept at manipulating elec-
tricity, though he has also demonstrated the ability to bend 
reality in unrelated ways.
While those born with inherent abilities have the easiest time 
manipulating reality, those born with no special talent can learn 
to become “Type Blue” reality benders by learning to practice 
Thaumaturgy (a.k.a. magic).  This allows individuals with no 
inherent abilities to cast a wide variety of spells.  The path of 
a Type Blue reality bender requires more study and work than 
that of a Type Green, and the spells it allows one to cast can 
be quite limited, but a sufficiently knowledgeable and experi-
enced Thaumaturgist can match just about any feat of reality 
bending another can perform, regardless of their Hume count. 
SCP Material tends to avoid depicting heroes and power 
fantasies, so there are not many SCP stories about reality 
benders using their abilities to accomplish traditional feats of 
mystical heroism, especially if it would cause those Charac-
ters to resemble superheroes.  This is more an aesthetic limita-
tion than an in-universe one.  There is nothing preventing you 
from running a campaign about a group of reality bending 
heroes if you wish, though the feel of such a campaign likely 
Setting
The SCP Universe
91


won’t resemble the traditional SCP tone, which leans towards 
strangeness and horror over power fantasy.
The Foundation regards all reality bending as anomalous and 
believes that those who possess this ability must be contained. 
Thus, anyone with such abilities must be extremely careful to 
prevent themselves from being exposed.  If the Foundation 
catches them they will almost certainly imprison them for life. 
The Foundation is hesitant to employ the help of humanoid 
anomalies even when it could be to their benefit to do so.  That 
said, some iterations of the Foundation have been known to 
recruit or conscript humanoid anomalies if they believe they 
can gain their loyalty and that the benefits of their service jus-
tify the risks.
Scranton Reality Anchors
Among the most powerful tools at the Foundation’s disposal 
are Scranton Reality Anchors (see page 120).  These ma-
chines, invented by Foundation researcher Robert Scranton, 
inflate the Hume level in a location.  In the presence of a 
Scranton Reality Anchor, almost all anomalous objects will 
lose their anomalous properties, and humanoid anomalies 
can lose their special abilities.  Sufficiently powerful anom-
alies may only be weakened in the presence of a reality an-
chor, and a few are so powerful that they overwhelm the an-
chor’s capacity to control them.
Cognitohazards
Many anomalies are capable of manipulating the minds 
of those who perceive them.  The Foundation refers to such 
anomalies as cognitohazards.  Most cognitohazards are sen-
sations, be they sights, sounds, smells, etc., which harm the 
person experiencing them.  
Cognitohazards have an extremely wide variety of effects, 
ranging from trivial to fatal or worse.  They often influence a 
person’s behavior, causing them to do things they would not 
normally want to do.  Some cognitohazards naturally tend 
to spread from person to person, often because their effects 
cause victims to deliberately expose others to the same haz-
ardous stimuli that is affecting them.  Such naturally virulent 
cognitohazards are called memes.  Memetic hazards are of-
ten among the most insidious threats the Foundation faces.
The Infinite Multiverse
The SCP Foundation does not exist in a single universe, but 
rather an infinite multiverse containing infinite, infinitely vary-
ing iterations of everything in it.  Every possible variation of 
the SCP setting that you can imagine is a universe located 
somewhere within the multiverse, as is every possible world 
which is at odds with typical descriptions of the SCP universe. 
This makes it trivial to cross the SCP universe with any other, as 
they all exist somewhere within this infinitely varying cosmos.
Generally speaking, transportation between universes is ac-
complished via “Ways” which are portals to other realities. 
Any given Way is not open all the time, but all will open when 
a “Knock” is performed.  Knocks can be a simple gesture, a 
key phrase, an elaborate ritual, or anything else a person 
could do.  When performed properly, the Knock opens the 
Way, and allows one to access the universe on the other side.
Several alternate universes have been elaborated upon in 
the Foundation’s various “canons.”  These include timelines 
where the Veil has fallen and the public is aware of the super-
natural (the “Broken Masquerade” canon), timelines where 
the Foundation never existed (the “Unfounded” canon), even 
timelines where humanity has abandoned earth and begun to 
explore the stars (“Ad Astra Per Aspera”).
Pataphysics
One of the best-kept secrets of the Foundation, known only 
to its highest-ranking members, is the fact that the Founda-
tion is well-aware that it exists within a fictional universe con-
trolled by a group of horror writers.  Within the Foundation’s 
cosmos, every fictional universe, and every fictional universe 
within each fictional universe, is real.  According to the “Nar-
rative Stack” theory, every universe exists as a fictional world 
nested inside another fictional universe, which is nested in-
side another, which is nested inside another, ad infinitum.  The 
Foundation has attempted on numerous occasions to alter the 
universe in their favor by influencing its writers behavior.  It 
may very well be that the Foundation that exists at your table 
is entirely aware of their existence inside a tabletop role-play-
ing game.  What, if anything, they are prepared to do with 
this knowledge is up to you to decide.
Pluripotent Entities
The term “pluripotent” describes the most powerful entities 
the Foundation is aware of, those many would regard as 
gods. There are many pluripotent entities in the SCP multi-
verse, varying widely in both personality and relative power. 
Unfortunately one of the most powerful pluripotent entities 
in existence is the Scarlet King (depicted on the cover of this 
book), an embodiment of cruelty and barbarism who seeks 
to drag every living thing into a new dark age.  His follow-
ers, and his many children, can bring widespread destruction, 
sometimes threatening the entire multiverse.
There are many other malevolent pluripotent entities, such as 
the violent and mind-controlling Hanged King and Starfish 
God.  Others have more complex motivations, such as the 
machine god Mekhane and the flesh god Yaldabaoth, as well 
as The Serpent, an embodiment of knowledge who seeks to 
disseminate forbidden truths.  Some pluripotent enemies are 
even benevolent, such as Rakmou-leusan, an entity who has 
dedicated himself to protecting the universe.
The SCP Universe
92


The Ancient World
Even in the modern day, the oasis of normalcy carved out 
by the Foundation is deceptively small.  However, in the dis-
tant past, it was even smaller, and if one travels far enough 
back in time, they will find a world in which the Veil did not 
exist at all, with parts of the world, especially northern Eu-
rope and Asia, having been dominated by highly anomalous 
civilizations.  Chief among these were the brutal Daevites, a 
civilization built on slavery and human sacrifice.  It was led by 
superhuman “Daevas” who magically modified themselves to 
be impossibly long-lived and powerful.  Any account of the 
Daevites’ history and origins is complicated by the fact that 
they have not always existed in this reality.  Rather, they seem 
to be retroactive additions created by SCP-140, an anoma-
lous history book which, when exposed to liquid suitable for 
writing adds to itself, and as it does so alters the real past to 
match its descriptions.  Through it, the Daevites have been in-
serted into a history which either did not include them before 
or included them in a very different form.
The Daevites are the core of the ancient anomalous world, but 
they had several rivals and contemporaries who were sim-
ilarly anomalous, such as the benevolent Earthen Ortothan 
Kingdom with whom they often warred, and the flesh-melding 
Sarkites who sprung from a slave rebellion within the empire. 
Given how consistently anomalous the Daevites’ neighbors 
and rivals were, it is possible that SCP-140 created all of these 
groups, and in creating them, created a staggeringly large 
chunk of the anomalous world.
The First Hytoth
Those various parallel timelines aren’t the only examples of 
the world ending.  Before the present iteration of reality, there 
was an entire, second multiverse, as vast and varied as our 
own, which was destroyed by an unknown cataclysm, only 
for it to be replaced by the cosmos we now inhabit.
The Second Hytoth
According to the Ortothan religion, our multiverse is the sec-
ond iteration of the cosmos - the second Hytoth.  Before it, 
there was another multiverse, just as vast, rich, and vibrant. 
Native to this first universe was a band of seven warriors - 
the Koru-Teusa, or Holy Seven - who, as their cosmos was 
dying, sought out and acquired divinity so they could protect 
the newly formed Second Hytoth from meeting the same fate 
as the first.
Their divinity, however, comes at a price.  Their divine forms 
constantly bleed, and the blood they lose must be replaced 
by blood offerings from mortal worshipers.  Should they re-
ceive too few blood offerings, they will surely die.  Many 
gods would have responded to such a need by becoming 
bloodthirsty tyrants, gulping down the blood of sacrificial vic-
tims on the excuse that their deaths help protect the universe. 
The Koru-Teusa have proven themselves above this.  For as 
long as they have had mortal followers, they have ordained 
that all blood given to them must be offered willingly.
This blood has been put to good use.  In their early explora-
tions of the Second Hytoth, the Koru-Teusa quickly discovered 
a threat worthy of their divine attention.  Our universe con-
tains a rift to a place beyond all ordered creation, called the 
Voru, which crawls with chaos-born beings called Voruteut. 
The Koru-Teusa regard the Voruteut as the greatest threat to 
the Second Hytoth, and have dedicated most of their atten-
tion to fighting them.  This battle has been costly, claiming the 
lives of six of the Holy Seven, leaving Rakmou-leusan, a pa-
tron of battlefield strategy who wields four holy spears, as the 
only remaining Ortothan deity.
The Ortothan religion is older than humanity, but Ortothan 
missionaries have brought the religion to earth multiple times. 
The Church of the Second Hytoth is the creation of an Or-
tothan missionary called Second Messenger, who brought 
the faith back to Earth around 1900 after it had been extinct 
here for millennia.  The Church, like all Ortothans, follow Rak-
mou-leusan’s teachings of kindness, curiosity, strength, and 
strategic thinking, seek to provide blood offerings, spread 
their faith, study the universe and its predecessor, and destroy 
any Voruteut who dare show themselves on earth.
The Church is a generally benevolent organization.  They are 
dedicated to protecting the universe and those within it, and 
their love of strategic foresight and open-mindedness limits the 
harm done by their immense zeal.  However, the extraterrestrial 
origins of their religion, and the Church’s extensive knowledge 
of Thaumaturgy, make them anomalous, and the Foundation 
prevents them from openly practicing their faith.   This makes 
the relationship between the two organizations tense, to say 
the least, despite the Church’s official policy of respecting the 
Veil.  The two organizations often compete for alien artifacts, 
as well as any traces they can find of the ancient Ortothan king-
doms that existed on earth thousands of years ago.  Though the 
Church is a much smaller organization than the Foundation, 
its access to alien technology and powerful magic allow their 
agents to pose a threat to Foundation operatives.
Directors should be aware that the Ancient World, as impact-
ful as it may be, will likely not affect modern SCP settings, and 
even the majority of Foundation staff are unaware of this history. 
The SCP Universe
93


Groups of Interest
The Foundation is only one of many factions inhabiting the SCP 
multiverse and while SCP.2e is great for campaigns where the 
players are Foundation agents, a wide variety of campaigns 
could exist where your players are aligned with other groups 
of interest. The following are some guidelines for how you 
might run such campaigns. Only a small selection of the SCP 
setting’s factions are covered below, as many Groups of Inter-
est (GoI) are either too mysterious, too otherworldly, too im-
personal, or too malevolent to be suitable for most campaigns.
The Chaos Insurgency
The Chaos Insurgency are among the most prominent antag-
onists in the SCP setting.  They are often relatively generic; 
bad guys with guns who pose a physical threat to Foundation 
agents.  Despite this, running a Chaos Insurgency campaign 
might prove surprisingly similar to running a Foundation one. 
While the insurgency is far more selfish and malevolent, they 
are still derived from the Foundation and much of its structure 
and operations reflect that.  As a result a Chaos Insurgency 
campaign will likely be similar to a Foundation one, albeit 
with the PCs being even more ruthless than the Foundation. 
Even if you emphasize the Insurgency’s utopian ideals, your 
players will do whatever they find necessary to acquire anom-
alous objects for Delta Command.
While the Chaos Insurgency’s stated goal is to use anomalies 
to build a utopian society, their actual motives are largely un-
defined and unknown.  As a Director you may wish to center 
a campaign around your own idea of what their goals are or 
you could maintain this ambiguity and run a campaign where 
the players largely don’t know why Delta Command has them 
doing what they’re doing.  This runs the risk of being boring if 
the PCs’ actions come to seem pointless, but the PCs’ struggle 
to trust the Insurgency’s thoroughly untrustworthy leadership 
could also be a rich source of conflict.
The Church of the Broken God
The Church of the Broken God is as complicated as it is 
fanatical, with a wide range of goals from promoting 
technological development, opposing other religions, to res-
urrecting Mekhane to bring forth a new age.  Any of these 
goals could be the focus of a campaign.  A campaign focused 
on opposing Sarkite forces would likely be similar to a Foun-
dation campaign with the same premise, just with more anom-
alous and devout PCs.  Alternately, the PCs could be fighting 
to retrieve the various pieces of the broken god, or otherwise 
working to restore their god to life via some complex ritual 
or powerful magic.  Such a campaign could turn out to be 
surprisingly conventional, as quests to acquire a set of arti-
facts are a staple of tabletop campaigns across all settings 
and systems, with the intriguing twist that the PCs are the ones 
fighting to remake the world rather than fighting to preserve 
the status quo.  The most extraordinary thing about a Church 
of the Broken God campaign will not be the goals of the PCs 
but the anomalous modifications they are sure to have.  The 
Mekhanite religion mandates that its followers physically alter 
themselves with advanced and anomalous technology, turn-
ing themselves into something more than human.  As a Direc-
tor you will want to flesh out the anomalous abilities this gives 
them as they will be important tools in the PCs’ arsenal which 
will also have immense personal and religious significance to 
their Characters.
The Church of the Second Hytoth
In a setting drenched in moral ambiguity and cosmic horror 
the Church of the Second Hytoth stands as a point of light 
and benevolence full of heroes who, despite the overwhelm-
ing power of the cosmic horrors that surround them, fight to 
protect others and stand by their moral convictions, remaining 
stalwart in the face of threats which drive other factions to 
despair and malevolent “pragmatism.”
An Ortothan campaign would reflect this, allowing your play-
ers to stand not as agents of a pragmatic bureaucracy, but 
as true heroes fighting to beat back the darkness in the face 
of impossible odds.  Such a game will explore how heroic 
Characters remain strong in the face of evils that leave others 
broken.  In a way this is a power fantasy, but one centered 
on mental fortitude, integrity, and willpower instead of physi-
cal prowess.  Ortothans may be defeated but they never give 
up, and are never broken.  To make the most out of this, you 
should ensure that your Ortothan players face real darkness 
and genuine moral dilemmas.  Ortothan determination is only 
impressive when it persists in the face of evils that can corrupt 
and destroy other factions.
While religious groups like The Church of the Broken God and 
Sarkic Cults have beliefs that lead their followers to cultivate 
certain anomalous abilities, the Church of the Second Hytoth 
have access to a wide variety of strange powers and tools. 
Ortothan PCs could be aliens, sentient machines, powerful 
mages, ordinary humans, or anything in between.  A party of 
Ortothan PCs is likely to be a diverse collection of anomalous 
and non-anomalous people.
The Serpent’s Hand
Like the Church of the Second Hytoth, the Serpent’s Hand is 
one of the SCP setting’s few definitively benevolent factions. 
While the Church of the Second Hytoth accomplishes this 
with endless moral fortitude in the face of cosmic horror, the 
Serpent’s Hand instead looks past those horrors to see the 
multiverse’s infinite wonder.  This doesn’t mean that they are 
blind to the world’s evils.  Indeed, their appreciation of the 
universe’s wonders motivates them to protect them but it also 
makes them open-minded and uniquely capable of recogniz-
ing when an anomaly is not a threat.
The SCP Universe
94


While the Serpent’s Hand stereotypically focuses on liberat-
ing anomalies, they are generally less wedded to their pre-
ferred approach than the Foundation and the GOC.  Because 
they are uniquely capable of reacting in a variety of ways to 
different situations, a Serpent’s Hand campaign could have a 
variety of goals depending on what anomalies they find them-
selves dealing with.  It could be focused on protecting people 
from anomalous threats or it could be about protecting hu-
manoid or harmless anomalies from the Veil and spreading 
knowledge that others wish to suppress.  A Serpent’s Hand 
campaign is likely to delve into themes of guerrilla warfare, 
given the Hand’s relative weakness compared to the Foun-
dation and its allies.  To evade capture and thwart the plans 
of more powerful organizations, they must rely on constant 
stealth and trickery.
A Serpent’s Hand campaign is a great way to explore a party 
of anomalous Characters as many agents of the Hand pos-
sess anomalous abilities or practice Thaumaturgy.  Unlike with 
many of the religious GoIs covered above, members of the 
Serpent’s Hand may have very different anomalous capabil-
ities from each other and be anomalous to different degrees. 
You may even have a mixed party containing both regular 
humans and anomalous beings.
The Wanderer’s Library
Members of the Serpent’s Hand also have access to the Wan-
derer’s Library, an extradimensional location containing 
nearly every book ever written in any universe, accessible by 
a vast network of two-way portals that transport wanderers 
between the library and a wide variety of locations in various 
dimensions.  Both of these properties may prove important 
tools in a party’s arsenal.  The Library’s books may prove a 
valuable source of information, though the Library’s vastness 
may make specific pieces of information hard to find.  The Li-
brary’s network of Ways are also an incredibly valuable re-
source, not only allowing the Hand to access many different 
dimensions but also potentially allowing access to a clean 
escape from a wide variety of locations, some of which may 
be tactically significant.  Because the Library bars access to 
many of the Hand’s enemies and prevents patrons from being 
violent to one another, escape into the Library likely is a great 
way for a wanderer fleeing a Jailor or a Bookburner to make 
a clean getaway.  Because the Library contains portals to nu-
merous universes, it acts as a hub for dimensional travelers.
The Library lends its texts freely to those who obtain a library 
card.  Such cards are not difficult to obtain, however obtain-
ing one places an individual under the Library’s power, al-
lowing it to inflict grave punishments on those who break the 
Library’s rules.  Most often, such people are conscripted into 
service as librarians and security guards for eternity.
The Library is fully sentient and extremely powerful.  It is capa-
ble of barring entry to anyone it believes will break its rules, 
which includes most agents of the Foundation.  The Library 
was created by The Serpent, an embodiment of knowledge 
who resides deep within it.  
See also wanderers-library.wikidot.com
Gamers Against Weed
Gamers Against Weed (GAW) has a lot of overlap with the 
Serpent’s Hand, with most members of GAW also being 
members of the Hand.  If your campaign is primarily focused 
on PCs as GAW, their Characters will be pranksters and most 
of their adventures will center around them “trolling” the SCP 
setting’s less sympathetic factions.  This is sure to put them in 
the position of underdogs, up against groups with more pow-
er and resources but it also means they don’t have to fully 
defeat or even seriously harm those groups, just get one over 
on them in a humorous way.  In such a campaign you should 
probably let the players take the lead in devising the pranks 
they’ll play and then build adventures around the conse-
quences and chaos that erupts as a result.
A GAW campaign is sure to start with a relatively lightheart-
ed tone but there is no guarantee that it will stay that way. 
While members of GAW usually try to avoid violent conflict, 
they may not stick to their pacifism in extreme circumstances, 
and during their career as pranksters members of GAW may 
encounter something too serious to joke about, perhaps even 
something they’re willing to truly fight for.
Global Occult Coalition
The Global Occult Coalition (GOC) is a lot like the Founda-
tion.  It’s a large, powerful organization that fights to preserve 
normalcy and protect the public from anomalies.  The primary 
difference is that the GOC is far more aggressive and inclined 
to destroy anomalies rather than contain them.  How far they 
take this tendency differs between different iterations of the 
organization, but they often destroy harmless anomalies even 
if there is no tactical advantage to do so.
Because the GOC doesn’t mind destroying anomalies, a 
GOC-centric campaign may focus more on action and com-
bat than a Foundation one.  If you’re looking to run a “dun-
geon crawl” or similarly combat-focused campaign, this is the 
faction for you.  If you do intend to run such a game be care-
ful, as combat in the SCP.2e is generally difficult and deadly, 
especially when players find themselves up against anoma-
lous creatures.  Directors should try to balance player power 
levels equal to the threat of the anomaly (see Threat Levels on 
page 135).
The SCP Universe
95


The Horizon Initiative
Despite its ethos being built around faith, which some might 
view as the opposite of the Foundation’s cold, science-based 
pragmatism, the Horizon Initiative is ultimately quite similar 
to the Foundation.  Its agents seek to collect relics which they 
usually store away.  What separates the two is the motive 
and ideology behind these actions.  The Foundation wants to 
maintain normalcy and protect the general public while the 
Horizon Initiative wants to advance their religious beliefs. 
Horizon Initiative PCs will have somewhat different interests 
and goals than Foundation ones, even as they engage in sim-
ilar behaviors.  In practice, the primary differences will be 
which anomalies they are interested in and what they want to 
do with them.  So long as you keep these differences in mind 
designing Horizon Initiative adventures will not be all that dif-
ferent from designing Foundation ones.
The Sarkic Cults
Sarkites are a deceptively complicated and diverse group. 
Despite generally being regarded as a single group of inter-
est, Sarkicism is ultimately a belief system rather than a fac-
tion, and different followers of the religion may not always 
be aligned with one another.  Though often cast as villainous, 
Sarkites are not inherently evil and could have a wide vari-
ety of goals.  Their primary shared features are an ability to 
bend flesh to their will and the hatred of numerous other or-
ganizations who regard them as anomalies to be imprisoned 
or abominations to be destroyed.  A Sarkic campaign could 
center around anything from the PC’s rise to power as an 
anomalously enhanced mafia, a pilgrimage to an important 
artifact, to a desperate struggle to evade Foundation or Me-
khanite persecution.  Much like with their Mekhanite nemeses, 
the most important thing about them for you as a Director to 
account for is their anomalous nature.  Before running a full 
Sarkite campaign, work with your players and help determine 
what anomalous aspects could be categorized as flesh mod-
ification and any adjustments to them that may be required.
Unusual Incidents Unit
The UIU is another group that is similar to the Foundation. Both 
organizations seek to preserve normalcy and protect the pub-
lic from anomalous threats. There are two main differences 
between them. First, the UIU work directly for the US govern-
ment. Second, despite their alignment with the most powerful 
government in the world, the UIU are far, far less equipped to 
handle most of the threats they deal with. They have fewer re-
sources, fewer agents, and less funding than the Foundation. 
They are constantly competing against more powerful organi-
zations to be the first on the scene of an anomalous incident. 
This power dynamic will likely be the defining feature of a UIU 
campaign as they aspire to be an organization on par with 
the Foundation but lack the resources to easily match them.
As Characters, Foundation personnel and UIU agents are 
likely to be very similar.  After all, the FBI is one of the many 
organizations the Foundation recruits from and some UIU 
agents may even encounter former colleagues who have 
jumped ship to work for the Foundation.
The Wandsmen
The Wandsmen are the strangest group on this list.  All Wands-
men are, at least superficially, nonhuman, and a campaign fo-
