cused on them likely wouldn’t take place on Earth as we know 
it.  Civilians seeing a Wandsman is a violation of the Veil, 
and their presence here would attract an enormous amount 
of Foundation attention. If they do spend time on our earth 
they will need to move about carefully and stay in the shadows 
or use disguises to pass as ordinary humans. The anomalous 
powers of a Wandsman will also need to be represented me-
chanically, as all Wandsmen have the ability to fly & teleport, 
and many of them are liable to have other anomalous abilities, 
including a fair degree of thaumaturgical skill.
The Wandsmen are explorers and journalists.  Much like the 
Serpent’s Hand they seek to spread information throughout 
the multiverse.  The main difference is that while members of 
the Serpent’s Hand are mostly from our world and seek to 
spread information in defiance of the Veil, the Wandsmen are 
mostly alien and content for their publications to be read by 
those already aware of the supernatural.  They do not have 
any active agenda in our world other than to learn and write 
about it.  While a party aligned with the Serpent’s Hand ul-
timately seeks to expose the anomalous, a Wandsmen cam-
paign will likely wish to make as little noise as possible as they 
solve mysteries and uncover hidden truths to report on.
An Independent Campaign
There are countless Factions in the SCP universe, but your 
players need not act on behalf of any of them.  Individuals 
within the SCP universe could have countless reasons of their 
own to go on adventures.  Because of the sheer power of or-
ganizations like the SCP Foundation, it is often difficult to get 
along in the SCP universe without some large organization to 
protect you from amnesticization or containment.  However, 
independent persons can find various ways around this and 
even if they do exist under the protective shadow of a group 
like the Serpent’s Hand they may not work for them, and even 
if they do, they may sometimes get up to adventures that are 
not related to the Hand.
For unaligned persons in the SCP setting, maintaining secrecy 
and not drawing attention is a constant challenge.  A per-
son with knowledge of the anomalous, especially one who 
is anomalous in their own right, will earn the attention of just 
about every faction in the SCP setting. Some will want to con-
tain them.  Others destroy them. Others may protect them, 
The SCP Universe
96


and if the PC’s anomalous ability is useful, many may wish to 
recruit them.  Navigating all of this while maintaining one’s in-
dependence will prove a complex and interesting challenge.
An independent campaign doesn’t necessarily have to be one 
in which PCs are not members of a group of interest, just one 
where the adventures they’re going on are at least partial-
ly independent from any such loyalties they may have.  This 
means one or more PCs could still follow an anomalous reli-
gion or even be a member of a GoI and be able to draw on 
its resources for the party’s own ends.
A Civilian Campaign
For those who spend most of their time in the anomalous 
world it can be easy to forget that most inhabitants of the SCP 
setting are oblivious to the supernatural wonders and horrors 
around them.  If you wish, your players could start a cam-
paign in such a position as members of the uninformed masses 
whose worldviews are about to be shaken by an encounter 
with something inexplicable.
A Civilian Campaign will still feature the anomalous, but it will 
be centered around PCs who will begin with no frame of refer-
ence for it and will follow them as they slowly uncover the true 
nature of the world they live in.  They will begin without any 
contacts in the anomalous world and will be nearly helpless in 
the face of the magical dangers and global conspiracies that 
control the SCP Universe.
The most important consideration when running such a cam-
paign is how you are going to introduce your civilian PCs to 
the anomalous world.  Likely this will begin with some sort of 
supernatural encounter, and they will find themselves drawn 
further and further into the anomalous world as they try to 
uncover the truth behind whatever they experienced.  Make 
sure to give your PCs a powerful motive to uncover that truth. 
Mere curiosity won’t be enough for most people to keep look-
ing for answers once they begin to pick up on how powerful 
the conspiracies that maintain the Veil are, and how danger-
ous it is to defy them.  Perhaps the PCs lost a loved one to an 
anomaly or group of interest.  Perhaps they find themselves 
developing strange abilities that they need to understand and 
control.
In any case, this initial encounter will position the PCs against 
the overwhelming power of those who wish to keep them from 
uncovering the truth.  Note that this does not require the PCs 
to face off directly against the Foundation, especially at first. 
Even the more benevolent factions of the SCP universe will 
not take kindly to civilian PCs investigating them and will be 
both highly secretive and deeply suspicious of anyone prying 
in their affairs.  Groups that are accustomed to being hunted 
by the Foundation might not believe the PCs when they claim 
to be mere third parties investigating the anomalous world on 
their own behalf.
A person’s first exposure to the supernatural world is an ex-
citing role-play opportunity as it allows players to explore 
a Character whose worldview is being shattered.  This can 
be an intriguing way to begin a campaign even if you plan 
for the PCs to eventually join up with one of the anomalous 
world’s factions, many of which might view the PCs as poten-
tial recruits if they prove capable during their initial encoun-
ters with the supernatural.
At the beginning of a campaign it is possible for party to con-
tain a mixture of people who are and are not aware of the 
supernatural.  Thanks to the Veil, even those who are some-
what familiar with the supernatural are often forced to keep 
those connections secret from their civilian acquaintances.  As 
a result there may be groups of associates where one or more 
members already know something about the supernatural or 
are even members of a Group of Interest.  If you’re feeling 
conspiratorial, you can add some excitement to a civilian 
campaign by having one or more players begin with their 
Character already embedded in the anomalous world but 
forced to hide this fact from the people around them.  Make 
sure to discuss this in private with the player in question so the 
other players are just as surprised as their Characters once 
they learn what their friend has been hiding from them.
If you utilize this technique, make sure the PC’s secret alle-
giance isn’t too dangerous.  You don’t want one member of 
the party to betray the others, because parties in tabletop 
RPGs generally need to stick together to survive.  To prevent 
this don’t have them aligned with an overtly malevolent group 
of interest.  They’re not meant to be a villain who will harm the 
other PCs, just a person who has some secrets to keep which 
will generate a bit of extra drama.
For more about specific Groups of Interest check out:
scp-wiki.wikidot.com/groups-of-interest
Look for GoI specific SCP.2e expansions found on our 
discord:
discord.gg/9KjeBsn6Sg
or 26letterpublishing on Drivethrrpg.com!
drivethrurpg.com/browse/pub/8469/26-Letter-Publishing
The SCP Universe
97


Campaign Types
There are many ways you can play the SCP Tabletop Role-
Playing-Game 2nd Edition.  This rulebook covers options for 
you to run a strict-realistic game where all your players are 
D-Class personnel trying to escape the clutches of the SCP 
Foundation, to a game where your players can bend reality, 
play as an SCP themselves while going toe-to-toe with the 
Scarlet King -  and everything in between!
There are six primary areas of focus you should consider be-
fore beginning your campaign.
Diversity - How diverse are your players’ strengths?
Length - How long will your game be?
Power Level - How strong are your players?
Anomalous Aspects - How anomalous are your players?
Lethality - How deadly will your game be?
Canon - What is your overall universe like?
Campaign Diversity Level (CDL)
How varied do you want your players’ Characters to be in 
terms of power?  Directors should select a Standard Disrup-
tion Class (SDC) and determine a Campaign Diversity Level 
(CDL) that suits their storyline.  Players can then choose the 
SDC that aligns with the game’s standard, or they can opt for 
a level higher or lower than the CDL by up to ±2. The CDL can 
be set at 0, 1, or 2.
For instance, if you’ve chosen the Vlam Disruption Class as the 
standard, with a CDL of 0, all players 
must select Vlam as their Disruption Class.
If your SDC is Keneq, and you’ve set 
the CDL at 1, this allows for Character 
choices from Vlam, Keneq, or Ekhi.
The most diverse option is Keneq ±2, 
which allows all disruption classes 
open to your players, while a Dark ±0 
game would be perfect for a survival horror experience.  An-
other solid choice would be a Vlam ±2 game, which allows 
any Disruption Class except for Amida.
If you are new to Directing the SCP.2e rpg, you may want to 
try something simple like Dark ±1 which limits players to just 
Dark and Vlam options.
Note:  Fast Character Creation yields a Dark Disruption Class Character. 
Campaign Length
How long will your game take to complete?  Are you just 
playing one session or playing indefinitely?   Estimating how 
many gaming sessions it will take to complete your story is 
important for players to know and what to expect.  
One Shot 
Your game will last just one session.  This is usually a great 
option for people who are not able to meet up very often.  The 
Director would likely want to create the players’ Characters 
for them to save time.  The story is fast, often lethal, and will 
be wrapped up by the end of the night.  One Shot games are 
a great way to introduce people to the mechanics of SCP.2e. 
Directors should try to focus on a fast-paced simple story with 
an epic ending.  One Shot games shouldn’t be focused on the 
players or their backgrounds, but instead focused on a single, 
interesting event where the outcome (good or bad ending) is 
determined by your players’ actions.
Short Campaign
Your game will take a little more than two sessions.  Short 
campaigns have plots akin to that of most movies.  There is 
enough time to form a three-act structure that includes an 
introduction to the Characters and situation, a few sessions 
devoted to the primary conflict of the story, and a final con-
frontation and resolution.  Directors should limit the Campaign 
Diversity to avoid wild imbalances to PC power levels.   
Medium Length Campaign
Your game will likely last over six sessions.  Medium cam-
paigns are a perfect length for most campaigns Directors 
will run.  They are short enough that they maintain play-
er engagement while being long enough to allow a play-
er-driven plot.  The Director should keep pacing in mind, 
but should allow the players to set the pace whenever pos-
sible.  You can safely implement a ±1 to your CDL, and 
allow for any SDC you feel appropriate.  
Long Campaign
Your game will last several months.  Long Campaigns al-
low Directors to build up some very epic stories with sub-
plots, player background driven side-quests, mini-bosses, 
and legendary climactic showdowns.  Directors can safely 
allow a Keneq ±2 diversity (allow any Disruption Class) 
but it should be made clear that Amida Characters while 
starting off incredibly strong, may face challenges as the 
campaign progresses.
Chapter Eight:  
The Director’s Handbook
The Director’s Handbook
98


Endless Campaign
Your game has no ending planned.  Your players may change 
throughout the years but the universe will remain impacted by 
the heroes of the past.  Think of Endless Campaigns as a series 
of comic books with ongoing turmoil and several antagonists. 
Your campaign can be composed of several of the previous-
ly mentioned Campaign Lengths strung along into a series of 
ongoing events.  When a new PC joins into your story several 
sessions in, Directors are encouraged to increase their Power 
Levels (see blow) to help bring them up to the rest of the party. 
Campaign Power Levels
Not only can you adjust what options your players have 
available during Character creation, but you can also alter 
the overall power level of your campaign by including or ig-
noring specific rules throughout this rulebook.  Deciding how 
often to provide Chapter Rewards will impact the Power Level 
of your campaign.  You can also increase how many Power 
Points your players begin with to further increase a PCs Power 
Level, or limit Items, Uniforms, and Weapons by assigning a 
standard loadout to all PCs.
Survival Horror
Your players are unprepared for the deadly horrors around 
every corner.  Chapter Rewards are as scarce as ammunition, 
and the monsters your players are up against are a force be-
yond their ability.  Fast Character Creation is encouraged due 
to the lethality of your campaign and players will likely not 
survive the adventure.  Survival itself is the only reward.
Low Level Grunts
Your players are a team with the potential to become a 
powerful force, but start off as rookies.  They are provided 
standard equipment that is supposed to be enough to han-
dle their given task, but it is soon discovered they are in over 
their heads.  Chapter Rewards are provided once per gaming 
session, or less.  Enemies are powerful but may be overcome 
with great teamwork and effort.
Veteran Task Squads
Your players are well familiar with anomalous encounters. 
They have a history within their organization as being com-
petent soldiers, but haven’t yet faced apocalyptic dangers. 
Chapter Rewards are provided at least once each session. 
Players may work with Directors to create specialized Aspects 
to further make their Characters unique.  
Elite Forces
Your players are the best of the best.  When things go bad 
they are called in to clean up the mess.  Chapter Rewards are 
provided at the end of each gaming session, sometimes also 
at the beginning, and after a major enemy is defeated.  The 
Director can provide around 5-20 additional Power Points 
during Character creation to help enhance Characters.  Direc-
tors may even lower the player’s Disruption Class post-Char-
acter creation to allow for greater rewards and higher stats. 
Players are encouraged to work with Directors to create spe-
cialized Aspects to further make their Characters unique.  
Power Fantasy
Your players are almost superheroes in terms of power.  Many 
are SCPs themselves, reality benders, or something entirely 
unique they designed along with the help of the Director. 
Chapter Rewards are plentiful (2 per session?), and can be 
adjusted to lower Disruption Classes to further the potency of 
the rewards.  Directors can provide 20 or more Power Points 
on Character creation, or lower all Character’s “dividers” by 
a standard amount (dividers can not go below 2).  Your players 
are not only the stars of the show, but it’s going to be quite 
difficult to defeat them!
Anomalous Aspects
Most SCP Canons would not allow Foundation personnel to 
utilize anomalous objects, nor would they employ someone 
with anomalous abilities.  If you would like to mix this up and 
allow your players to cast spells or even play as an SCP, you 
can choose to open the floodgates and see what happens! 
Several other GoI may not only encourage their members to 
possess anomalous properties, they sometimes even require it!
Non-Anomalous plus limited aspects
Directors can ban not just Anomalous Aspects, but they can 
ban other categories as well!  If you want to run a very ba-
sic campaign (great for first timers) you can ban Anomalous 
Aspects, Power Gamer Aspects, Combat Aspects, Disad-
vantage Aspects, or anything else you desire to keep things 
as simple as possible.  This option is perfect for short, sim-
ple games where your players are not going to need to learn 
many nuanced combat rules while still allowing them to create 
a unique custom Character.
Non-Anomalous
Directors can ban all Anomalous Aspects from their game, 
allowing for more storytelling options while keeping the uni-
verse feeling more grounded in reality.  Most SCP.2e cam-
paigns should have Anomalous Aspects banned until the 
Director has a story or campaign where allowing the PCs 
to have these extremely rare abilities becomes relevant 
to the plot.
Limited Anomalous
Instead of banning Anomalous Aspects outright, it might be 
a good option to allow limited Anomalous Aspects.  Perhaps 
the PCs must meet a certain Disruption Class threshold.  Per-
haps each player is allowed just one Anomalous Aspect, or 
allow Anomalous Aspects but then your Chapter Rewards are 
modified to the next highest Disruption Class.  Directors can 
come up with any limitation or additional cost or rule modifi-
cation they desire to fit their storyline.
The Director’s Handbook
99


Anomalous Authorized
Anomalous Aspects are allowed, as long as the player comes 
up with a unique and interesting background to explain why 
their Character has this paranormal power.  All Characters 
should have a reason as to why their Anomalous Aspects ex-
ist, especially when combining several together.  If the Direc-
tor suspects that the player is trying to make the best Charac-
ter mathematically instead of an interesting protagonist, they 
can veto or disallow Aspects as they deem fit.  For example, 
if a player is asked why their Character has three arms, can 
fly, and create portals, if their answer is just “because those 
abilities seem overpowered lol” then the Director can (and 
should) deny the Character until a theme is decided upon and 
built around.
Anomalous Required
Your players are participating within a group where Anoma-
lous abilities are required.  Perhaps the players are all a part 
of an elite squad working for the Serpents Hand and all must 
purchase some form of Thaumaturgy.  Or maybe your game 
takes place during an SCP outbreak and all your players must 
choose to take on the role of a contained SCP.  When you al-
low your players to purchase Anomalous Aspects you should 
always have a reason as to why this type of Character is al-
lowed within your story.
Campaign Lethality
How deadly is your game?  If you are playing a one night 
horror session your players might die once every half hour.  If 
you’re playing a longer campaign with several missions and 
over-arching storyline you’ll probably want to slowly intro-
duce stronger enemies as your players survive each chapter.
Cosmic Horror
Your players are up against an unstoppable threat.  Your sto-
ry should focus on the overwhelming power of this threat and 
preventing it from unleashing upon the planet.  If this threat 
escapes (or already has!), stats will not matter.  Damage will 
not be calculated.  Dice rolls may be ignored.  The threat is so 
deadly that the Director may simply narrate the outcome of 
the inevitable.  
Deadly Encounters
Your players will encounter threats that test their limits very 
often.  A Deadly Encounters campaign will likely have a high-
er PC death rate to that of a Cosmic Horror, because your 
players may feel like the threat can be defeated with the right 
weapons and dice rolls.  While this may have a bit of truth 
to it, Deadly Encounter campaigns often throw the PCs up 
against enemies far above their combined Power Threat Level 
(see page 135).  Combat encounters should be so imbal-
anced that your players should quickly realize that running is 
the only smart thing to do. 
Common Combat
Although combat encounters favor the players in most ways, 
they are common and barely give your players time to breathe 
(or heal!) between encounters.  Waves of enemies are around 
every corner.  Combat is expected once or twice per session. 
Can the players survive the onslaught?  
Rare Combat
Combat is rare, and occurs only during the climax of your sto-
ryline or when the enemy is actively sought out by the players. 
The focus of your story is mystery and Character driven.
Investigation Focused
Very little to no combat is expected in your story.  The story is 
developed through the investigation of events and talking to 
interesting NPCs to uncover the truth.
Campaign Canons
There are several SCP Canons to consider.  If you or your 
players are not familiar with every single SCP universe can-
on in existence, that’s not only expected, it’s almost a cer-
tainty.  Don’t assume your players know how your universe 
works.  Try your best to provide them with details on how you 
expect to run things.  Is the SCP Foundation known by the 
general public or a secret organization?   How many SCP’s 
have they contained so far?  Is the Foundation world-wide? 
Have they traveled through space?  What Groups of Inter-
est exist in your world?  Has the apocalypse already begun? 
What year is it?  Is the Foundation military-strict or do the 
employees often goof around?  The nearly limitless options 
you have in SCP Canons can be intimidating, but don’t let it 
be.   You can come up with the world that best suits the story 
you’re trying to tell, then do your best to explain the “rules 
of the universe” to your players so they have a basic idea of 
social norms and how the world works.  You should also visit 
scp-wiki.wikidot.com/canon-hub for an in-depth look at var-
ious official canon universes, each with a wildly different in-
terpretation on the SCP universe.
Remaining Campaign Options
Aside from Diversity, Length, Power Level, Anomalous Aspects, 
Lethality, and Canon, there are a few other elements you may 
consider for your campaign:
• 
What Character Creation methods are allowed?
	
(See The Three Methods on page 16)
• 
What is your Foundation Presence Level?
	
(See page 83)
• 
If not The Foundation, what organization will 
your PCs be in?
	
(See Groups of Interest on page 94)
• 
What is your Campaign Security Level?
	
(See page 111)
The Director’s Handbook
100


Writing an introductory paragraph
One you have established the rules of your universe and cam-
paign, you can begin to introduce your players to your world.
Type up about a paragraph or so to read to your players as 
a way of warming them up to the themes of your universe, 
story, and broad expectations.  Assume your players are un-
familiar with the SCP universe and explain the situation as an 
opening narrative.  Explain how the players ended up in the 
situation they are currently in, and any global news that has 
occurred in the year you decided the story takes place in.  Af-
ter the players have Characters to play as, read them all the 
summary you typed up and then get deeper into the details 
around your story and how the PCs are tied into the situation.
See example on page 156.
SUS Rules
Why SUS is Important
SUS (Suspicious Uniform Score) is a rating determined by 
the PCs Uniform (clothing/outfit).  Each uniform has weap-
on slots that conceal a weapon of a certain size within them. 
The more eye-catching an outfit, and the less it is capable 
of hiding weaponry, the higher the SUS level.  SUS is very 
important for Directors to keep tabs on.  If your players are 
walking around downtown New York City, and wearing full 
SWAT gear while publicly armed with Assault Rifles, not only 
will this cause a panic, but the SCP Foundation’s secrecy (or 
whatever GoI you are using) is put at risk.  The SUS system 
balances out allowing your players to constantly be armed to 
the teeth in every situation.  SUS subtracts from all Charisma 
related rolls when dealing with the general public, however 
the Director should veto this rule depending on the situation. 
SUS can actually add to a Charisma roll when attempting to 
intimidate some NPCs, and SUS can be ignored if the Char-
acter is wearing attire that is not out of the ordinary for the 
current situation.  
Always consider SUS
Each time the PCs enter an area with civilians, always ask the 
players what their SUS Rating is before continuing.  SUS isn’t 
just a Charisma modifier, it will tell the Director how wary and 
paranoid nearby NPCs are of the PCs.  Players with a score 
of 2 or less will likely be dismissed, but anything 4 or higher 
will absolutely cause a scene.  Level 3 is up to the Director to 
decide.  If the PCs are trying to blend in, any SUS Rating of 
1 or more can be a cause for questioning.  Some Uniforms 
(Like the Business Suit, page 146) can reduce SUS below 
zero while in certain situations.  The Business suit has a SUS 
of zero, and applies a -3 SUS modifier while in a workplace 
environment.  This means that the PC would actually gain a +3 
to Charisma related rolls.
Drawn Weapons
Regardless of what Uniform a player is using, if at any time the 
PC draws their weapon, they get a +4 SUS modifier (This ac-
tion may or may not aid in Intimidation rolls.  Director’s discretion.) 
As denoted on page 145, SUS can cause various levels of 
general panic depending on the level of SUS by one or more 
PCs.  Do not add all players’ SUS together when determining 
the level of disturbance.  
Reverence Rules
Reverence is a system that allows the Director to award play-
ers that enrich the role-playing experience.  Reverence can be 
awarded for several reasons, both in and outside of the role-
playing universe.  Directors should award Reverence as often 
as possible, and allow players to suggest Reverence awards 
to other players as well.  If the entire group does something 
unexpected or triumphant, the Director can award everyone 
at the table a Reverence Point.  Avoid giving Reverence points 
out for good dice rolls (although it’s still fine to do this on oc-
casion) and try to award players evenly to an extent as not 
to play favorites.  You can also give out more than one point 
if you’re feeling generous, but avoid giving out more than 3 
at a time.
Here is a short list of things that should get Reverence awards:
• 
A player accomplishes a goal they set for themselves.
• 
A player helps another player with the system.
• 
A player buys food for everyone (in real life).
• 
A player performs a memorable Fatality.
• 
A player outsmarts the Director in a clever way (don’t punish this!)
• 
All players show up to gaming on time.
• 
A player makes a point to turn off their cellphone all session.
• 
A player helps the Director in some way.
• 
A player describes their Action in an entertaining, 
cinematic way.
• 
A player points out an inconsistency or error by the 
Director (and the Director agrees!)
• 
A player does something funny in-Character.
• 
A player does something funny out-of-Character.
This list could take up five more pages, but the idea is that Rev-
erence can (and should) be awarded often.  As a Director it 
can be easy to forget to let your players know you appreciate 
their actions that help bring a positive atmosphere onto the 
table.  Remind them by giving out Reverence!
Reverence Follows the Player
Another way of making Reverence feel unique is to have the 
number of Reverence points earned follow the player, not 
the Character.  If a PC dies and the player has to make a new 
Character, the Director could allow all Reverence Points to 
carry over onto the new one.  Or if you start a new campaign, 
give the PCs their previous Character’s Reverence Points so 
they feel that their history matters!
The Director’s Handbook
101


Director Advice
This section provides general advice to Directors when run-
ning an SCP.2e campaign.  The advice provided may be of 
use to first timers and veterans alike.
How to Deal with Rules
The Director is allowed to change or add any rule in SCP.2e 
as they desire.  This is a staple for almost any table-top 
role-playing game.  The reason is that if looking up a rule 
breaks the pacing of your game, it’s not worth looking up. 
Judge the situation!  If there’s not a lot going on at the moment 
and the rule in question will likely come up often, sure!  Take 
a moment and see if that rule can be quickly clarified.  Players 
want consistency so they know how to proceed moving for-
ward.  However if there is a stressful situation and you have 
all of your players on the edge of their seat, the worst thing 
you can do is pause the tension while you go read a book for 
ten minutes.  It’s like putting a commercial break in during the 
climax of a movie.  Nothing is more important than player 
Buy-in, so don’t shatter the reality that you worked so hard to 
develop.  Another thing Directors can do is make up their own 
rules or “House Rules.”  Directors are encouraged to play a 
few sessions before implementing house rules to make sure 
they add to the experience rather than detract.
Rules As Written (RAW)
There may come a time in roleplaying where the rules get in 
the way of a realistic outcome.  In these situations you should 
not go by “Rules As Written”.  Always go with what makes 
sense to you, as the Director.  For example, according to the 
Favors System (page 101), the HR Favor type includes 
celebrities.  This means your players could perform a few fa-
vors for a popular TikTok influencer and cash in those points to 
gain a higher Security Clearance or a Military Grade Vehicle. 
Don’t do that.  Also if a player is unconscious they would not 
get to roll Dodge just because the rules don’t explicitly state 
otherwise.  Always use common sense when applying rules to 
any table-top rpg.
But it’s still a Game...
Keeping realism in mind, it’s also important to note that this 
is still a roleplaying game.  There may be some rules and 
standards that are not realistic, namely the rules for weapon 
Mass.  The Mass (size) of a gun is directly related to how good 
the stats are, and this is admittedly unrealistic.  Game me-
chanics that deal with damage and HP should be exempt from 
the previous paragraph.  Monitoring ammunition for firearms 
does not need to be scrutinized, especially if ammunition is 
abundant.  The Director should use their best judgment in each 
case and adjust their game accordingly.  Directors who make 
these types of judgment calls should attempt to remain consis-
tent so PCs can make educated decisions.
Dealing with Death Saves 
When a PC has below zero HP, they can not be healed and 
must roll Fate + Resist Death a the beginning of their Turn. 
The Director should try to push meaningful PC deaths.  If a 
player fails a Death Save, try to allow their final action to 
have a meaningful impact.  Allow the player to have a brave, 
heroic moment or dramatic last words whenever possible. 
Make sure that once the PC dies, it’s clear to all players that 
this is the end of a life.  After death, try to mention the de-
ceased Character as often as possible to add weight and let 
the players reflect upon the struggles they are going through. 
Do not forcefully prevent a PC death as this can also spoil 
player Buy-in.
Dealing with Power Diversity 
SCP.2e features the Disruption Class System.  This system al-
lows Directors to enable players to have wide offset in power, 
with some Characters being much stronger than others, par-
ticularly in combat.  The system is designed so that weaker 
starting Characters (Dark Class) have the potential to out-
shine other Disruption Classes during the late game in terms 
of health, damage, and all around basic combat.  Higher Dis-
ruption classes offer early power levels, with a wide range 
of options both in and out of combat.  This power diversity 
can create interesting dynamics among players, with different 
Characters taking the spotlight at different points in the story. 
However, it may not suit all campaign types; one-shot ad-
ventures, for example, may limit opportunities for Dark Class 
Characters to shine.  Directors should consider the campaign’s 
focus when determining class restrictions to match the desired 
experience.  A diverse range of classes can significantly im-
pact the party’s Threat Level (see page 135) requiring adap-
tation to make combat encounters feel meaningful and ap-
propriately challenging. 
NPC vs NPC combat
Directors often have a lot to manage, and while player in-
teraction should remain the focus of your games, there are 
occasions when NPC interactions arise.  To keep these en-
gagements swift and efficient, utilize simplified rules instead 
of diving into lengthy combat sequences behind your GM 
screen.  Start by taking the attacking NPC’s Perception Rat-
ing (for ranged attacks) or Dexterity Rating (for melee strikes) 
and adding their Physical stat.  Then, compare this total to the 
defending NPC’s Dexterity + Physical stat.  If the attacker’s 
value surpasses the defender’s, the attack lands.  Damage 
calculation can follow two approaches: either roll as normal, 
or take half the dice (rounded up) and treat them as if they 
rolled the maximum value.  For instance, an attack dealing 
4D6 damage would instead deal a flat 12 damage, while 
one inflicting 5D10 damage would result in a flat 30 damage. 
Directors are encouraged to employ methods that expedite 
NPC vs NPC (or SCP!) combat.  Alternatively, they can opt 
for narrative descriptions of combat actions, deciding on out-
comes or damage as appropriate.   Avoid going through the 
Standard Combat Procedures unless the battle is supposed to 
be the centerpiece of action.  
The Director’s Handbook
102


What is Horror? 
Great horror comes from getting your players 
attached to their Characters, immersed into your 
universe, and then putting them in a situation they 
don’t understand and are not equipped to handle.  
Next we will break this all down to explain why it is the case.
Getting your Players Attached
Getting your players attached to their Characters requires 
two things.  Personalization, and Time.
Personalization simply means that your players put time and 
effort into making a Character that is tailor made to their 
specifications.  It allows the Character to feel personal to 
them, possibly even a part of them.  The death of a deeply 
thought out personalized Character will feel far more dev-
astating than a premade Character who’s backstory is never 
touched upon.  
Time is just that.  After enough time (gaming sessions) have 
passed, the player will grow more and more attached to 
their Character.  Again, the more attached a player is to their 
Character the more terrified they will be of experiencing that 
Character’s death.
Once your players actually fear death within your universe, 
great tension and drama can flourish.
Player Immersion/Buy-in
Player immersion is very difficult to attain.  Immersion is how 
engaged and emotionally invested your players are in the 
game world.  It’s about creating a sense of “being there” 
within the fictional universe, where players temporarily in-
habit their Characters’ roles and feel a strong connection to 
the story and the environment.  Another term for this is player 
“Buy-in.”  Tension and suspense is paramount to horror, and 
neither will be achieved well if your players have no Buy-in. 
There are several ways to help achieve immersion.  We will 
touch briefly upon seven important ones of note, in no par-
ticular order.
• 
Atmosphere
	
When trying to provide a good atmosphere for your 
players there are three things to consider.  The first is the 
physical location you decide to role-play in.  Be sure 
there is enough room and enough comfortable chairs to 
accommodate everyone.  Make sure the lighting is bright 
enough for players to read their character sheets, but not 
too bright or shining in a location that makes it uncomfort-
able to view for long periods of time.  Including props or 
cosplay into your game can also help, but it isn’t always 
necessary or practical.  The second is your in-universe 
atmosphere.  How well you describe important moments 
and add unexpected detail to your environment can help 
your players convince themselves that your world is a 
tangible force.  The third atmosphere is your social envi-
ronment.  Playing with a group of friends you’ve known 
for years is an easy way to play in a healthy social envi-
ronment.  You don’t want real-life drama between two 
players who don’t like each other being a toxic dynamic 
in your games.  This can spoil your game for a multitude 
of reasons.  It’s also important to know your audience.  If 
you’re playing with people you don’t know all that well, 
it may be beneficial to distribute an anonymous question-
naire which allows players to discreetly ban topics they 
consider off limits, such as animal harm or sexual content. 
Any topic that can create awkward moments can spoil 
your social atmosphere and likely will lead to players 
dropping out of your campaign.  
• 
Music
	
Possibly the biggest mistake you can make as a Direc-
tor is to forget to have soft non-vocal music playing in 
the background.  Don’t just shuffle your favorite playlist. 
Find something generic and simple to play quietly (but 
not too quiet) in the background to help set the mood. 
Whenever possible you should change the music to 
fit the scene.  Combat encounters should have exciting 
dramatic music playing in the background while the low 
points should have something much slower and calm. 
Avoid using “ttrpg soundtracks” you find on YouTube 
that include sound effects like howling wolves or wick-
ed laughter.  Adding sound effects in your game can 
come across as cheesy and break immersion.  Finding 
video game soundtracks are often a great choice, es-
pecially if you are able to quickly switch between tracks 
to match the appropriate mood.  Music is vastly import-
ant, possibly the most important item on this list, so do 
your due diligence and prepare your soundtrack before 
you start playing.  If you think it will take longer than 
five to ten seconds to alter the music for a scene, don’t 
worry about it and just keep the game moving forward. 
• 
Memorable Characters
	
Your NPCs are people too!  Give them unique speaking 
traits and quirks.  Give them interesting scars the players 
will be tempted to ask about, or have them wear jewelry 
that seems out of place.  Make sure you have a name 
at the ready for every NPC your players come across. 
Nothing will tell the players that an NPC can be ignored 
faster than if the Director is noticed to come up with a name 
on the fly.  This doesn’t mean every NPC in your universe 
needs to have different colored eyes or extreme accents, 
but you should always try to keep each NPC interaction 
memorable in some way.  This also goes for combat en-
counters.  Remember that your NPCs won’t always want 
to fight to the death!  Some will cry for medical treatment 
and beg for their lives.  Some may be impressed by a PCs 
combat ability and show admirable respect.  Others may 
The Director’s Handbook
103


even switch sides!  Use combat encounters to remind your 
players that the people (or creatures) they are fighting 
have lives too.  Before an NPC takes their Combat Ac-
tion, try to give them at least one line of dialog before 
they strike.  This will remind the players they are fighting 
against more than just stats.
• 
Use PC Backstories
	
Another optimal way to make your players immersed 
while also growing the attachment to their Characters is 
to include elements of a PCs backstory into your storyline. 
This typically isn’t ideal for shorter campaigns, and it can 
be off-putting to the other players if they feel it’s “The 
Steve Session” and they don’t get to be as involved as 
the star of the show.  The best way to include player back-
stories is to pull threads of their history into your base 
plot, which will allow the knowledge the player has to 
help push the story forward.  For example, if a PC has a 
backstory about how they tried to contain an SCP but lost 
a dear friend in the process, the Director could alter the 
SCP in the players backstory to involve one they already 
wanted to include.  Then instead of revealing the SCP di-
rectly, the party could come across a clue which relates 
to the deceased friend.  Then the PC could put two and 
two together and warn the rest of the party of the po-
tential SCP threat, and explain the origin of their knowl-
edge if they wish.  It’s just a small backstory inclusion 
into the Director’s story, but it will make the game world 
feel all the more lived in, while also making the play-
er feel that the story is tailor made for their Character. 
• 
Consistency
	
Consistency is important to player immersion because the 
more you make your game universe have a set series of 
laws and a standard level of realism, the more the player 
will get the vibe that your game universe is a separate enti-
ty than the Director’s brain.  At the core of every table-top 
roleplaying game are game mechanics.  These mechanics 
help maintain the consistency of your in-game universe. 
Traveling long distances causes fatigue.  Characters need 
to sleep.  Weapons hurt people.  NPCs have feelings.  Ev-
ery fabric of your made-up universe relies on universal 
truths maintaining consistency.  If you break these rules 
too often to force your per-determined storyline, your uni-
verse loses consistency.  If you use metagame knowledge 
to have an NPC perform an action they wouldn’t nor-
mally take, your universe loses consistency.  If you allow 
your favorite player to reroll a Death Save, your universe 
loses consistency.  It’s important that you provide a simu-
lated world with concrete rules so that your players know 
what they can expect from any given result.  The more 
your universe feels like it’s surfing the Director’s mental 
whim, the less your universe will feel consistent and real. 
• 
Player Agency
	
Your players need to feel like their decisions (or lack 
thereof!) matter.  Your players are the stars of your ta-
ble-top rpg movie.  If they don’t have a significant impact 
on your in-game universe and story, then they won’t feel 
like anything they do matters.  If your players ever say 
they are not sure ‘what they are supposed to be doing’ 
it is a red flag that your players are not immersed in your 
game.  Your players shouldn’t feel like they are not find-
ing the right answer to your questions/situations.  They 
should be thinking about any possible solution to them, 
and pick the best one.  You must do your best as a Direc-
tor to remind your players that they can do anything they 
want, but not just by telling them directly - show them! 
Let their crazy plans work if the dice roll favorably.  Use 
the “rule of cool” which means that if the decision the 
players came up with would make a memorable scene, 
allow it to work with entertaining results!  Nothing will 
break immersion faster than when a player thinks that 
the thing they did was “incorrect” and they need to keep 
guessing until their solution matches the solution that the 
Director thought up previously.  It’s important for the Di-
rector to make players feel like they’re contending with 
the universe itself rather than the Director.  The Direc-
tor must be willing to acknowledge successful dice rolls 
and clever player ideas, even if it necessitates adjusting 
the storyline to accommodate the new circumstances. 
• 
Consequences
	
Once your players understand that they can do any-
thing they desire, they may often try to overstep their 
bounds.  If your players run amok, rob banks, kill NPCs 
indiscriminately, and hack into Foundation computer ter-
minals, then you need to roll up your sleeves and bring 
down the destruction and misfortune.  Not necessari-
ly when it happens, but when a player does something 
that would warrant bad consequences, that’s when your 
NPCs get to work in the background of your universe. 
Bank security cameras are being looked at.  The body 
is being investigated.  The Foundation is making room 
for another D-Class.  Then when the timing is right the 
consequences for the careless deed are met with the iron 
fist of justice.  Your game universe will only feel real if 
it doesn’t also feel like a playground where your play-
ers are free to do anything at all without consequences. 
The Director’s Handbook
104


A Situation Players Don’t Understand
At the heart of every SCP entry on the wiki is an object, Char-
acter, location, or situation that isn’t fully understood.  If it was 
fully understood then the SCP would no longer be considered 
anomalous.  Your players will never fully understand the situa-
tion they are put into.  This is the nature of any plot-line or sto-
ry involving an anomaly.  The players are battling against the 
unknown, and their lack of understanding on how to properly 
handle the situation is the catalyst of ambiguity which drives 
the horror.  The Director, on the other hand, should have a 
strong idea on what unknowable clockwork is happening in 
the background to maintain a coherent set of laws which can 
determine any given outcome.  
It is then up to the players to try to make an educated guess 
about the situation in order to end up with a favorable out-
come.  Each decision the players make regarding the dilem-
ma should be tinged with a pervasive sense of insecurity and 
doubt about the plan’s likelihood of success. This prevailing 
lack of confidence will heighten tension and intensify player 
anticipation as they await the outcome of their choices.  
A Situation Players are Not Equipped to Handle
To bring a feel of hopelessness and dread, your players will 
not have the knowledge or resources to overcome the situa-
tion (or entity) which they are up against.  To accomplish this, 
the Director has two very different methods of delivery.  The 
first is simple - restrict higher Disruption Classes and force sit-
uations which require low SUS levels.  This will reduce the par-
ty’s combative capabilities and make them feel unequipped 
for the mission.  Forcing your players to all start off as D-Class 
Characters is another great way to drive a feeling of despair 
and desperation.  The second option requires a little more 
time, but is just as effective.  Allow your players to become 
powerful and confident.  Let them take on several missions 
that feel easy as they level up their stats and gear.  That’s 
when you pull the rug out from under them and pit the players 
up against an SCP where stats don’t really matter.  They wit-
ness a powerful ally NPC (or careless PC) die in a horrifying 
fate worse than death.  It’s important that the Director strongly 
telegraphs that they just jumped into a situation in which it 
doesn’t matter how many big guns they own, or how many 
HP they have, they are not equipped to handle this new ene-
my or circumstance.
Once you get your players attached to their Characters, 
immersed into your universe, and then put them in a sit-
uation they don’t understand and are not equipped to 
handle, you will achieve Directing a great horror expe-
rience which will keep your players scared but eager to 
keep pressing forward with cautious terror.
Beyond Horror 
Fear of the unknown is paramount to the SCP universe, but 
there are other types of horror you can present to your play-
ers.  Fear of disease, body horror, fear of technology, fear of 
isolation, cosmic horror, the directions you can go are end-
less.  Although the SCP Universe rose from a seed of science 
fiction horror themes, the wiki has grown to a far broader 
scope.  Why not skip horror altogether?  You don’t need ev-
ery situation to frighten your players.  Make it action focused, 
espionage, super heroes, or a murder mystery.  Mixing ab-
surd situations caused by anomalies can be a great way to in-
corporate fun and lighthearted scenarios into your game.  For 
example, you could force your players to participate in a cup-
cake eating contest....to save humanity!  SCP-871 has gotten 
out of hand and the players must eat two dozen cupcakes 
daily until they find a practical solution.  You can also check 
out the countless SCP-J files found at wikidot.com/joke-scps 
for ideas.  With the SCP.2e Don’t feel constrained to having a 
horror themed plot.  Play whatever kind of game that makes 
you have the most fun.  It’s easy to forget as a Director that 
it’s your job to make sure everyone at the table is having fun - 
and that includes yourself!
Let players help shape your world
You can help make your job as Director a bit easier by ask-
ing your players to help world-building.  Next time they enter 
a bar, tell the players “Give me the name of the bar, and a 
theme.”  This will help make the story feel a bit more person-
alized for the players and help them remember locations of 
interest.  Give the players the ability to describe in detail a 
scene whenever possible and as often as possible.  A great 
question to ask your players after they declare an Action is 
“What does it look like when you do this?”  When a Director 
asks this question it’s like they are letting the players hold the 
steering wheel of gaming for a moment, as they explain in 
personalized detail how they truly envision their Character.
How to prepare 
Before you begin your first gaming session, you must pre-
pare.  But how much?  Ask yourself what you expect to hap-
pen next session - just next session.  Picture yourself at a ta-
ble going through your scenario and think about what maps 
would be 100% necessary, what NPC stats you will need to 
create, and what detailed clues will need to be typed out. 
Most of the rest can be improvised.  Not every session is 
going to require a map.  Using a piece of paper and some 
pocket change can be enough to explain relative distances 
in a pinch.  Detailed maps and miniatures are typically ideal 
in that they more accurately describe the scene at a glance, 
but you’re not going to have a map at the ready for every 
possible location your players decide to travel to.  NPC stats 
may be important in case combat arises, but what’s even 
more important is to have a mentally prepared Character 
to interact with the players.  What secrets are they hiding, 
The Director’s Handbook
105


what are their motivations, what are their mannerisms, etc. 
Put yourself in the NPCs mind and limit your knowledge to 
their world-view.  Once you’ve done this you will be excit-
ed for when your players speak to this Character instead of 
scrambling for notes about what clues they might mention off-
handedly.  It’s important to prepare before each gaming ses-
sion, and often that can be very rewarding, but don’t make 
the mistake of over-preparing.  If you lose yourself too much 
into world-building you will overshadow your players whom 
are the stars of the show.  Not only that but if you over-pre-
pare you will feel frustrated when your players don’t explore 
every minute detail and will often subconsciously push the 
party forward because you’re too excited to get to the next 
great plot point.  Another thing you can do as a Director is to 
pick one thing to improve on each session.  Don’t expect that 
since you’ve read this whole chapter and watched ten hours 
of GM tutorials that your brain is going to be reprogrammed 
into the perfect storyteller.  Pick one thing to improve on each 
session and focus on that.  Once you see if and how it im-
proves your game then you can incorporate it into future ses-
sions when appropriate.  
Pocket Pacing
There’s one trick that you should consider using to prepare for 
any game session, and that is Pocket Pacing.  Come up with 
an event that you can spring on your players at any time, at 
any location.  This surprise event should be exciting, like a 
combat encounter, or have significant relevance in your story 
that it shifts things to a new gear.  Spring your event onto your 
players whenever you feel the pace of the session has gotten 
too slow for too long. 
Unpredictable Sessions
Having your bases covered in preparation for the next session 
doesn’t mean you’ll know what’s going to happen, and that’s 
a good thing!  It can be very fun for Directors who don’t know 
what’s going to happen next.  Will the players pull the story 
in a direction you never expected?  Adapt to the new situation 
and see where they take you!  Who knows maybe the players 
will create a new friend (or rival!) due to their actions.
 
PC Limitations
Your players have limitless choices in your game, but there are 
a few things they may attempt that should be discouraged.
Using the SCP wiki 
Your players should be discouraged from looking up the SCP 
wiki on their phones while playing (unless their Characters 
would have access to specific data).  Your PCs may attempt 
to find specific SCPs to help them deal with their current situa-
tion.  There are thousands of SCPs, and finding one that could 
drastically effect your storyline in the players favor is highly 
likely.  Avoid allowing your PCs to have access to any SCP 
they look up.  You don’t have to allow every SCP to have a 
place in your universe, or allow the PCs access to them.  Even 
if you involve a specific SCP in your storyline, the Director 
is allowed to alter the nature of that SCP in order to fit the 
plot.  Allowing your players to access the SCP on the wiki 
may interpret the entity in a way that was not intended.  If one 
of your PCs has vast knowledge of the SCP universe, let that 
player know that the knowledge they have may not accurate-
ly reflect how they work within your universe, and it is always 
best for the player’s Characters to do the research themselves 
over using “real-world” knowledge about it’s nature.
Player Requested Checks & Written in Skills
The Director should almost always be the one whom requests 
Attribute Checks.  Players should be discouraged from re-
questing checks themselves, as this is the responsibility of the 
Director.  For example, a player could ask the Director for a 
Fate Check to see if they find a rocket launcher inside of the 
utility closet.  The Director may be tempted to just assign a 
high difficulty to dismiss the unlikely outcome, but don’t do 
this.  Likewise, if a player asks the Director if they can perform 
an impossible task, such as inventing a new type of gravity 
propulsion technology, the Director should tell the player that 
it isn’t possible with current day technology rather than just 
giving them a very high Intellect + Technology difficulty.  It’s 
okay to say “no.”  When the Director provides difficulties for 
too many outlandish avenues, the universe starts to be less 
grounded in reality and it pulls the direction of your story to 
whatever the dice can allow.  A good example of this is when 
a player asks for a roll to detect lies.  The problem with asking 
for this type of check is that if the player fails the detect lies 
check, they will know that whatever the Director tells them is 
the opposite.  This is why rolls such as these should be made in 
secret by the Director.  Some of the more powerful skills such 
as Detect Lies and Engineering are written-in skills.  These 
types of skills can break the rules stated so far.  Characters 
that specialize in detecting lies or inventing new technology 
should be given a few opportunities to “break” your universe 
slightly.  These written-in skills are unique to that Character 
and are what make them special, and as such should be al-
lowed some lenience as to how far these skills can be pushed 
depending on their levels.  
Managing Real-World Knowledge in Your Game
Occasionally, you may find players who become irritated 
when their real-world knowledge doesn’t mesh with the fic-
tional universe you’ve crafted.  These players might attempt 
to challenge the Director’s knowledge with armchair science. 
While it’s valuable to encourage engagement and creativi-
ty at the gaming table, it’s equally important to maintain the 
integrity of the game world.  Directors hold the role of the 
world-builder, and it’s wise to be assertive when necessary, 
and move forward quickly.  If a player’s real-world knowledge 
threatens to disrupt the narrative or overpower the game, con-
sider discussing potential compromises or house rules outside 
of the game session.  Striking a balance between real-world 
expertise and the game’s fictional universe can lead to a more 
enjoyable gaming experience for everyone involved.
The Director’s Handbook
106


a Simulation-Style RPG
While narrative-focused tabletop RPGs excels in collabora-
tive storytelling and improvisation, they often lean toward 
abstract resolutions and human structured storylines.  This 
approach can sometimes compromise the depth of realism 
and consistency that this setting demands.  The SCP universe, 
known for its detailed containment protocols and cold sci-
entific demeanor, benefits from a simulation-style type RPG 
like SCP.2e.  In a narrative-focused system, the unpredictable 
nature of SCP anomalies might be handled with more lenien-
cy, potentially diluting the tension and risk that makes SCP 
scenarios exciting.  A simulation style RPG allows for more 
emergent storytelling, where the actions and consequences of 
Character decisions often create a tangible story in a world 
where the chance of failure is a very real possibility.  SCP.2e’s 
simulation approach stands as a deliberate choice for play-
ers who seek to navigate the complex and often hazardous 
anomalies, and players will find great pride in the rare in-
stances of surviving victory in such a harsh setting.  All that out 
of the way, if an SCP or enemy is so powerful that the game 
mechanics shouldn’t matter, the Director should opt for nar-
rative combat (see also Combat Procedures on page 60). 
Veteran PCs should see a red flag anytime a hostile SCP is en-
countered and Combat Time never triggers.  Directors should 
use their best judgment as to which encounters should be nar-
rative and which should use Standard Combat Procedures.  If 
the power level of the SCP is unknown, you can implement the 
combat rules until it becomes obvious.  If your players have 
had ample warning about the SCP, then forgo the rules and 
simply explain the outcomes of the carnage.  The same can be 
done for very weak NPC encounters.  Don’t slow down your 
game just because there’s a single guard in the way.  Allow 
the Cup De Grâu (see page 71) and keep the game mov-
ing forward.  
aged, and thus discounted slightly.  Conversely, Aspects with 
mechanics that potentially disrupt the game, such as over-
powered abilities like invisibility, lie detection, or flight, may 
warrant increased costs.  This ensures that the game’s enjoy-
ability and player interactions remain engaging and fair.  A 
great example of an Aspect with a low cost because it en-
courages PC backstory integration is Training Partner (page 
49).  This aspect is cheap because it encourages players 
to tie in their characters backstories, and pushes characters 
to have a similar skill-set (which may be a bad thing!).  Invis-
ibility and Flight can be the solution to many problems in a 
tabletop rpg setting, and lie detection often circumvents NPC 
interaction and intrigue.  This is why Aspects of these levels 
have either increased costs or greater limitations to them. 
Aspects that give straight up skill bonuses (like college or past 
experience Aspects) have a basic formula that should be fol-
lowed, and modified as needed.  Take how many total Skill 
Points that Aspect would cost (+1’s cost 1 Skill Point, +2’s cost 
3 Skill Points, and +3’s cost 6 Skill Points) and apply this cost 
directly to how much the Aspect costs in Talent Points.  Reduce 
this cost by roughly 20% and you can determine the History 
Points cost.  Aspects that give Crucial Skill multipliers (espe-
cially to Dodge) should include 1 Power Point added to the 
cost to obtain them, while Aspects that have niche bonuses 
should be discounted even further.  Then you could apply the 
standard bonus “If both point types are used to purchase this As-
pect, increase all above multipliers by 1” or you could come up 
with something else.  Directors that have played SCP.2e for a 
while should get an idea about what Aspects seem too strong 
or too weak and adjust costs as they deem necessary.  Power 
Points are about three to four times as valuable as Talent or 
History points.  When creating Anomalous Aspects, keep tabs 
on how many Power Points each Disruption Class gets to start. 
If the Aspect seems too strong for a Vlam level Character, in-
crease the Power Point cost to above 5.  If you have an idea 
for a very overpowered Aspect but would still like to offer it 
as an option, have the cost around 25 Power Points so that 
it is only available to Amida Class Characters.  Aspects are 
designed so that players can choose to either buy them with 
one point type and have a good bonus or ability, or spend 
multiple point types into them to allow the Aspect to really 
define their Character.  Keep this fact in mind when coming up 
with bonuses associated with spending multiple point types. 
Whenever you are stumped on how much an Aspect should 
cost, simply compare it to the others listed in this book and 
attempt to gauge where the costs might land.  Also be mindful 
that History Points represent the Characters History, and past 
events that may have helped shape their current skills.  Talent 
Points represent a natural, or born ability for something, and 
Power Points are simply a generic point type that trumps them 
all.  Remember, anomalous Aspects can only be purchased 
using Power Points, but just because an Aspect isn’t anoma-
lous doesn’t mean it can’t be just as powerful.
Customizing the System
Directors can further customize SCP.2e however they like to 
better fit their narrative.  Below you will find some advice on 
how to expand your game content.  Note that these are all ad-
vanced Director techniques, and should only be implemented 
by Directors who are very familiar with the game mechanics of 
SCP.2e.  Let your players know ahead of time if you are exper-
imenting with adding new things to the game, and warn them it 
may be possible that the additions may be altered mid-game.
Creating your own Aspects
Even though there are many Aspects in SCP.2e, you may want 
to make extra to help your players have even more unique 
skill-sets and abilities tailored to your game.  This section is 
the Director’s guide to creating new Aspects.  While striving 
for balance, remember that fostering a positive gaming envi-
ronment takes precedence in creating new Aspects.  Aspects 
that contribute positively to teamwork, enrich character back-
stories, and enhance the overall narrative should be encour-
The Director’s Handbook
107


Custom Player Abilities
Often times you will find players wishing there was an Aspect 
that perfectly fits the idea for their Characters concept.  For 
example, if a player had their hopes on making a character 
with a pet talking horse.  In this case since there isn’t a “Own 
Talking Horse” Aspect, the Director could quickly come up 
with a cost.  An Aspect such as this doesn’t sound like it could 
be broken or ruin the game.  It’s just a strange fun thing the 
player would like to have as a part of their Character.  Direc-
tors should come up with a fair cost for something like this on 
the spot, typically using only Power Points.  If an Aspect is 
suggested to the Director before the game, the Director could 
come up with more detailed stats for such an Aspect that in-
clude multiple point type costs if they wanted to.  Maybe if 
you spend some additional Talent points the horse is very 
smart and can do calculus.  Maybe if History points were also 
spent, the horse could speak to you telepathically.  Directors 
can put as much time and thought into player requested As-
pects as they feel necessary, but remember that just because 
you created a custom Aspect for the player doesn’t mean they 
are forced to buy it!  Directors should be very cautious of cre-
ating custom Aspects that drastically alter the games mechan-
ics.  If such an Aspect was created and later deemed to be 
game-breaking or too powerful, the Director should reduce 
the power of the Aspect (a.k.a. Nerf) the ability between ses-
sions, and explain to the player why the change was made. 
An easy way to lower the power of an Aspect is to make it cost 
Exertion, or increase the cost of Exertion required.  This will 
limit the PC from overly relying on the Aspect for every situ-
ation.  Directors should allow the player to refund the Aspect 
if they are still unhappy and purchase standard Aspects from 
the book instead.
Altering Aspects for Aesthetics
Directors should allow players to alter the appearance of their 
characters or the actions of their characters if it better suits 
the players original character concept.  Additionally, players 
should buy Aspects that mechanically relate to their character 
concept even if the Aspect doesn’t specify the same theme. 
For example, if a player wanted to play as someone who had 
rock skin, they could purchase several levels of the Damage 
Reduction Aspect, then either buy the Ugly/Creepy aspect 
(since they have skin that looks rocky) or buy the Always SUS 
Aspect and say their skin is actually made out of rock.  None 
of the aforementioned Aspects mention having rock skin, but 
adding this bit of flavor helps the character concept.  Another 
example is the Enhanced Attribute anomalous Aspect, which 
greatly enhances a single Attribute.  The player should come 
up with a reason why!  As long as the explanation doesn’t 
force more complex mechanics, the Director should allow 
the reasoning.  Maybe an enhanced Dexterity Character 
experiences gravity differently.  Maybe enhanced Perception 
makes the PCs eyes glow white, or buy enhanced Willpower 
and Intellect because the Character is secretly an A.I.!  As long 
as introducing strange and unusual flavor into a Character 
doesn’t mechanically alter the game, the Director should not 
only approve these alterations, but encourage them! 
Create your own Expansion
SCP.2e was designed to allow for easy expansion material. 
You can create a list of a dozen or so new Aspects, new 
Character Classes, new weapons, Outfits, gear, or even rule 
sets.  Exertion can be used as a generic “energy” source 
for new mechanics introduced.  The Custom Meters on the 
character sheets can also be utilized.  Create entire Foundation 
Sites with specific SCPs in the containment chambers. 
Expand upon your favorite Group of Interest, or build one 
around the many SCP-001 submissions.  26letterpublishing 
will have a template available for you to use to create 
your own SCP.2e expansion content on our SCP Discord - 
discord.gg/9KjeBsn6Sg.  You can even upload your final 
product to drivethrurpg.com and sell it!  Make sure you follow 
all guidelines the template gives, don’t use copyrighted or 
A.I. content, and always credit the creators of any SCP’s 
mentioned throughout the book.  Approved expansion 
content will be linked to on our discord server.
The System Serves Your Story
SCP.2e is brimming with options for the Director to alter 
the system to best fit the story they are trying to tell.  Once 
Directors are comfortable with the system they should alter 
the rules of the game to tell the story they want to tell.  
There’s no limit to what you can change.  You could force 
your players to a single Uniform, Disruption Class, and/or 
Weapon.  Limit Crucial Skill Multipliers to x2 instead of x3, 
or get rid of Skill Multipliers completely.  Get rid of Attacks 
of Opportunity, or the option to spend Exertion to Dodge 
before your turn comes up.  Get rid of the Mutilation rules, 
or make up your own list of outcomes.  Make up new rules, 
like dropping a weapon if it has too high of recoil, alter how 
Max HP is calculated, or include rules you like from other 
roleplaying games.  Get creative!  The more you experiment 
with adding limitations or creating new options, the more 
you will get a feel for the type of game you enjoy running.  
Try to tailor your ruleset to match the feel and level of 
danger you’re aiming for.  You don’t even have to play in the 
SCP Universe, make up your own!  
Let your players know up front what rules you are including that are 
not in the book, and what rules you are ignoring or vetoing.  
 
The Director’s Handbook
108


The Director’s Handbook
109


Other RPGs of Interest
There are many other Tabletop-RPGs that make a great fit 
for use with the SCP universe.  There are many variations 
of tabletop RPGs which fall somewhere on a scale of rules-
light to rules-heavy.  Simulation based to narrative.  Direc-
tors are encouraged to try using these different dice sys-
tems or simply utilize the additional resources they provide. 
Here are a list of notable games that take heavy inspiration 
from the SCP universe:
SCP The Tabletop RPG (1st edition)
The first edition of the SCP Tabletop RPG was released in 2021 after 
a successful Kickstarter campaign.  There are plenty of resources 
and maps from first edition that translate over to SCP.2e quite nicely! 
drivethrurpg.com/product/334521/SCP-The-Tabletop-RPG
Fear in the Foundation (FITF)
Fear in the Foundation was released in 2020 by Nightshade games. 
FITF uses a traditional D20 class-based dice system and includes 
extensive resources while also taking place directly in the SCP uni-
verse.  It features 100 anomalous entities, 70 anomalous phenome-
na,100 anomalous items, and 54 premade maps.  
www.fearinthefoundation.com
Delta Green
Delta Green, introduced in 1997 as a setting for Call of Cthulhu, im-
merses players in a world where they must safeguard the American 
populace against insurmountable Lovecraftian horrors.  Players find 
themselves equipped with extensive paramilitary resources, offer-
ing a unique balance of power.  Despite this advantage the game 
remains challenging, presenting a brutal and relentless narrative 
that tests the limits of the players’ abilities and resolve.
www.delta-green.com
Triangle Agency
Triangle Agency raised $379,603 on Kickstarter in 2023 and is 
heavily inspired by the SCP universe and the video game Control. 
It uses a unique D4 dice system and the players have the ability to 
alter reality to accomplish goals.
www.hauntedtable.games
External Containment Bureau
Extremal Containment Bureau was released in in 2023 by Myth-
ic Gazetteer, LLC and is inspired by the SCP universe, X-Files, The 
Magnus Archives, and many others.  It uses a D6 dice pool system 
similar to the ttrpg Blades in the Dark.
mythicgazetteer.itch.io/external-containment-bureau
Fear In The Foundation, Delta Green, Call of Cthulhu, Triangle Agency, 
and External Containment Bureau are not associated with 26letterpublish-
ing and are mentioned as reference only.  Summarization of materials may 
not accurately reflect the products listed.
Expansions of Interest  
For use with the SCP.2e game system
Be sure you follow 26letterpublishing on drivethrurpg.com to 
keep up to date with the latest SCP.2e expansion content!
Weapon Customization 
This expansion will allow players to customize their own weapons 
using attachments of all sorts and craft the perfect weapon for their 
character build.  If you get one expansion for SCP.2e, make it this one!
100+ Anomalous
An expanded list of Aspects, including several more Anomalous As-
pects and others tied to various GoI.  A great addition for players 
who are seeking rare and obscure character builds!  Also includes 
rules for players to own an SCP/anomalous object!
More to come!

or join our discord server for resources to create and sell your very 
own!  discord.gg/9KjeBsn6Sg
You can find even more resources for SCP.2e over at
Arcane Vault Assembly
