The Director’s Handbook
110


Level 3 Security Unlock card, however, is not added to the Level 2 
deck until two additional chapters have been successfully complet-
ed.  Then it takes four chapters for Level 4, and so on.
Drama Cards are entirely Optional
The system is only in place for Directors that choose to incorporate 
them.  Drama Cards are often 4th wall breaking and provide play-
ers with a very meta option or effect.  This can be off-putting to some 
players as it can possibly feel like it cheapens your story.  SCP2.e 
functions perfectly fine without Drama Cards.  Directors should “Ban 
all Drama Cards” during final boss encounters and the climax of 
their game to prevent unsatisfying conclusions that get silly and 
weird due to rapid use of cards.
Quest Cards
Some Drama Cards require the player to enact out a specific sit-
uation in order to receive a reward.  The Drama Card “Friend or 
Foe” requires the player to convince an enemy to switch sides 
and become an ally.  If a player successfully does this in game, 
they may play the card and receive the reward “Gain +3 Mental 
Attribute Tokens.”
Used Cards
After a Drama Card is used it should be set aside in a separate pile 
and not shuffled back into the deck.  Directors may keep used cards 
in a used pile for the remainder of the campaign, or restock decks 
with old cards as a sort of reward for the party.
The Drama Card Deck
The Drama Card deck itself contains 25 Drama Cards for each Se-
curity Level, plus 4 Security Level Unlock cards - for a total of 129 
cards.  It can be purchased at Drivethrurpg.com or by following 
the QR code.
Drama Cards
What are Drama Cards?  Drama Cards are an optional deck of 
cards that can be purchased to provide players with “meta-agen-
cy” while giving Directors a fun surprise.  Each Drama Card has a 
special effect or ability that can come in handy to players in various 
situations.  For the most part Drama Cards will be self-explanatory. 
They will tell you when you can play them (if they don’t, you can 
play them at any time) and will detail their effect.  Your Director will 
come up with the outcome after you play a card.  Sometimes the ef-
fect may not be as impactful as you hoped.  The Director does have 
the final judgment, so don’t be shocked if your card doesn’t have the 
capability to destroy an SCP outright.  Players should (try to) keep 
Drama Cards they’ve drawn hidden from other players and even 
the Director.  Drama Cards in SCP.2e are split up into five different 
decks.  Each deck represents the Drama Cards Security Level, 
which determines how powerful the effect might be.  (Drama 
Card Security Levels are not related to your Characters Security 
Level in any way.  Security Levels are only used for the cards to 
maintain the SCP theme and feel.)
Note:  Attribute Tokens and Skill Points obtained from Drama Cards 
must be spent during or before the next Chapter Reward is awarded. 
Drama Cards can not increase Skill Multipliers above x3.
Campaign Security Level
Directors may limit the power scale of their campaign by eliminating 
specific power levels of Drama Cards.  If you’d still like to enjoy the 
excitement of using Drama Cards but do not want to allow players 
to decimate your storyline, Directors can declare a Security Level for 
the campaign itself.  Players are only allowed to buy Drama Cards 
of equal or lesser than the campaigns Security Level.  Directors could 
even reward players for completing certain tasks or defeating ene-
mies by unlocking a new Security Level for the campaign.
Security Level Advancement
Each Security Level will have an optional card that unlocks the next 
Security Level Deck.  Directors can slowly unlock higher grade Dra-
ma Cards by placing one or more of the unlock cards within the 
decks.  This helps the Director to set the pace for how often new 
Drama Card Decks are unlocked.
Purchasing Drama Cards
To buy a Drama Card, a PC must spend Reverence points equal 
to the Drama Cards Security Level plus one.  You can only pur-
chase a number of Drama Cards per session equal to the 
latest Drama Card Security Level that has been unlocked 
(i.e. if you have Security Level 2 unlocked, you may only buy up to 
two cards per session).
Drama Card Exchange
Players can exchange Drama Cards for Reverence points equal to 
the cards Security Level minus one.  For example, if you exchange 
a Security Level 2 card you will get back one Reverence point.  This 
is useful for Drama Cards that are not relevant to your Character or 
situation.  Drama Cards Exchanged for Reverence points are shuf-
fled back into the deck.
Suggested Use
Players start with a Campaign Security Level of one. After success-
fully surviving one Chapter, the Level two Security Level Unlock 
card is added to the Level one Drama Card deck.  Once this card 
is drawn, the Level two cards become available for purchase.  The 
The Director’s Handbook
111


The Favor System
The organizations your players work for (such as the SCP 
Foundation) more often than not have nearly unlimited finan-
cial resources.  Players should have access to nearly all the 
supplies and money they require for any given circumstance. 
As such, PCs may add any Items (page 148), Uniforms 
(page 145), or Weapons (page 149) to their inventory 
any time they are at their home base without needing to con-
sult the Director in most circumstances.  Since SCP.2e does 
not use a currency system Characters are free to alter their 
loadouts as they see fit during downtime, however there are 
rare items more difficult to obtain not contained in these lists. 
Requests for extravagantly expensive items & weaponry will 
be met with resistance.  That is, unless someone owes you a 
favor...
What is the Favor System?
The Favor System is a way for Directors to keep track of what 
allied NPCs are willing to go above and beyond for the PCs. 
Each time a PC engages with an NPC within their organiza-
tion, the Director may offer a side-quest that, once complet-
ed, will tally a Favor Point in relation to that NPC and their 
profession.  Players may then “spend” these Favor Points to 
gain various items, information, or events in order to aid their 
adventure.  For example, a PC could talk to a Foundation Sci-
entist and discover they are depressed because they no lon-
ger have access to visit SCP-999.  If the PC talks to other staff 
members and reunite the Scientist with SCP-999, then that 
NPC would grant the PC with a Favor Point.  Then at any point 
in the future the PC could cash in the Favor Point and obtain a 
Science Department related item, such as access to a Scran-
ton Reality Anchor or Kant Counter.  Different NPC roles will 
provide different types of Favor rewards.  Military personnel 
could provide powerful heavy weaponry or even an air strike 
on a certain location.  Favors completed for an MTF soldier 
could offer backup for a later combat encounter.  The Site 
Director would be a very difficult person to gain Favor with, 
but they would have access to any Favor type.  
The Favor System is entirely optional.
If you decide to include the Favor System in your 
campaign, be sure you inform your players!  
How do Favors help roleplaying? 
The Favor system is a great way to incentivize your PCs to 
interact with NPCs, which helps encourage roleplaying and 
create comradery between Characters.  Directors are not re-
quired to utilize the Favor System if they don’t feel it is neces-
sary, but it can be a great tool to further motivate players to 
speak to other NPCs in-Character as often as possible.  This 
helps your NPCs reveal their goals and desires, making them 
more memorable and interesting to your story.  If those Char-
acters end up having a gruesome demise, your players will 
feel a stronger sense of loss, having lost not only a friend, 
but also losing all the Favors they worked so hard to build up! 
Keeping track of Favors
Directors can either keep track of Favors Points in a logbook, 
or ask a player to write down Favors using the space provid-
ed.  It is important to note that Favors 
Points are shared among all players. 
One player can keep track of all Fa-
vors earned for the party, or players 
can keep track of Favor counts indi-
vidually and use Favor Points from 
other players.  It is up to the Director 
which option works best for their cam-
paign, and to what extent they want 
the Favor System to reflect a narrative 
vs mechanical utility. 
Favor Rewards
Different levels of difficulty for side-quests will provide one or 
more Favor Points.  For example, finding a lost cat may pro-
vide 1 Favor Point, while hacking into the Foundation security 
system to reclassify a D-Class personnel is far more risky and 
could grant 5+ Favor Points.  After a Favor is complete, the 
Director will determine how many Favor Points are awarded 
based on the difficulty of the quest, time taken, and execu-
tion (with an average reward of around 2 points).  Each Favor 
Point will have a type, such as Medical or Military.  If you 
gain favors from multiple NPCs of a single type they are com-
bined.  The party is limited to having 7 Favor Points for each 
type.  The example list of Favor Rewards are suggestions for 
the Director, but anything related to the Favor Type could be 
offered as well.  If a player comes up with a custom Favor 
reward, the Director will decide the number of Favor points 
required (if they deem it viable.) 
Buying Rewards
When you choose a Reward look at the Cost for the number 
of Favor Points the reward will cost.  You can then use one or 
more of the Favor Types (listed after “Type”) to obtain this re-
ward.  Directors may increase or decrease this cost depending 
on the situation and request.  The list of Favor Rewards should 
be used as an example of options.  Players and the Director 
alike can come up with Favor Rewards sporadically during 
game play.  The Director can use the list of Favor Rewards as 
a guideline, or offer the book to the players and allow them 
to choose from the list.  The Director has final say in what the 
NCP(s) are capable of providing, and how many Favor Points 
are required for any given request.  
Directors should also be made aware that most weaponry obtained 
through Favors (such as explosives) will have a significant damage 
spike when compared to standard guns or melee weapons.
Special Note:  The Favor Type DIR can be used as any type of 
Favor Point (see next page).
The Director’s Handbook
112


Favor Types 
Each Favor Type has an abbreviation code associated with it. 
Use these when documenting your Favor Types.  Directors can 
slightly alter the descriptions to better fit the campaign.
Anomalous Staff (AST)
Anomalous Staff are allied NPCs whom possess some type of para-
normal gift or practice the study of Thaumaturgy.  Characters that 
work for the Serpents Hand would commonly be considered AST. 
Anomaly (SCP)
Anomalies in containment can grant Favor Points as well (if they 
can be reasoned with).  Any non-human entity that requests favors 
would also be considered SCP-type.  
Financial (FIN)
Characters that deal with large sums of money are FIN-type.  Examples 
include Bankers, the Filthy-Rich, and Foundation Logistics employees. 
Human Resources (HR)
Human Resource NPC are those that deal with the allocation, util-
ity, and influence of several other Characters.  Foundation hiring 
staff are considered HR, but social media specialists and celebrities 
would also be branded as HR-types.
Intelligence (INT)
Intelligence agents working for the Foundation would likely be off-site, 
possibly spies working for other GoI.  Other INT-type examples in-
clude information specialists, geniuses, or even non-anomalous arti-
ficial intelligence.
Maintenance (MNT)
Blue collar workers and Janitorial Staff are considered MNT-types. 
Any employee who does physical labor for a living would be as well. 
Medical (MED)
Doctors, nurses and EMTs are considered MED-types, but so 
would magical healers and morticians.
Military (MIL)
Security Personnel are plentiful with the SCP Foundation.  Any 
Character that specializes in knowledge or use of deadly weapons 
would be considered MIL-types.
Science (SCI)
SCI-type NPCs are at the forefront of technology and ingenuity. 
Lab workers, mathematicians, physicists, and brilliant engineers 
would all be considered SCI.
Special Forces (MTF)
Mobile Task Forces are the primary force of action within the SCP 
Foundation.  Green Berets, Delta-Force, Navy SEALs, and even the 
Chaos Insurgency would be MTF-types.
Director (DIR)
Director Type Favor Points are a universal point type that can count 
as any other type of Favor point.  A few rare, powerful rewards 
require DIR type favor points.  DIR-type Favor Points can only be 
obtained by performing high-class quests from a site Director and 
high ranking officials (i.e. POTUS) all the way up to the O5 Council. 
Favor Examples
Investigate a Potentially Anomalous Occurrence
MIL Example:  A Security Guard needs the players to 
investigate a series of strange occurrences on a specific floor 
of the facility.  They suspect something anomalous is behind 
the disturbances.  The players must discover the source.
HR Example: HR has received an anonymous complaint 
about a staff member exhibiting unusual behavior.  They want 
the players to discreetly look into it.
MED Example: A patient in the medical wing claims to have 
seen something horrific during a procedure.  Medical staff ask 
the players to interview the patient and determine if there’s an 
anomalous entity involved.
INT Example: An intelligence agent believes they’ve found 
a potential location of interest related to an SCP outside of the 
facility.  They ask the players to investigate without alerting 
local authorities.
Find a Lost Person or Item
DIR Example:  The Director has lost a valuable personal 
item somewhere within the facility and asks the players to help 
locate it without mentioning specifics of the item to staff.
SCI Example: An archaeologist on staff needs the players to 
retrieve and anomalous artifact from a remote location.  The 
item is believed to be linked to a recently acquired SCP.
MED Example: Medical staff need assistance in developing a 
new amnesic formula.  The players must collect rare ingredients 
and protect the research from unauthorized access.
INT Example:  An agent has gone rogue, stolen a powerful 
SCP artifact and gone into hiding.  The players must track 
down the rogue agent and recover the dangerous artifact.
MTF Example:  After a botched field mission, an MTF 
squad member went MIA.  The Foundation has declared the 
individual as KIA, but one member of the MTF squad believes 
there’s a chance they are still alive.  The players are asked to 
search a dangerous area and rescue the squad member, or 
bring back definitive evidence of their death.
The Director’s Handbook
113


Investigate the Motives of Another Character
INT Example:  An agent believes that the Foundation has a 
spy leaking information to another GoI.  Investigate how the 
information is being leaked and apprehend the spy if applicable.
SCP Example:  An SCP entity requests and audience with the 
players, claiming to possess information that could be vital to 
the Foundation.  Is the entity to be trusted?
HR Example:  HR received a formal request from an 
employee to transfer to a different department due to personal 
safety concerns.  The players must investigate and determine 
if the request is valid.
Resolve a Lack of Resources
FIN Example: Due to a supply chain issue, a critical 
containment item is running low.  The players must find a way 
to expedite its delivery or find an alternate substitute.
SCI Example: Researchers need a rare substance for an 
experiment.  The players must venture into a dangerous SCP 
containment area to retrieve it.
FIN Example:  A shipment of anomalous items was 
accidentally sent to the wrong facility.  The players must 
coordinate with another site to correct the error.
Assist a Party with a Difficult Task
MTF Example:  A specialized MTF is assigned to capture 
a particularly elusive SCP entity, but they need additional 
backup and containment expertise from the players.
AST Example:   An anomalous staff member with unique 
abilities requests assistance in practicing and controlling their 
anomalous powers safely, and discreetly.
Social Quest
SCI Example:   The NPC would desperately like to sit down 
and have a drink with a brilliant minded person whom they 
have become infatuated with.  However this brilliant person 
currently views the NPC as bothersome.  
The players are asked to help set up a meeting or alter the 
NPCs reputation in some way.
HR Example:   The players are asked from HR to organize 
a surprise morale-boosting event to maintain a sense of 
normalcy in the midst of anomalous incidents.
SCP Example:  An SCP in containment wants access to a 
trivial item to alleviate boredom, however, the scientist in 
charge of their containment believes this item will be used as 
a method of escape.
Unethical Dilemma
MIL Example:   A Security Guard has become good friends 
with a D-Class personnel who is scheduled to help contain an 
SCP which is almost a certain death sentence.  The Security 
Guard asks the players to find a way to either break the 
D-Class out of containment, or hack Foundation records to 
change their duty or personnel class. 
MED Example:   A dispute arises between the medical staff 
and scientists over the ethics of an experiment.  The players 
must mediate and ensure that the research is scientifically 
valid and necessary, and if it is they will receive a SCI Favor 
Point instead.
AST Example:  An anomalous staff member has been strictly 
warned to refrain from using their anomalous ability or skill 
on-site.  The staff member asks the players to help them 
anomalously alter records and staff memories in order to 
allow their ability freely.
Clean Up Duty
MIL & MED Example:  During a containment breach, a 
security guard and medical staff member are trapped inside 
of a containment chamber.  The players must rescue them 
while managing the threat of the escaped SCP.
MNT Example:  After an incident that left one D-Class dead 
within a dangerous containment cell, maintenance is tasked 
with cleaning up the mess.  The players must find a way to 
keep the SCP at bay during the cleanup.
AST Example:  After a ritual went horribly wrong, a staff 
member has been hurt or altered in some anomalous way. 
The players are asked to remedy the staff member while 
keeping the botched ritual a secret from other staff.
Secure, Contain, Protect
MNT Example:  The Foundation has decided to relocate an 
SCP entity to a different containment site.  The players must 
oversee the secure transfer and ensure containment integrity.
SCI Example: A research team has accidentally created a 
new SCP entity, and they ask the players to help contain and 
study it safely.
DIR Example:  The Foundation has decided to decommission an 
SCP entity and transport it to an undisclosed location.  The players 
must oversee the execution and transportation of the entity.
MTF Example:  MTF are in charge of maintaining containment 
of a very Euclid SCP.  They were also requested to help in 
another affair, which has the potential to prevent an XK-
Class event.  The players are asked to take over a difficult 
containment procedure for the duration of one week.

The Director’s Handbook

Favor REWARDS (ITEMS)
The acquisition of the following items is more difficult and can necessitate Favor points from within your organization.  Generally, 
these items are not available during Character creation or at home base.  However, the Director may introduce them as needed 
based on specific missions or circumstances.
The Bio-Detriment Sensor is a handheld device that can detect the physical state of an entity in relation to it’s prime health 
state.  To put it simply, it detects HP.  To operate a Bio-Detriment Sensor, spend one Combat Action and roll Intellect + 
Technology against your targets Heath Rating plus the number of Units between the sensor and the target.  On a success, 
the Director will tell the PC how many HP that the Character/entity has.  On a Critical Success, additional information can 
be provided, such as changes to HP, Max HP, and elemental vulnerabilities (if any).  Directors may opt to deny any and 
all information against anomalous targets, as the device is calibrated only for standard biological lifeforms.  
Costs 2 Favors of the following type:  INT FIN SCI MTF 
Requires 1 Item Slot
Bio-Detriment Sensor  
A silent spring-loaded grappling hook that launches up to 18 meters vertically.  Roll Perception + Firearms to at-
tach hook (takes one Combat Action), difficulty determined by Director.  If roll is failed, increase difficulty by +2 for 
subsequent attempts.  You can then roll Strength + Athletics difficulty 8 to ascend, or Dexterity + Stealth difficulty 
14 to ascend silently.  Failed Ascension checks result in the hook losing it’s grip.  Ascending requires 4 consecutive 
Combat Actions (i.e. two full Combat Rounds).
Costs 3 Favor Points using the following types: FIN MNT MIL MTF 
Requires 1 Item Slot
Grapple Gun
After consuming an Emergency Boost pill, Characters gain +1 to their Move Speed temporarily, and restore 2 
Exertion at the end of every Combat Round.  This effect lasts 1 hour.  After this period the Characters body goes 
into recovery and is limited to only 1 Combat Action per Turn until they get a full night of sleep.  If a Combat Boost 
pill is taken while a Character is in recovery, the effect duration is halved, and the recovery duration is doubled.
Costs 1 Favor of the following type:  MED MIL MTF FIN 
Requires 1 Item Slot
Emergency Boost Pill

Gain access to use Retrograde Amnestics for one mission.  Retrograde Amnestics are an anomalous/chemical com-
pound which, when introduced to the bloodstream, will create a gap of recently accumulated memories.  The strength 
of the Amnestic will determine the length of time that is forgotten, as well as the difficulty to resist.  To resist an Amnes-
tic, roll Willpower + Self Control with the difficulty listed below.  If the roll succeeds, the Amnestic will only be half as 
effective, but will still cause memory loss.  If the roll is a Critical Success, the Amnestic will cause very minor memory 
loss of just a few seconds or minutes.  Class C requires hypnotic counsel from a Medical Professional, While Class 
D requires a 2-Stage Dose using an IV.  This process takes several hours and also requires a Medical Professional.

Bottle contains 12 pills.  After consuming an Amnestic Immunity pill, your Character becomes immune to anoma-
lous/chemically induced memory loss/manipulation for a period of 24 hours.  If Amnestic Immunity pills are taken 
more than two times in a 72 hour period, Character may experience vivid hallucinations and intense nightmares. 
Take with a large glass of water.  Do not exceed recommended dose.  Avoid Alcohol.  Do not crush or chew.  Call

The Director’s Handbook
118


Heartbeat Detectors are a handheld device which can detect small vibrations through photoelectric sensors and 
high-frequency sonic pulses.  The device requires one hand to operate, submits a pulse every other combat round 
(4 seconds) and displays the approximate location & distance of any nearby living beings that have a circulatory 
system.  Device may give false readings if Character used a Move Action on the same Turn as the pulse (i.e. it works 
best while standing completely still).  This device is simple to use and does not require a skill check for operation.  
Costs 3 Favor Points using the following types:  INT MED MIL SCI 
Requires 1 Item Slot
Heartbeat Detector
Any Anomalous Expert worth their weight in salt won’t leave base without their trusty Kant Counter.  Kant Counters 
have two modes:  Ambient Mode, and Precision Mode.
Ambient Mode
Ambient Mode measures the Spatial Hume levels of your current localized area (an approximate 20 meter radi-
us).  Spatial Humes (a.k.a. Ambient Humes, Baseline Reality) are a measurement of how difficult it is to alter reality 
in that location.
A reading of 100 is “Normal” for Ambient Mode.
There is no roll required to use a Kant Counter in Ambient Mode.
When reading Hume levels for an Area, remember that the further away from “100” the Hume levels are, the more abnormal 
the reading.  Higher Hume levels than 100 represent stronger reality in the area, while lower Hume levels than 100 represent 
a weaker reality level.  Natural Spatial Hume levels on Earth range from low the 60’s to high 140’s.  Readings outside of 
this range should be met with caution.  
Precision Mode
Cognizant of Hume levels, Precision Mode gauges the emanations from a designated person or object.  Should 
an individual or object exhibit a Hume level proximate to the Spatial Hume levels in a given area, they possess the 
capability to influence reality within that spatial domain.
A reading of 1 (one) is “Normal” for Precision Mode.
To use Precision Mode, Characters must roll Intellect + Anomalistics.  The Director will keep the difficulty secret (with an 
average difficulty of 14).  If the roll is a success, the Hume levels of the object or being are revealed.  If they are failed a false 
reading could occur (Director’s discretion).
While using Precision Mode, you are reading the Hume levels of a specific person or object.  The target in question must 
be within 5 meters of the Kant Counter in order to get a proper reading.  When reading Hume levels for a being, typical 
readings will be between 1 and 10, while nearly all objects will return a reading of one (1).  SCP Objects will most likely 
give off readings much higher than one.  When an Object or persons Hume levels exceed that of their local Spatial Hume 
levels, the object or persons often can alter reality to their Will in some way.  Type Green Reality Benders read Hume levels 
approaching 80 and 100, while more powerful Reality Benders can have Humes exceeding 120+.
See also Low and High Hume Environments on page 79, or the Reality Benders and Thaumatology Chapter on page 127.
Costs 2 Favors of the following type:  INT FIN SCI  
Requires 1 Item Slot
Kant Counter 
Bottle contains 12 pills.  After consuming Memetic Immunity, your Character becomes immune to anomalous 
Memetic Hazards, including Memetic Kill Agents.  Effects last 24 hours.  Reported side effects include dizziness, 
prophetic night-terrors, dry mouth, and missing time.  Take with food.  Do not exceed recommended dose.  Avoid 
prolonged mental activity.  Avoid using as directed.  May cause aixelsyd.
Costs 2 Favor Points using the following types:  MED SCI MTF
Requires 1 Item Slot
Memetic Immunity

Tracking Device comes with three tracking bugs.  A tracking bug is a small item (recorded on back of sheet) about the

can be detected using the Tracking Device.  When activated, the tracking bugs will provide GPS coordinates to the 
tracking device in real-time.  This device is simple to use and does not require a skill check for operation.

A higher quality First Aid package.  Performing First Aid with a Trauma Kit requires 4 consecutive Combat Actions. 
Roll Intellect + Medical.  Your Character will recover an amount of HP equal to the result of this roll times two.  If 
used on self, do not double the result.
Costs 1 Favor of the following types:  FIN MED MIL MTF
Requires 1 Item Slot
Trauma Kit   
Scranton Reality Anchors (SRAs) artificially increase Spatial Hume levels in proximity to the anchor.  The SCP Foun-
dation has a finite number of reality anchors and manufacturing new ones is a very difficult process that requires 
time and resources.  Each anchor inflates the surrounding Spatial Hume levels by a set value, which inhibits anom-
alous activity to varying degrees.  Anchors can reduce the level or range they output, however they cannot re-
duce base reality Humes.  The Favor cost varies by the strength, duration, and output of each anchor (see below). 
After the duration of a reality anchor expires, it must be recharged using special housing at a Foundation site. 
You can extend the duration of a reality anchor by reducing its output.  
For every 10 Humes or 10 Meters below its maximum output, increase the duration of the anchor by 6 hours.  
For example, the SRA-40 could output +30 Humes within 50 Meters for 24 hours.
No dice rolls are necessary to operate a Scranton Reality Anchor for use with normal operation.
See also Low and High Hume Environments on page 79.

The Phosphorus Grenade, a destructive and morally questionable military explosive, triggers an explosion of chem-
ical fire that proves nearly impossible to extinguish.  After pin removal, the grenade detonates at the end of the 
following Combat Round, enveloping everything within a 6-Unit radius in a hellish inferno.  All Characters within or 
passing through these Units immediately gain a Fire Intensity level of 5 (refer to page 66).  Those within the fire 
zone remain at Intensity Level 5 indefinitely.  While this type of Fire Intensity never increases, it only diminishes by 
one at the beginning of each Combat Round after the Character has escaped the fire zone.  Characters ignited by the 
Phosphorus Grenade cannot reduce their Fire Intensity by going prone and continue to suffer the fire damage until 
their Intensity reaches zero.  The fiery zone persists for 12 Combat Rounds.  One-time-use item.  (Damage Type: Fire)

nation, an anomalous surge of chaotic reality warping quantum energy will erupt and affect the surrounding 8 Unit

Reality Warping Grenade
D20 roll
Outcome
Effect
1
Sentience Alteration
Random objects manifest facial features and become sentient, having personalities, 
goals, memories, and speak the same language as the players. 
2
Location Alteration
Randomize the locations of all objects, structures, and Characters.
3
Identity Alteration
Characters swap minds with other random Characters (control of the Character).
4
Size Alteration
Characters and objects randomly grow or shrink in size constantly.
5
History Alteration
Time briefly rewinds, causing deja vu for all those affected.
6
Planar Alteration
A rift in space causes Characters to travel through a series of random dimensions.
7
Meta Alteration
All Characters temporarily realize they are inside of a tabletop roleplaying game.
8
Vision Alteration
Characters are suddenly able to see colors, objects, spirits, and other things not meant to 
be seen by human eyes.  May cause an X-Ray like effect or a complete sanity meltdown.
9
Spatial Alteration
The third dimension begins to shift between other levels, both positive and negative. 
Characters may experience a two dimensional plane of existence, or be able to teleport 
or chase each other through time.
10
Gravity Alteration
Increase or decrease the levels of Gravity randomly (see page 77).
11
Light Alteration
Colors begin to invert, and the speed of light is slowed, causing extreme disorientation 
to all Characters.
12
Sonic Alteration
Characters are able to hear sounds of things several thousand miles away, hear heart-
beats, and the vibrations of insects.  This does not harm hearing, though it will confuse 
and bewilder everyone who experiences it.
13
Mind Alteration
Characters within the zone are able to hear each others thoughts, and temporarily be-
come similar to a hive-mind.
14
Power Alteration
All Characters within the zone temporarily become Pluripotent entities (a.k.a.Gods). 
Each get infinite HP, and have nearly any super-power they can imagine, but cannot 
prolong their power surge, manipulate time, or give themselves permanent bonuses.
15
Campaign Alteration A rift in space opens up into a Dimension of one of the Director’s previous ttrpg campaigns.
16
Spiritual Alteration
Spirits of the dead, and religious/mythical entities begin to surround the area.
17
Time Alteration
Characters begin to experience time at random rates.  Some Characters freeze in time, 
while others experience a feeling of “slow motion.”
18
Multiverse Alteration
Characters are transported into an adjacent multiverse, where minor differences are 
commonplace.
19
Death Alteration
All Characters that died in the area within the past 50 years return from the dead.
20
Life Alteration
Everyone in the zone dies (but return to life after zone expires).  Director may incorporate 
afterlife scenarios depending on campaign canon.

The mushroom cloud forms the image of a giant skull.
After the pin is removed, the Wondertainment Grenade will detonate after 1D4 Combat Rounds have ended.  Upon 
detonation, a random effect will occur.  The Director rolls 1D20 on the below chart to determine the outcome.
Heavy Director discretion is necessary.  One-time-use item.  
The Director’s Handbook
126


Reality Bending
In order to attempt Reality Bending, a Character needs the 
Reality Bending (FTE) skill.  The only way to obtain this Writ-
ten In skill is through the Type Green Aspect on page 53.
Reality Bending is a powerful and dangerous anomalous 
ability that few posses through naturally high Hume Levels. 
Directors should only allow the Type Green Aspect to be pur-
chased if they are very comfortable with running a game.  Re-
ality Bending requires extra work from the Director to ensure 
that the effects are fair (both in-universe and mechanically) 
well executed, and requires PCs to be understanding and 
compensating when their intended effect is altered to accom-
modate system mechanics and fairness.  Players considering 
the Type Green Aspect should be prepared to collaborate 
closely with the Director and maintain flexibility when their 
intended effects are adjusted to ensure fairness and uphold 
game balance.  Reality Bending may not suit players who fre-
quently engage in contentious discussions regarding realistic 
outcomes or game mechanics, as this could disrupt the flow 
of gameplay and compromise the overall experience for ev-
eryone involved.  Reality Bending and Thaumatology are not 
suggested to be used unless the Director feels confident they 
have a deep understanding of the SCP.2e game mechanics. 
Players are encouraged to avoid Reality Bending and Thau-
matology if they have not played SCP.2e previously or are not 
familiar with Advanced Character Creation.
Characters that can bend reality can literally attempt 
anything.  Fly, turn into a dinosaur, shoot fireballs, turn 
invisible, create an army of rabbits... anything.  The only 
limitation is the Characters Hume Levels and the difficulty of 
the task.  Simple Reality Bending, like unlocking a door or 
causing a spark would have a much more consistent and safe 
effect than summoning SCP-2662 or attempting to crash the 
Moon into the Earth.  Characters that attempt huge effects 
and fail will feel the effects of reality backlash, which will 
permanently reduce their Hume Levels as reality itself tries to 
conform the offender.  
The difficulty to perform Reality Bending is based on several 
factors.  Namely Spatial Humes, Theme, and the difficulty of 
the effect.  None of these factors are exposed to the players, 
so Reality Benders will need to get a feel for the potential of 
their abilities and know the risks of attempting big effects.  
After a Bending of Reality effect has been declared, the 
player rolls Intellect + Reality Bending and the result (score) 
is established first, before any Difficulty is given or even 
determined by the Director.  Characters may spend one or 
more Exertion to add 1D12 to their dice pools as normal (see 
page 59) however if zero Exertion is used, then Reality 
Bending is treated as a Punished Skill Check.
Reality Dice determine the difficulty for the reality bending 
attempt.  The difficulty for Reality Bending is equal to the total 
of all Reality Dice added together.  Reality Dice do not ex-
plode.  After the player determines their score, the Director 
will gather Reality Dice and shake them up (rolling a few dice 
and adding them together slowly to build tension if the Director 
chooses) keeping the total number of dice hidden until all have 
been rolled.   
• 
If the Reality Dice total is equal to or less than the players 
Intellect + Reality Bending roll, then Reality Bending 
succeeds and the Director narrates the outcome.  
• 
If the total of all Reality Dice exceeds the score (but not 
double) the player has the option of spending 2 Exertion 
to remove one Reality Die from the pool.  This may be 
repeated multiple times to meet the difficulty.   If the diffi-
culty cannot be made, the effect fizzles and the PC loses 
1 Exertion.  
• 
If the total of all the Reality Dice doubles the score rolled 
by the player, a Critical Failure occurs, and the effect 
causes “Reality Anchoring.”  This will reduce the Charac-
ters Hume levels by the total number of Reality Dice that 
were rolled.  Exertion can not be used to remove Reality 
Dice after a Critical Failure has been determined.
To determine what Reality Dice to roll, the Director determines 
three factors.  Spatial Humes, Theme, and Difficulty.
This section will cover the rules for when reality is altered in an anomalous way by humans who possess abilities or knowledge that allow 
for seemingly paranormal energy creation and manipulation.  The Director is encouraged to limit or restrict excessive use of these abilities.
Chapter Nine:  
Reality Bending & Thaumatology 
127
Reality Bending & Thaumatology


Spatial Humes
The Director will determine (but not announce) the Spatial 
Humes in the area.  This is typically a number between 90 
and 110 and hovers close to 100.  For more information on 
Spatial Hume Levels see page 79.
The Hume Level for the PC is then checked against the Spatial 
Hume Levels.  The Director will take the players Hume Level, 
and subtract the areas Spatial Humes.  After this number is 
determined, round down to the nearest tenth.  
For example, if the player had a Hume level of 89 and the 
Spatial Humes were 118, you would get -29.  This would be 
rounded down to just -20.  Like wise if the player had a Hume 
level of 115 and the Spatial Hume Levels were 83, the result 
would be 32 rounded down to 30.
Use the below chart to determine the first Reality Dice.

*Remove one die
*Remove one die means that after the Director determines 
any additional Reality Dice, they will remove one of the high-
est die types from the pool.  For example, if later on the Di-
rector adds 2D8, 1D10, and 2D12 Reality Dice later on, they 
would remove one of the D12’s from the pool.
Theme
Most Reality Benders have a theme, element, or specialty.  If 
the reality being bent does not perfectly match up with the 
theme the player chose, the Theme die type is increased.  
Theme Matching
Reality Dice
Opposite of Theme
3D10
Not Related to Theme
2D10
Somewhat Off Theme
1D12
Vaguely Matches Theme
1D10
Tangentially Matches Theme
1D8
Closely Matches Theme
1D6
Perfectly Matches Theme
1D4
The Director will determine if the effect matches the Theme of 
the Reality Benders Specialty, but not tell the player to what 
degree it matches.  
Lets say a player has chosen to be a Reality Bender with 
a Fire Theme.  This is a great Theme to choose because it is 
very succinct.  This will allow the Theme to be more clearly 
matched with any given desired effect.  Throwing a fireball 
is on theme, reversing gravity is not, and making it snow is 
the opposite.  When Reality Bending Themes are chosen, the 
more specific that Theme is, the better it is for both the Player 
and Director.  It will help the Director quickly determine how 
well the action matches the Theme, and it will make it easier 
for Players to “Perfectly Match” the Theme they desire.  Gen-
eralized, or non-specific Themes will be nearly impossible to 
“Perfectly Match” and therefore will on average be more dif-
ficult to perform.   Avoid Themes such as “Creates Anomalies” 
“Elemental Control” or “Causing Destruction.”  Some good 
choices could include “Electricity” “Decay & Poison” “Sum-
moning Portals” “Pocket Dimensions” or “Digital Glitch.” 
Conversely, a Reality Bender can choose to have “No Theme” 
which will make all Theme Matching Reality Dice default to 
1D12 but never any more or less.
Additionally, very powerful Themes such as “Time Travel” 
“Summoning Gods” or “Superman Skillz” should all be scru-
tinized by the Director as well, forcing the maximum Theme 
Match to be vague at best.
Difficulty (Scope and Complexity)
Finally, the Director will Determine how many additional Re-
ality Bending Dice they should add.  Basic tasks that don’t 
effect the game much should add zero dice, while complex 
tasks that alter the game world, kill characters, or are too 
powerful or overused could have five or more dice added. 
Directors should look at the SCOPE of the reality being bent 
when deciding on how many additional Reality Dice to add. 
Use the following example chart as a guideline.

Next Determine how COMPLEX and “broken” the action 
would be to resolve.  If the effect requires the Director to 
quickly come up with some damage dice or allow the players 
to move through a wall, the effect is pretty simple in terms of 
mechanics.  If the effect requires the Director to create stats for 
a whole new Character, a ruleset for a new ability, or in some 
way will simply break your game with how powerful the effect 
is, then the effect is complex and broken.  Changing the color 
of your shirt would be D4.  Dealing roughly twenty damage 
would be D8, and summoning a personal T-Rex would be 
D12.  Finally combine step 1 and 2 to determine how many 
dice, and what type are added to the Reality Bending die 
pool.  But don’t stop there!  The Director can judge the sit-
uation even further and replace these dice individually with 
higher or lower types.  Maybe the player keeps attempting 
to perform the same effect over and over.  The Director could 
increase one of the Reality Dice by one type for each addi-
tional attempt.   Directors can manipulate the Reality Die pool 
however they see fit, but keep in mind the guidelines thus far 
to help gauge an accurate and fair difficulty.

Reality Bending Example
Ryan is a Reality Bender.  His Hume Level is 82.  When he 
purchased Type Green he chose the theme “Haunted Hallow-
een.”  Ryan is attempting to create a cloud of bats to distract 
some enemies.  Ryan rolls Intellect + Reality Bending.  He de-
cides to spend 1 Exertion so that the roll is not a Punished Skill 
Check.  Ryan has 3D8 in Intellect and a Reality Bending skill 
of 2.  Spending the point of Exertion means he rolls 3D8 and 
1D12.  He does, and after a few exploding dice his top two 
dice end up being a 9 and 10.  19 plus his skill gives him a 
score of 21.
Next, the Director checks Humes, Theme, Scope, and Com-
plexity.  The Director decides the areas Spatial Humes are 
97.   -14 rounded down is -10, which equates to 1D12 Real-
ity Die on the Hume chart.  The Director sees this action Per-
fectly matches a Halloween Theme, so he adds 1D4 Reality 
Die.  Next the Director feels that manifesting bats to cause 
a distraction would be a Minor ability with lingering effects, 
and notes he will add 3D?.  Thinking about how complex this 
will be in the game, the Director thinks that he will come up 
with some sort of dice roll to allow the enemies to push past, 
and maybe a bit of damage may be caused by hostile bats. 
So, “somewhat complex.”  The Director gathers all Reality 
Dice together.  1D12, 1D4, and 3D8.  The dice are rolled: 
2, 3, 4, 8, and 8.  Adding these all together is 25, meaning 
that the Reality Bending attempt fails.  Ryan has two extra Ex-
ertion left.  He decides to spend 2 Exertion to remove one of 
the 8’s rolled.  This makes the Reality Bending Difficulty only 
17, which turns the attempt into a Success.  The Director says 
that a flurry of bats distract the enemy and Ryan gets away.
129
Reality Bending & Thaumatology


Thaumatology 
The Foundation usually does not encourage the use of Thau-
maturgy to maintain the containment of anomalies, neither 
does it forbid Thaumaturgy experimentations or controlled 
usage by experienced staff members.  Thaumaturgy is com-
mon practice for members of The Serpents Hand, Are We 
Cool Yet, The Children of the Scarlet King, students of Deer 
College, and many other Groups of Interest.   
Thaumatology is the study of rituals, actions, and tech-
niques which, when performed correctly, will cause anom-
alous effects to occur without any known scientific reason 
or cause.  Thaumaturgy is the act of performing said ritu-
als, and Thaumaturgist refers to an individual that practices 
Thaumaturgic rituals.
The study of Anomalistics slowly bleeds into the study of Thau-
matology after enough time.  The Thaumatology skill is special 
in that it cannot have its own Skill Multiplier, instead it copies 
the Multiplier of Anomalistics.  For example, if your Anomalistics 
skill was 1 (x3) and you had Thaumatology at level 4, you would 
add 4x3 (12) to Thaumatology skill checks.  
Obtaining new Thaumaturgy rituals and techniques requires 
Characters to find or own a Grimoire.  (See below)
Learning Thaumatology
Aside from purchasing the Type Blue Aspect on page 53, 
Characters whom have an Anomalistics skill of 4 or more are 
capable of learning Thaumatology by finding a teacher.  The 
Teacher must have a Thaumatology and Teaching Skill of 4 
or greater.  If the student and Teacher perform a Dual Tech 
skill check of Intellect plus Anomalistics difficulty 27, then the 
student obtains the Thaumatology skill at level 1.  The teaching 
process requires about thirty hours to complete.
Note that anomalous effects of Thaumaturgy are manifested 
via Akiva Radiation and Elan-Vital Energy and thus are un-
affected by local Hume levels and Reality Anchors.  Directors 
may stifle or dampen the effects of Thaumaturgy in abnor-
mally high Spatial Hume environments at their own discretion.
Grimoire 
(a.k.a. Anomalous Codex, Spellbook)
Thaumaturgy requires the use of ancient text (or sometimes 
extensive documentation of newly discovered techniques) and 
sometimes ritual components.
A Thaumaturgist that possesses a Grimoire is able to create 
any effect that is contained within the pages of the book. 
Grimoire’s will have a “Knowledge Index” and a “Theme” 
(similar to that used in the Reality Bending system).  Themes are 
short descriptions of the context of the book.  Examples 
include “Tome of Fire”  “Grimoire of the Spirit Realm”  or 
“Book of the Dead.”  When a Thaumaturgist would like to 
manifest an effect, the closer that effect meets the Theme of 
their Grimoire, the easier it will be to find the effect within the 
pages.  Grimoire’s also have a Knowledge Index (KI).  With 
this rating, the lower the number the better.  A typical Gri-
moire would have a Knowledge Index of around 10, a badly 
burnt Tome may have a Knowledge Index of 18 or more, 
while a crisp Ancient Spell Book from the Wanderers Library 
could have a Knowledge Index of zero (or even lower!).
Finding the ritual
A Thaumaturgist can request to the Director an effect that 
might be documented within the pages of their Grimoire. 
They roll a punishing skill check of Intellect + Thaumatology 
with the difficulty to find the ritual listed in the first chart. 
The Director keeps this difficulty unknown by the player. 
If the roll is resolved and failed, it can never be attempted 
again (the ritual is not in the book.)  If the effect is found within 
the pages, then that effect will be available to the PC from 
that point forward.  Each time a Grimoire is used this way, re-
gardless of success or failure, it will increase the Grimoire’s 
Knowledge Index based on how well the ritual matches its 
theme (see KI increase on the first chart).
Creating the ritual
The number of points by which the player exceeds the ritual’s 
Theme Difficulty becomes their available Preparation Points for 
this ritual.  For example, if the difficulty was 12, and the play-
er scored an 18, they would have 6 Preparation Points to craft 
the ritual.  Preparation points can then be divided among the 
Difficulty, Time to Cast, and Time to Gather Supplies by 
the player however they want, to determine the final require-
ments to execute the effect.  
Note that the Time to Gather Supplies time must be spent per ritual, 
and must be gathered within two days of the ritual itself.
Performing the ritual
After the Supplies are gathered and the Time to Cast has 
elapsed, the Thaumaturgist rolls Intellect + Thaumatology. 
Then the Director will roll the Difficulty to see if the Thaumat-
urgist succeeded.  This Difficulty is to be noted for future ref-
erence in case the spell is attempted again in the future, but 
goes up by (KI increase) for each additional time the ritual is 
performed later on. If the Skill Check succeeds, the effect re-
quested occurs. If a Critical Success, the effect will occur with 
twice the intended outcome.  If the Skill Check is a Critical 
Failure, Akiva Backlash occurs (See page 132).   
The ritual can then be completed over and over by the player 
in the future, by spending the necessary time to gather supplies 
and perform the ritual, using the same difficulty as last time but 
adding (KI increase) for each additional ritual attempt.
Note that because the ritual difficulty is rolled second, the player may 
not spend Reverence to reroll after the difficulty has been determined.
130
Reality Bending & Thaumatology


Tip: Staying on theme allows rituals to be cast more often and 
with less risk. Off-theme rituals increase your Grimoire’s decay 
and raise difficulty faster.

NOTE:
Both the Grimoire’s Knowledge Index and the difficulty 
to perform a ritual increase according to the ‘KI increase’ 
die listed on the Theme Matching chart.  The Grimoire’s KI 
increases each time a new ritual is searched for, while the 
difficulty to perform a known ritual increases after each 
attempt, regardless of whether the ritual succeeds or fails.
Dual/Triple Tech Thaumaturgy
A Thaumatology Dual Tech can be performed only if both the 
Characters Thaumatology skills added together is 4 or great-
er.  A Triple Tech may be performed only if when all three 
Characters Thaumatology skills are added together come out 
to 6 or greater.  (For example, one Character with a Thaumatol-
ogy skill of 4, another with a skill of 2, and the third Character has 
zero would add up to 6, allowing a Triple Tech.)
131

1D20
Akiva Backlash
1
Dimensional breach: A portal opens, allowing 
extradimensional entities to enter the area.
2
Reality decay: Objects or structures 
deteriorate rapidly or age unnaturally.
3

Thaumatology.  Characters Thaumatology

6
Hallucinations: Characters see horrors for

8

9

and have vivid nightmares.
10

12
Cats:  Characters become house cats for 
3D10 minutes.  (House cats cannot speak)
13
Death:  Characters must make a Death Save 
difficulty 16, or die.
14
Inferno:  Everything within 20 Units of 
the casting location is engulfed in flames.   
Characters gain a Fire Intensity level of 5.  
Fire intensity cannot be lowered until the 
Character leaves the area. 
15
Vengeful Shadows:  2D20 flying shadow

deal 3D8 damage to anything they pass 
through and cannot be killed.  Creatures

1D20

16

(determined randomly).
17

Thaumaturgy Example
Nelly has Thaumatology at level 2.  Her Anomalistics is 2 (x3). 
Nelly has a Grimoire titled “The Tome of Forbidden Knowl-
edge.”  This Grimoire specializes in obtaining knowledge and 
has a Knowledge Index of 14.  Nelly asks the Director if she 
can attempt to find a ritual that will tell her the identity of a 
spy working for the Global Occult Coalition.  The Director de-
cides that this request “Perfectly” matches the theme of the 
Grimoire.  This means that the difficulty to locate such a ritual 
is 14.  Nelly rolls her Intellect + Thaumatology (which gets to 
use her Anomalistics Multiplier) and scores a 21.  This means 
Nelly finds the ritual within the pages of her Grimoire and has 
7 Preparation Points to distribute among the Time Required to 
Gather Supplies, Time to Cast, and Difficulty.
She decides to assign 2 points to Supplies, 2 points to Casting 
Time, and 3 points to Difficulty.  
This means it would take Nelly 2 weeks to gather supplies, 1 
hour to cast, and the difficulty would be 4D12.  Her Character 
spends 2 weeks gathering supplies and then sets up the ritual. 
The ritual itself takes 1 hour.  She performs the ritual and rolls 
her Intellect dice of 4D10 and gets 1, 7, 8, & 10.  She had one 
D10 explode.  She rolls 1D12 and gets an 11.  Her top two 
dice are 10 and 11.  She then adds her Thaumatology skill mul-
tiplied by her Anomalistics Multiplier (2x3).  Her final result is 
27.  Now the Director rolls 4D12 to determine the difficulty.  He 
rolls a 5,9,2, and 9.  This sets the difficulty to perform the ritual 
to 25.  Nelly succeeds!  The ritual reveals the identity of the spy 
to her through mystical smoke as described by the Director. 
She rolls 1D4 (KI Increase) and gets a 1.  The Tome now has a 
Knowledge Index of 15, but Nelly may repeat this ritual in the 
future by spending 2 weeks to gather supplies, taking 15 min-
utes to cast, and rolling a Thaumatology check at difficulty 26. 
(The ritual perfectly matched the theme, and therefore gets a +1 to the 
difficulty for each additional time it’s performed.)    
 
Akiva Backlash
When a Thaumaturgy effect results in a Critical Failure, Akiva 
Radiation causes a negative reaction to the intended effect. 
This can result in anything the Director feels appropriate, but 
if you would like to choose from or roll on the chart (next page) 
it lists several good examples of possible backlash effects.  If 
Dual or Triple Tech was involved, choose an effect for each 
involved Thaumaturgist.
132
Reality Bending & Thaumatology


133
Reality Bending & Thaumatology


The Stat Block
Instead of using up a whole character sheet every time you 
need stats for an NPC, you can use a quick process to gener-
ate the stats you require, and then confine those stats in to a 
very small “Stat Block” as seen below.
First let’s show how these stats translate into a full Character.
A:  Character Name is simply the name or description of 
the NPC/SCP.
B: Title is a one word description of the Character, such as 
“Medic” “Soldier” or “Bodybuilder.”  Title is used to give the 
Director an idea of the Character at a glance.  For SCP stats, 
this section may be replaced with the SCPs Containment Class.
C:  Instead of listing all skills for NPC/SCPs you can shorten it 
to the three primary skill types:  Physical, Mental, and Social. 
These ratings are added to the appropriate Attribute dice roll 
instead of individual skills (NPC and SCP skills do not use multipliers).
D: Attributes are not shown as dice icons but instead their At-
tribute Ratings.  Unlike PCs, NPC and SCP stats are forced to 
buy dice from left to right, bottom to top.  This means that the 
ratings of each attribute directly relate to the below chart.
For example, an Attribute with a rating of 10 could be imag-
ined to look like this:
...and therefore would roll 2D10 and 2D8 for that Attribute. 
Directors can even have stats that go beyond 16 for more 
powerful Characters.  A rating of 17 would represent 4D12 & 
1D6, 18 would be 4D12 & 1D8, and so on.  
E: S, C, and P are shorthand for Speed, Combat Rating, and 
Projectile Rating.  Speed represents how many Units the Char-
acter can move per Combat Action, while the other two are 
damage multipliers for Melee and Gun attacks.
F: Keep track of the Character’s Exertion points here.
G: Keep track of the Character’s HP here.

H: Here you will list the Character’s attack options in com-
bat.  Because NPC stats do not fluctuate like PC stats, 
several of the stats can be pre-calculated to speed 
up the Director’s combat.  Instead of listing To-Hit for 
the weapon stats, the “To-Hit” column is the weapons To-Hit 
modifier combined with the Character’s Physical stat.  For ex-
ample, “DEX+2” would mean to roll Dexterity Dice and add 
2 to the final score.  Additionally instead of listing the Damage 
and X Damage for weapons, the damage is pre-calculated. 
For example, if the NPC had a Combat Rating of x2, and 
were using a weapon that dealt 1D8 Base Damage and 1D6 
X Damage, the damage column will simply list “1D8+2D6.” 
The Notes section can be used to list weapon types, and also 
how many actions it takes the NPC to draw, or activate the 
weapon.
I: The Special section here will be any special rules associat-
ed with the Character, such as damage reduction, immunities, 
and anomalous abilities.  For complex SCPs the Special Sec-
tion should be expanded as needed.
Creating NPC and SCP stat blocks
Now that you understand how to use the Stat Block, let’s 
learn how to make stats for them that will be as dangerous to 
the players as you deem.
Choose a Threat Level
NPCs and SCPs are assigned a Threat Level, which provide 
an estimate of their strength.  Add up the total ‘Chapters 
Survived’ scores of all PCs to determine their collective PC 
Threat Level.  For “Moderate” difficulty encounters, Directors 
can match the PCs against an NPC/SCP with a Threat Level 
equal to the total PCs Threat Level.  If multiple enemies are 
involved, their combined Threat Levels should match the PCs’ 
Threat Level.  Adjusting the battle’s difficulty can be achieved 
by raising or decreasing the Threat Level of the opponents. 
SCPs (or “Boss Characters”) would typically have a Threat 
Level twice that of the PCs.  For instance, if your PC party’s 
Threat Level is 5, the ‘Final Boss’ would have a Threat Level of 
10.  It’s generally not recommended to pit the players against 
an encounter with a Threat Level much more than twice that 
of the PCs, unless the scenario calls for a tactical retreat or if 
the Director believes victory should come at the cost of some 
PC casualties.  SCP Characters are allocated more Character 
Points per Threat Level, so keep this fact in mind as well.  
Note that most non-humanoid SCPs should be immune to Mutila-
tions, and even humanoid SCPs may include this trait.
Directors may also ignore Threat Levels entirely to make each 
encounter realistic, chaotic and unpredictable.
Choose a Creation Method
Now that you have an idea of what Threat Level your NPC 
should be, you can either choose from the list of premade NPC 
stats (found on page 137) or you can create your own stats.
Spend Character Points
Character Points are determined by the Threat Level you 
choose.  These points are spent into four things:  Attribute 
Ranks, Skills, Weapons, and Special Characteristics.
Attribute Ranks are straightforward.  Add up the Attribute 
Ranks for all your Attributes and that is how much it costs in 
Character Points.
Skills cost 3 points per rank, so a Physical rank of 3 would cost 
9 total Character Points.  The Physical Skill shouldn’t exceed 
any of the Character’s Physical Attribute Ratings, Mental 
shouldn’t exceed the Character’s Intellect Rating, and Social 
shouldn’t exceed their Charisma Rating.  (These Skill Caps are 
more of a suggestion than a hard rule, you’re the Director you can 
do whatever you want.)
Provide Gear
Give your NPC a Uniform and weapons.  Keep in mind that 
higher Mass weapons and damage reduction will increase 
the power level of your NPC.  Typically you won’t need to 
give an SCP gear.  After you decide on any weapons you can 
begin to fill in their stats on the character sheet.  Remember to 
pre-calculate To-Hit rolls and Damage based on your NPCs 
Physical stat and multipliers.
Special Characteristics
Next, there are Special Characteristics.  If your NPC has im-
munities, damage reduction or special attacks the Director 
can have these cost Character Points as well.  SCPs will spend 
Character Points to buy anomalous abilities.  See page 138 
for a list of options, but feel free to invent your own on the 
fly for each Character.  Typically it’s best for Directors to use 
premade anomalous abilities as a guideline to help estimate 
the level of power for their own invented anomalous abilities. 
Directors may break any rules throughout this book and can 
stat NPCs and SCPs off the cuff if they feel capable.  This is 
especially true when creating unique SCPs, as each one has 
unique powers and properties impossible to itemize in a sin-
gle list.  NPC and SCP Characters can also spend Character 
Points as if they are “Power” points and purchase Aspects just 
as your players do.  
 
Background and Personality (NPCs)
There are four parameters to consider for the background and 
personality for your NPCs.  Use the “OGAS” system created 
by Guy Sclanders from the “How to be a Great GM” youtube 
channel.  OGAS stands for Occupation, Goal, Attitude and 
Stake.  Decide on what your NPC does for a living, what they 
want out of life, their attitude about obtaining this goal, and 
what is at stake if they don’t achieve it.  Answering these four 
questions will allow you to roleplay your NPCs appropriately 
in any given situation by keeping a mental persona based off 
of the answers.  For more information check out:
tinyurl.com/HTBAGGMOGAS
 
135
NPC’s & SCP’s


Calculate Substats
You can then calculate the remaining stats as follows:
(S) Speed = Dex Rating divided by 3 (rounded up) +2
(C) Combat = Str Rating divided by 3 (rounded up)
(P) Projectile =  Per Rating divided by 3 (rounded up)
Exertion = Wil Rating divided by 3 (rounded up) +1
HP = Hth Rating plus 15, times (*1-5)
*The Director should estimate a value between 1 and 5 when de-
termining an NPCs/SCPs Hit Points.  For every 20 Character Points 
over 40, increase this multiplier by 1.
Character Building Considerations
As a Director your role includes fine-tuning stats to match the 
desired level of challenge.  The system for creating stats for 
SCPs and NPCs has its limitations.  When allocating points in 
specific areas to create exceptionally strong or weak Char-
acters you may encounter a balance offset.  Similarly, if your 
players excel in combat, you might find the need to adjust the 
power levels of your NPCs accordingly.  It’s important to un-
derstand that this system serves as a flexible guideline rather 
than a strict rule for crafting perfect combat encounters.  Con-
sider it as a tool to provide a solid starting point, but feel free 
to adjust details to suit your game’s needs. 
NPC Fate Score
Another note of consideration is your NPCs Fate Score.  NPCs 
with a low Fate Score can often get killed instantly if Stag-
gered.  If you would like your NPC to only be defeated af-
ter their HP reaches zero, give them a moderate to high Fate 
score rating.  SCPs are often immune to Mutilation Effects, 
and therefore may not require this consideration.
SCP and NPC Reaction and Stagger scores
Instead of including Reaction and Stagger on the stat block 
itself, Directors can quickly calculate these ratings by add-
ing DEX + Physical (Reaction score) or FTE  (Stagger score). 
Note NPCs using this creation method do not add a skill to their 
Stagger score.  
SCP and NPC unarmed damage
Instead of using the chart on page 65, SCPs and NPCs 
Melee damage is equal to all of their Strength Dice added 
together.  This will simplify your damage output.
NPC Stat Types
There are eight different archetypes on the opposite page, 
sorted by Threat Levels.  Directors can quickly obtain stats 
for any given NPC by deciding an archetype, choosing a 
Threat Level, supplying the Character with an appropriate 
Uniform, Weapons, Gear, and finally coming up with a 
brief background.

NPC’s & SCP’s


Example SCP Abilities
The following is a short list of example abilities you can give 
your SCPs which are not listed in Aspects (and therefore not 
available to the PCs).   The Cost listed is how many Character 
Points the ability requires.  Directors are encouraged to create 
their own abilities unique to the SCP they are creating, using 
the list below as a guide on how to gauge Power point costs.
Made of Shadows	
	
Cost: 20
The Character has no physical form.  It is immune to all non-
anomalous damage except for Fire.  Character is immune to 
Mutilations.
Liquid Form	 	
	
Cost: 10
The Character can spend a Combat Action to transform into 
a puddle of liquid.  While in this form it is immune to attacks 
except from the Ice Damage Type, but it can not attack while 
in this form.
Flight	 	
	
	
Cost: 12
The Character can fly.  Move speed gets a +5 when flying at 
a downward slope.  Projectile attacks against the Character 
while flying further than 3 Units away get -5 to hit.
Enhanced Melee Damage	
Cost: 5
When the SCP attacks, increase the damage by +1D10.  
Enhanced Melee Skill	
Cost: 9
When the NPC attacks, increase the To-Hit roll by +3.  
(Unarmed Melee Attacks roll Dexterity + Physical for To-Hit rolls)
Telekinesis	
	
	
Cost: 8
The Character can manipulate objects within 5 Units of 
themselves with their mind.  The Mass of the object must be 
equal to or less than the Characters WIL Rating.
Hard to Destroy	
	
Cost: 15
Character is immune to damage caused by Cutting or 
Piercing Damage Types, and damage caused by other 
sources is reduced by 20.  
Teleportation	
	
Cost: 6
The Character can vanish in a puff of smoke and appear in 
another location.  This action requires 2 consecutive Combat 
Actions.  Option to spend Exertion:  Reduce the number of 
Combat Actions required for every 1 Exertion spent.
Deathtouch	 	
	
Cost: 30
Choose an attack.  When that attack is successfully deals 
damage, the Target must roll Fate + Resist Death difficulty 16 
or they die instantly.
Summon Minion	
	
Cost: 15
By spending 1 Combat Action and 1 Exertion, the SCP can 
summon a minion with Threat Level 10 or less.  That minion 
may take Combat Actions the following Round.
Glitched	
	
	
Cost: 7
Each time the SCP takes damage, it is teleported to a 
random location within 10 Units.
Incorporalize	
	
Cost: 5
SCP is capable of traveling through walls, ceilings, and 
floors but with a maximum speed of 1 Unit per Move Action.
Shapeshifting	
	
Cost: 13
The SCP is capbale of altering it’s own shape and form, 
allowing the Director to come up with new stats, attacks, and 
abilities on the fly to match any situation.
Telepathy	
	
	
Cost: 5
The SCP is capable of communicating telepathically to any 
Character within 50km.
Elemental Adaptation 	
Cost: 14
Each time the SCP takes damage note the Damage Type.  
The SCP can alter its form to become immune to attacks that 
use that Damage Type.
AoE Attack	
	
	
Cost: 8
Choose a Melee attack.  Add +2 to the Range of the Attack.  
When performed, this attack targets all Characters within 
range instead of just one.
Super Senses	 	
	
Cost: 8
SCP is capable of noticing things very well.  If an Awareness 
check is made (Perception + Mental) then double the result of 
the score.
Reality Bending	
	
Cost: 18
The SCP is capable of Reality Bending (use Mental Skill as 
it’s Reality Bending score).
Sonic Screech	
	
Cost: 9
Choose an attack.  This attack targets all Characters that can 
hear the SCP scream.  
Wallwalking		
	
Cost: 3
SCP gets +2 to Move Speed and is capable of walking on 
walls and ceilings.
Dimensional Phasing	
Cost: 17
SCP is able to spend 1 Exertion and 1 Combat Action 
to escape the area and transport itself into an alternate 
dimension.
Invisibility	
	
	
Cost: 13
SCP may spend 1 Exertion to render itself invisible until the 
end of it’s next Turn.  While invisible it gets +8 to Dodge rolls, 
and +8 to its Reaction score (Dexterity Rating + Physical).
Shield Bubble	 	
	
Cost: 8
SCP may spend 1 Exertion to manifest an energy shield 
around itself.  The next time the SCP takes damage, instead it 
destroys the energy shield.
138
NPC’s & SCP’s


Creating Playable SCP Stats
Directors should always be the ones to create the stats for 
playable SCP Characters.  When creating the stats, the 
Director is allowed to bend any rules they desire in order to 
conform and mold the stats to best reflect the intended SCP.  
To create an SCP, use the Advanced Character Creation 
Method and create an Amida Class Character.  All SCPs 
are created using the Amida Disruption Class, meaning 
they will never receive Chapter Rewards.  Instead of using 
the Disruption Class and Age modifiers for PC creation, use 
the following:
Rather than treating Attribute Tokens and Power Points 
separately, they are combined into 120 Power Points.  These 
points are used both for assigning Attribute Tokens, and 
purchasing Aspects and SCP Abilities (previous page).  For 
Aspects that do not have a Power Point cost, you can convert 
1 Power Point into 3 Talent or History points, just as normal. 
Again, this is all just to be used as a guideline on how to 
create a playable SCP Character for a player.  If the Director 
feels that there are too many or too few Power Points, they 
may adjust this value as needed.  The same can be said for Hit 
Points.  If after substats are calculated something looks off, 
simply adjust to the level desired.  It’s okay if SCP stats are 
a bit stronger than typical Amida Class Characters.  This is 
fine since the Director is expected to create the SCP based 
off of it’s wiki equivalent rather than just buying all the best 
Aspects.  As for skills, use the following list below for easy stat 
calculations instead of buying educational Aspects.
1 Power Point = Put Three Skills at Level 1
1 Power Point = Put a Skill at Level 2
2 Power Points = Put a Skill at Level 3
3 Power Points = Put a Skill at Level 4
5 Power Points = Put a Skill at Level 5
7 Power Points = Put a Skill at Level 6
4 Power Points = Standard Skill Multiplier x2
12 Power Points = Standard Skill Multiplier x3
10 Power Points = Crucial Skill Multiplier x2
30 Power Points = Crucial Skill Multiplier x3
Directors should stay focused on making an SCP accurate to 
the wiki rather than maximizing potential, so use caution to 
not create an SCP which is too powerful when compared to 
most Amida Class Characters.  For examples on playable SCP 
stats, see the pdf which came along with this books purchase.
SCP - 5/0 All Dividers = 6
HTHx = 1
Base HP = 40
Power Points = 120

ATTACK NOTES:  Hair Trap can be set up in two Combat Rounds (4 consecutive 
actions).  This manifests a hair-like web surrounding SCP-352.  Noticing this trap 
requires a Perception + Awareness check of 16.  Characters that enter these zones 
are limited to moving 1 Unit each Action, and automatically take damage at the end 
of their Turns from Hair Trap.  Characters that take damage from SCP-352 become 
infected with “Enzyme.”  When a Character is infected with Enzyme, they must roll 
Health + Resilience difficulty 18.  Failed rolls result in extreme hallucinations, feelings

All Weapons and gear are contained within this chapter, including two Character Creation steps for Advanced creation.
Generic Weapon Stats
It is important to remember that in SCP.2e most generic weapons (guns and blades) use fictional names and represent generic 
weapons with no specific real-life counterpart.  This may seem strange at first, but if the Director and PCs get into the habit 
of giving their weapons custom names and descriptions it will help visualize the weapon moving forward.  Additionally, the 
“Mass” of the weapon doesn’t need to be scrutinized.  A handgun could have a Mass of 12, while an Assault Rifle could have 
a Mass of 8.  A weapons Mass is a game mechanic, and the narrative of your story should be treated separately so long as 
it doesn’t alter the weapons stats.  While SCP.2e attempts to provide an intuitive game experience, we acknowledge that the 
abstraction of weapon specificity in relation to it’s Mass may cause unrealistic outcomes, such as being able to hide an Assault 
Rifle inside of a briefcase.  It’s important to remember that the goal is to create an immersive narrative experience, and some-
times gameplay mechanics need to prioritize fun and balance over objective realism.  We understand that it may take time 
to adjust to this approach, especially if you’re accustomed other tabletop roleplaying games.  This arbitration is necessary to 
provide enhanced weapon customization options for future expansions (see page 110) as well as balance gameplay in a 
tabletop rpg which does not have a monetary system.
  
Uniforms............................................. Page 146
Outfits that Characters can wear which provide Weapon Slots.  
Uniforms determine how large your Weapon Slots are, and how many Combat Actions it takes to draw the weapon.
Weapons that are inside of your Weapon Slots are considered “concealed” and will not increase your SUS unless held (see 
Drawn Weapons on page 101).
Uniform Augmentations............. Page 147
Alterations you can apply to your Uniform.
Common Items................................... Page 148
Common items and goods that Characters may find value in keeping on them at all times.  
Items require one or more Item Slots, listed under “Every Day Carry” on your character sheet.
Items inside of Item Slots are considered “concealed” and will not be seen under normal circumstances unless held or used.
Standard Firearms.......................... Page 150
A list of standard Firearms.  Each weapon requires a Weapon Slot of equal or greater slot size.  
To harm an enemy with a Firearm, use the Fire Combat Action (see page 63).
Standard Melee Weapons............. Page 152
A list of standard Melee Weapons.  Each weapon requires a Weapon Slot of equal or greater slot size.  
To harm an enemy with a Melee Weapon, use the Melee Attack Combat Action (see page 63).
Special Weapons............................... Page 154
Weapons that have unique properties or rulesets.  Each weapon requires a Weapon Slot of equal or greater slot size.
Chapter Eleven:  
Weapons & Gear
Weapons & Gear
144


Choose your Uniform Style from the chart on the next 
page.  You can record up to two Uniform Styles on the 
back of your character sheet.  Uniform A, and Uniform B. 
It is suggested that players choose one Uniform with little to 
no SUS, and another Uniform for combat, however players 
can choose any two Uniforms they please. 
Note:  Uniform Style B will be unlocked after 3 sessions and Direc-
tor approval.  You will then be able to go back and forth between 
either Uniform Style while at base.
If you want to speed up this process, you can simply copy a Class 
Uniform, weapons, and Items from page 34.
Once a Uniform Style is chosen, don’t worry if you want to 
change your mind later on.  Changing Uniform Styles is free, 
and you can have up to two of them recorded on your sheet 
at a time.  
Uniform Styles represent how you will appear to other people. 
When choosing a Uniform it’s important to keep it’s SUS Rating in 
mind.  SUS stands for Suspicious Uniform Score.  Your SUS Rating 
reduces all of your Charisma rolls while in public, and determines 
how dense of a populated area you are allowed to enter without 
turning heads or causing a panic.  High SUS Ratings represent your 
Character having weapons visible and seen as a general threat, 
while low SUS Ratings will blend you into the general public.
 
See below for examples of a SUS Rating’s impact around the 
general public.
SUS of 0 or less
Nobody will see you or remember you were there.
SUS of 1
A few people may give you a second glance.
SUS of 2
You stick out in a crowd.  You will often get stopped by airport 
security.
SUS of 3
People feel generally nervous around you and your presence 
will be remembered by most people.
SUS of 4
People may think there is a dangerous situation in the area 
and quickly leave.  Figures of authority will actively question you.
SUS of 5
People may flee the area, call the police, or possibly attack you.
SUS of 6+
The authorities are on their way.  You hear a helicopter ap-
proaching.  The streets are being vacated. 
The Director has final say on how your SUS affects each situation. 
SUS modifiers may be ignored if your attire is common for your 
surroundings (such as dressing like a Soldier in a Military Base).
Once you choose an Uniform, record the Slot Size and Draw 
Actions for each of the Weapon Slots that are unlocked.  Re-
cord the SUS modifier in the top right after “Loadout A SUS.”
After you have chosen your starting Uniform you may choose 
a weapon for each of your Weapon Slots.  The Weapons 
Mass cannot exceed the Slot Size.  You can get weapon stats 
from page 150 and name them anything you’d like.  After 
you have chosen what weapons you are holding in each of 
your Weapon Slots, record the weapon stats on the front of 
your character sheet.
Lets look at a quick example.  We chose the Loadout style 
“Business Suit” for our Character.  This Loadout style will 
allow us to walk out in public with ease, and synergies well 
with the briefcase augmentation.  We will record the stats on 
the back of our sheet as shown.   
Now that we have our Uniform chosen we can see we have 
two weapon slots.  One with a slot size of 6 (the suit), and 
another with a size of 12 (the briefcase).  This means we can 
choose up to two weapons.  One weapon with a Mass of 6 or 
less, and another with a Mass of 12 or less.  We will record 
which weapons we are holding in the Weapon in Slot area. 
Our Characters are only holding weapons that are in the 
weapon slots of the Uniform we are wearing at any given time. 
For our example we will choose the F1911 and XR 7 as found 
on page 150.

Keep track of additional perks your Uniform may provide in the Misc Notes section on the back of your character sheet.  You 
can list Damage Reduction (DR) on the front of your sheet above your HP.
The descriptions of the Uniforms once chosen can be whatever you’d like as long as it generally would cause the same amount 
of suspicion.  For example, you could choose the Tuxedo Uniform Style but say your Character is wearing an iridescent blazer, 
or choose the Combat Uniform but they are dressed up like a superhero.  The same can be done with Uniform Augmentations- 
You can wear an Army Helmet but declare that it is actually a Motorcycle Helmet with the same stats.
